About this document
This document constitutes a notice under sections 20 and 22A(4) of the Water Industry
Act 1991 (WIA91). It sets out our proposal to issue an enforcement order to South East
Water Limited (South East Water) as a result of its contraventions of section 37 of the
WIA91 and Condition P12 of its instrument of appointment (Licence). It also sets out our
proposed decision to impose a financial penalty on South East Water. It is accompanied
by the proposed enforcement order, requiring it to remedy the contraventions that we
have identified.

The document summarises the evidence that we have gathered and sets out our
findings in relation to the issues under investigation, including why we believe that
South East Water has contravened its statutory duty and the obligations of its Licence.

Interested parties are invited to comment on our proposed decision and the proposed
enforcement order. We will take representations received into account before making a
final decision on whether to issue an enforcement order and the terms of that
enforcement order, as well as whether to impose a penalty and the level of that
penalty.




                                           2
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



Executive Summary
This notice describes our ﬁndings of contraventions by South East Water and how these
have occurred. The investigation was opened due to South East Water's continued poor
performance regarding water supply interruptions and several major loss of supply
events since 2018 in its Kent and Sussex regions (referred to as its Eastern region).
Whilst South East Water considered that the root cause of these events was due to
extreme weather, we have found that they were in part attributable to and/or
exacerbated by failures by South East Water itself to develop and maintain an efﬁcient
water supply system and meet its general duty under section 37 WIA91 to act in a
manner which is best calculated to ensure that it has in place adequate resources and
systems of planning and control to enable it to carry out its functions as required by
Licence Condition P12. In the course of this investigation we uncovered fundamental
issues with South East Water's basic maintenance of assets and systems planning as
well as a failure to plan for extreme weather.

Throughout our investigation we made detailed requests for, and obtained, information
and data from South East Water.

Having reviewed this information, we have identiﬁed that:


     •   South East Water has failed to maintain key assets including its boreholes,
         service reservoirs and trunk mains which meant that its asset base in the
         Eastern region was not sufficiently resilient to adequately perform when
         demand increased. Further, it failed to have adequate proactive maintenance
         plans and to increase storage levels at service reservoirs in the lead up to
         critical periods.
     •   Regarding its systems planning, we found that South East Water failed to
         manage its supply demand balance and headroom which resulted in the
         company being with less headroom and less able to respond than we would
         expect a reasonable company to have be able to in the lead up to, and during
         periods of high demand. This included failing to effectively use planning tools
         available to it (such as area plans, peak week production capacity (PWPC), up-
         to-date hydraulic models, root cause analysis) and to mitigate known risks
         posed by extreme weather and climate change.
     •   This has had a detrimental effect on South East Water's customers. Compared
         to its Western region and other companies in the south east of England, South
         East Water's Eastern region has been disproportionately affected by supply
         interruption incidents since 2020 which were attributable to and/or
         exacerbated by these failures of South East Water. These events affected a
         total of 286,645 customers, with some customers being affected by repeat
         incidents. Our investigation makes no ﬁndings with respect to the Western


                                            3
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



          region (which was not covered by this investigation). To the extent that
          speciﬁc actions by South East Water are company-wide, we ﬁnd that it has
          failed to take appropriate action with respect to the vulnerabilities that existed
          and were known in its Eastern region.
      •   Further, South East Water's operational response to incidents has been poor, it
          had insufﬁcient incident and emergency response, particularly with respect to
          availability of bottled water, mutual aid, tankers and for the support for
          vulnerable sites within its emergency plan. In general, we observed a failure
          by South East Water to learn relevant lessons from past incidents and
          implement actions identiﬁed following those incidents.
      •   The relevant period for this investigation is from 15 November 2018 and
          remains ongoing (the Relevant Period). However, given that our investigation
          was at an advanced stage during the recent supply interruption issues in
          Sussex and Kent in November 2025 to January 2026, we have not obtained
          evidence in relation to those incidents or examined them as part of this
          enforcement case. Ofwat has opened a separate investigation regarding these
          incidents to consider whether it has complied with its obligation to provide
          high standards of customer service and support for its customers under
          Licence Condition G. 1

We consider that our analysis of the evidence we have gathered, at this stage,
demonstrates that South East Water has failed to meet the following duties and
obligations:


      •   To develop and maintain an efﬁcient and economical system of water supply
          within its area and to ensure all such arrangements are made for providing
          supplies of water to premises in that area and for making such supplies
          available to persons who demand them and for maintaining, improving and
          extending water mains and other pipes, under section 37 WIA91; and
      •   To, at all times, act in a manner which is best calculated to ensure that it has
          in place adequate ﬁnancial resources and facilities, management resources
          and systems of planning and internal control, under Licence Condition P12.

Based on the evidence provided to us, in relation to section 37 WIA91, we are satisﬁed
that, during the Relevant Period, South East Water has breached and continues to be in
breach of its duty by:

•   Failing to develop and maintain an efﬁcient and economical system of water
    supply within its Eastern region and, separately, to ensure all such
    arrangements have been made for providing water supply to meet demand, as
    is necessary for securing its obligations under Part III, speciﬁcally:
    • Failings in maintenance of key assets including its inspection and
        maintenance of service reservoirs and boreholes, which reduced supply
        resilience. South East Water did not maintain adequate storage levels in
        advance of and during critical periods. Its general approach to
        maintenance was reactive instead of proactive and failed to adequately
        plan for critical or high demand periods or assess system-wide risks.
    • Failings in planning for its water resources within its water supply system
        at a sub-zonal level. South East Water failed to manage its supply demand
        balance and headroom at a sub-zonal level which resulted in the company
        being with less headroom and less able to respond than we would expect a
        reasonable company to have be able to in the lead up to, and during the
        Events in the Relevant Period. Sub-zonal planning is particularly important
        for South East Water's Eastern region because some sub-zonal areas lack
        interconnectivity and have single points of failure. This included failing to
        mitigate known risks posed by extreme weather and climate change,
        including implementing the actions and recommendations from the
        Drinking Water Inspectorate (DWI)'s and Ofwat's review of the 2018 Freeze
        Thaw Event.
    • Failings in its operational response to supply interruptions. South East
        Water failed to maintain a sufficient operational response to supply
        interruption incidents leaving customers at risk of being unduly without
        water during peak periods. In particular:
         • Failure to respond promptly at the start of incidents, using all available
             operational tools.
         • Failure to deploy alternative water supplies effectively, including
             bottled water, tankers, and mutual aid arrangements.
         • Failure to plan for and prioritise vulnerable sites to ensure support
             reached those most at risk within its emergency plan.
•   Failure to develop and maintain an efﬁcient and economical system of water
    supply within its Eastern region and, separately, to ensure all such
    arrangements have been made for maintaining, improving and extending its
    water mains and other pipes, as is necessary for securing its obligations under
    Part III, speciﬁcally:
    • Failings in the preventative maintenance of trunk mains. South East
        Water relied disproportionately on reactive maintenance and did not
        undertake sufficient preventative maintenance or renewal of its trunk
        mains. These assets were a contributing factor to supply interruptions in
        the Eastern region.

                                       5
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



Based on the evidence provided to us, in relation to Licence Condition P12 these failures
to comply were caused by the company:


     •   Failing to establish and implement effective programmes for the routine
         inspection and maintenance of key assets. In particular, failure to use
         internal targets and plans to conduct proactive programmes to carry out
         regular inspections and maintenance at service reservoirs, maintain trunk
         mains and optimise borehole yield; failure to maintain adequate storage levels
         at reservoirs during peak periods and failure to plan maintenance activities
         with sufﬁcient regard to peak demand and system-wide risk.
     •   Failing to use the planning tools available to it such as its area plans, PWPC,
         up-to-date hydraulic models, root cause analysis and to mitigate known risks
         posed by extreme weather and climate change, including implementing the
         actions and recommendations from the DWI's and Ofwat's review of the 2018
         Freeze Thaw Event.
     •   Failing in its preparedness and resilience for supply interruptions. South East
         Water failed to adequately plan for and manage supply interruptions, including
         establishing what support is required for vulnerable sites and reﬂecting this in
         its emergency plan, mitigating known risks, ensuring operational staff had the
         right information and controls, maintaining alternative water supply
         arrangements, and deploying sufﬁcient resources to prevent customers being
         unduly without water during peak periods.
     •   Failings in Executive and Board level oversight and assurance. South East
         Water’s Executive Team and Board did not ensure that adequate reporting
         metrics and management information were in place to provide a clear and
         accurate understanding of the company’s compliance with its general duty
         under section 37 WIA91. In addition, it failed to effectively monitor and
         challenge risks to supply system resilience, to properly scrutinise compliance
         with legal and regulatory obligations, and ensure that timely and appropriate
         action was taken to in response to those risks.

In response to our information request issued when we opened the investigation, in
November 2023, South East Water has developed an action plan to address short-term
supply system resilience issues, which also refers to the resilience schemes identiﬁed
in the price review process for 2025-30 (Action Plan). Notwithstanding the steps that
South East Water is now beginning to take, Ofwat considers that there remain
signiﬁcant additional measures that the company needs to take to become compliant
with its legal and regulatory obligations. We also consider that the contraventions that
have taken place are sufﬁciently serious to warrant the imposition of a penalty of
£22.46m in this case. Ofwat therefore proposes to require South East Water to:

      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



a) Obtain a report from an independent third party reviewer (approved by Ofwat)
   examining its approach to:
    (i) Review its root cause analysis process and approach to identify the underlying
        causes of failings to comply with section 37 WIA91 including maintenance,
         planning and operational response;
    (ii) Review its incident management procedures, including operational tools and
        escalation triggers; deployment of alternative water supplies and
        implementing lessons learnt from previous incidents.
    (iii)    Review the scope of the company's root-and-branch review of hydraulic
         models;
    (iv) Conduct a root cause analysis of the failings identiﬁed in this decision;
    (v) Make recommendations for remediating the failings, taking into account
           deliverability, affordability and ﬁnanceability.
b) Prepare a remediation plan (for approval by Ofwat), applying the recommendations
   from the independent reviews, setting out the further actions it needs to undertake
   to demonstrate how it will invest in and maintain resilience within its water supply
   system to minimise the risk of supply interruptions to its customers.
c) In line with our ﬁndings in this draft decision, we also require South East Water to
   undertake the following actions:
     (i) Conduct a root-and-branch review of all its hydraulic models.
     (ii) Establish what support is required for vulnerable sites and including this within
          its emergency plan.
     (iii)       Conduct a thorough lessons learnt, including root cause analysis, one
          month after each water supply interruption incident in the future.
d) Prepare (and report to Ofwat) on a management plan to ensure its senior
   management and Board actively review and challenge compliance with its general
   duty under section 37 WIA91 and the Security and Emergency Measures Direction.

1. Introduction
1.1.   This document constitutes a notice issued by the Water Services Regulation
       Authority (Ofwat) to inform South East Water that it proposes to issue an
       enforcement order under section 18 WIA91 and impose on it a penalty under
       sections 22A(1) and (2) WIA91.

1.2.   The proposed enforcement order and penalty relate to South East Water:

        •   Contravening its duty under section 37 WIA91 by not maintaining an efficient
            and economical system of water supply within its area and not ensuring that
            all such arrangements were made for providing supplies of water to
            premises in that area and maintaining, improving and extending water
            mains and other pipes.

        •   Contravening Licence Condition P12 in relation to acting in the manner best
            calculated to ensure that it has in place adequate financial resources and
            facilities, management resources and systems of planning and internal
            control to meet its duties under section 37 WIA91 and the Security and
            Emergency Measures Direction (SEMD).

1.3.   Ofwat considers that a penalty of £22.46m is appropriate.

1.4.   As required by sections 20 and 22A(4) WIA91, we provide more details of the
       proposed enforcement order and financial penalty referenced above, together
       with details of the contraventions to which they relate, in Sections 2 to 6 of this
       notice. This notice is structured as follows:
       • Section 2 sets out some relevant background on the operation the water
           industry and the relevant legislative framework;
       • Section 3 explains the nature of our investigation, summarises the evidence
           we have considered and sets out our assessment of this evidence;
       • Section 4 sets out our findings in relation to contraventions;
       • Section 5 outlines our proposal to issue an enforcement order in this case;
       • Section 6 details the facts which we consider justify our decision to impose
           a financial penalty, and the amount of that penalty.

1.5.   Under sections 20(1)(c) and 22A(4)(d) WIA91, this notice must allow at least
       21 days for representations on or objections to our proposal to be made. We
       explain how to make representations about or objections to this notice in
       Section 7.



                                            8
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited




2. Relevant background
2.1.    Section 2 provides background information on the provision, operation and
        maintenance of typical water treatment works and a water company's supply
        system; the relevant legislative framework for our investigation; and our
        approach to enforcement of the relevant provisions.



2.1 The water supply system
The subsections below provide details of relevant components of the water supply
system referred to in this investigation.

2.1.1 Water only companies
2.2.    Water only companies, such as South East Water, provide water by collecting,
        storing and treating it to high standards so it can be distributed to homes and
        businesses via a network of pipes. 2

2.1.2 Boreholes
2.3.    Boreholes are shafts bored into the ground that are used to extract groundwater
        via a pump, or to monitor and/or measure water quality and/or quantity. 3


2.4.    The maintenance of boreholes is extremely important as it ensures the lifespan
        of the borehole and maintains its efﬁciency and yield. If a borehole is not
        maintained, its efﬁciency will decrease and will eventually no longer be able to
        supply raw water. This leads to the need for boreholes to be replaced earlier
        than the estimated life of the asset.

2.1.3 Water treatment works
2.5.    Water treatment works (WTWs) are treatment plants where raw water is cleaned
        and ﬁltered. The water is then disinfected to ensure water borne diseases are
        eliminated and the pH level is adjusted if the water is too acidic or alkaline.
        Following the ﬁnal treatment, the water leaves the treatment works and is
        stored in covered service reservoirs where it is pumped to customers through a
        network of mains and pumping stations.




2
  Water and sewerage companies also deliver this function, but they also take used water and waste away
to treat it so that it can be discharged back to the environment (e.g. into rivers or the sea).
3
  Environment Agency, Borehole

                                                   9
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



2.1.4 Service reservoirs
2.6.    Service reservoirs are a vital asset for the continuous provision of water to
        customers by providing storage of treated potable water. This storage helps
        companies to respond to changes in demand from customers. As a key part of
        the water supply infrastructure, a regular programme of reservoir inspection,
        cleaning and maintenance is required to reduce the potential for
        microbiological, chemical or physical problems from service reservoirs. 4


2.7.    If service reservoirs are not regularly inspected and cleaned, it is possible that
        when a clean and inspection does eventually take place, several issues could be
        identiﬁed with drinking water quality and/or the structural integrity of the
        service reservoir. If this is the case, this may result in having to take service
        reservoirs out of use for extended periods of time to carry out remedial works.
        This impacts the amount of water available for a speciﬁc region or resource zone
        unexpectedly which, in the event of a supply demand incident, increases the
        risk of customers experiencing supply interruptions.


2.8.    In England, service reservoirs that are capable of holding 25,000m³ or more of
        water are regulated under the Reservoirs Act 1975. The undertakers (owners or
        operators) of these reservoirs are required to register them with the
        Environment Agency following which the reservoirs are designated as "high risk"
        or "not high risk". Those designated high risk are subject to inspection and
        service by reservoir engineers. Those designated not high risk are not subject to
        the same degree of inspection and supervision.


2.9.    High risk reservoirs that fall within the Reservoirs Act 1975 must be inspected
        every ten years. 5 According to UK Water Industry Research (UKWIR) 6 the
        targeted inspection interval should reﬂect the strategic importance of perceived




4
  Environment Agency, https://www.gov.uk/guidance/reservoirs-owner-and-operator-requirements
5
  Section 10(2) of the Reservoirs Act 1975 states that "[u]nless it is at the time under the supervision of a
construction engineer (…) a high-risk reservoir must be inspected under this section at the time specified
by regulations made by the Minister." Regulation 6 of The Reservoirs Act 1975 (Exemptions, Appeals and
Inspections) (England) Regulations 2013 sets out the requirements for periodical inspections of high-risk
reservoirs. Depending on the circumstances, the next inspection is due within 6 months, 2 years, 10 years
or at any time recommended by the supervising engineer or inspecting engineer. The 10-year period is a
stand-alone requirement and is not an alternative to the other requirements. Therefore, there are no
circumstances in which a high-risk reservoir may be inspected more than 10 years after the date of the
previous inspection. The Environment Agency has a duty to enforce the 10-year maximum period between
high-risk reservoir inspections.
6
  UKWIR is a research platform for the water industry in the UK and the Republic of Ireland. All of the water
companies in the UK and Republic of Ireland are its members.

        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        level of risk of the individual service reservoirs.7 Most water companies have
        standard inspection frequencies (linked to cleaning and maintenance) of
        between one and ten years. Some have a risked-based approach as assets age
        or problems arise e.g. inspect on a ﬁve-yearly rolling programme but increase to
        three-yearly when considered high risk, for instance, if a service reservoir is a
        single point of failure or its loss would lead to a supply interruption. 8 According
        to a 2015 joint publication by the DWI, Water UK 9 and Royal Society for Public
        Health, in addition to the requirements of the Reservoirs Act 1975, it is good
        practice that internal inspection should be carried out at a frequency of not
        greater than every ten years. 10


2.10.   As service reservoirs are an important asset in the provision of good quality and
        reliable water sources, it is imperative that storage levels are at the highest
        capacity as possible. According to the UKWIR, the normal operational volume of
        a service reservoir should consider the sum of diurnal storage, which is the daily
        variation between generally constant water production and constantly varying
        consumer demand, and strategic storage, which is the stored water reserves
        needed to meet consumer demands in the event of unplanned interruptions to
        water supply or emergency storage. 11 The normal operational volume of a service
        reservoir should also consider operating top water level to overﬂow depth, ﬂood
        depth, freeboard depth, anti-vortex depth, and washout volume. 12 UKWIR
        demonstrates this explanation by using a hypothetical service reservoir to
        demonstrate with a full operating range of 1974m3; however, the service
        reservoir's normal operating range would be 1680m3, of which 1274m3 would
        represent strategic storage. 13 This means that a service reservoir's normal
        operation volume would be 80-85% of the total volume of the service reservoir.
        As such, we consider an 80-85% target should be the standard target year-

         round for water companies. We acknowledge there are diurnal patterns 14 that
         affect levels of the majority of potable water storage structures, which makes it
         easier to achieve this target overnight but it is still the target to aim for during
         the day.

2.1.5 Booster pumps
2.11.    Booster pumps are used to help distribute water to customers on higher ground.
         They increase the pressure in water supply systems, particularly in areas with
         low pressure or during peak demand times.

2.1.6 Mains
2.12.    There are three different types of water pipe in a company's network. Water
         mains, which are the large company pipes that distribute water around the
         network. There are then two types of water pipes that transmit supply from
         water mains to customers: the communication pipes (which carry water from
         the company water mains and the boundary of private property) and the supply
         pipes (which carry water from company water mains onto private property).15

2.13.    Trunk mains are a subset of water mains and are pipelines that are generally laid
         with a depth cover of 900mm below the ground surface, conveying water in bulk
         between a reservoir, treatment works or pumping station and a service reservoir
         or a distribution network of pipes. Trunk mains are among the most critical
         assets operated by the water industry: a catastrophic failure of a trunk main
         could be hugely destructive and leave many customers without water. 16 As these
         are such critical assets, regular trunk mains investigations and leakage surveys
         ensure efﬁciency and reliability of these assets to reduce the risk of failure. 17


2.14.    Water company pipes are susceptible during winter to a period at or below
         freezing temperatures, followed by rapid thaws, that can cause signiﬁcant burst
         pipes and leaks that result in supply interruptions. These are referred to as
         Freeze Thaw events. 18




14
   Diurnal patterns/variation refers to the regular rise and fall in storage levels over a 24-hour day as water
demand changes. For example, demand may be highest in the morning when people are showering. As
such, storage levels are typically at higher levels ready for the peak demand.
15
   Responsibility for pipes and pumping stations - Ofwat

   Trunk Main Losses - Water Leak Location Services

                                 on South East Water Limited



2.15.   Ofwat assesses the health of companies' assets and considers their mains
        repairs and unplanned outage performance over the course of a year in the
        annual Water Company Performance Report. It is critical that water companies
        have assets, including trunk mains, that are well maintained and operate as
        intended so that they meet the requirements of their statutory obligations and
        the needs of customers, the environment and wider society. The maintenance of
        trunk mains should include both proactive and reactive maintenance.


2.16.   In order to address the issue of aging assets, water companies will generally
        replace or renew those assets. Mains replacement is generally considered
        preventative maintenance and is one of the ways companies can improve the
        resilience of their water supply systems. According to a UKWIR research paper
        entitled 'Trunk Mains Preventative Maintenance to Reduce Major Incident Risk,
        2020/21', many water companies have recognised the beneﬁts from trunk main
        preventative maintenance and have programmes in place to replace mains.

2.1.7 Valves
2.17.   Valves play an integral role in a water distribution system as they are responsible
        for regulating water ﬂow, maintaining pressure, opening and closing water ﬂow.
        The operation of valves can be used to redirect ﬂows through the network, for
        example to maintain or restore supplies to customers in response to incidents
        such as bursts. They can either be operated manually or can be automated.

2.18.   UKWIR states that a failure to maintain valves increases the risk of supply
        interruptions and creates water quality problems. 19

2.1.8 Preventative maintenance
2.19.   An asset management focused utility should operate an effective asset
        management system that allows it to make informed risk based decisions. As
        part of this system it will have an up-to-date asset maintenance strategy that is
        designed to ensure assets are maintained, repaired or renewed to deliver the
        expected levels of service to the wider system. This will include making informed
        decisions regarding the balance of proactive (preventative) and reactive
        (corrective) maintenance activities linked back to the probability and likelihood
        of failure and its consequence in delivering the desired service. Assets should be
        maintained in line with documented policy to reduce the likelihood of failure
        and operated in accordance with the expected level of service. One way to do



19
  UKWIR, Trunk Mains Preventative Maintenance to Reduce Major Incident Risk Workshop, Report Ref No.
22/WM/08/75.

                                                 13
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        this is to operate and maintain the asset according to the manufacturers’
        recommendations and known wear and tear in the local system. Preventative
        maintenance is often recommended at certain intervals by the manufacturer
        and adjusted based on known conditions. Preventative maintenance usually
        extends the useful life of an asset, lowering its cost of ownership and reduces
        risk of failure. It is also less expensive than reactive maintenance as companies
        can plan for peak times, reducing labour costs and customer impacts.

2.1.9 Supply/demand balance
2.20. Maintaining the balance between water supply and demand is important for
      water supply resilience and for meeting a water company's duty to maintain an
        economic and efﬁcient water supply system. Supply demand planning is
        undertaken at water resource zone (WRZ) level in a water resources
        management plan (WRMP). A WRZ describes an area within which the sources
        of water and distribution of water to meet demand, is largely self-contained
        (apart from any agreed bulk transfers). However, it is also important to consider
        whether there is sufﬁcient supply resilience at the sub-zonal level (i.e. within
        each WRZ) requiring appropriate interconnectivity and resilient supplies within
        the WRZ to ensure resource can be shared between customers within the zone
        and hence all and customers will experience approximately the same risk of
        supply failure. 20


2.21.   Two key metrics used in water resource planning are deployable output and
        distribution input. Deployable output (DO) is the reliable output of a source (or
        groupings / all sources across a water resource zone). It is constrained by
        factors such as the environment, licences, treatment or network constraints and
        water quality. Distribution input (DI) is a measure of demand based on the
        amount of potable water that enters the distribution system (from a WTW site, or
        sites across a WRZ) to downstream networks.


2.22.   Companies must retain headroom to ensure a secure and reliable water supply.
        Headroom is the difference between total water available for use (WAFU) and
        the volume of DI to meet demand. For instance, the difference between the
        amount of water a company has available to supply and the volume of water it
        expects to introduce into its network to meet demand. Target headroom is the
        amount of headroom that a company has set out in its WRMP, over its planning
        horizon, based on how much it thinks it will need as a buffer to cover future



20
   Environment Agency, 2016, Water resources planning guideline supplementary guidance – Water
resource zone integrity

                                                 14
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        uncertainties in supply and demand. Target headroom is not accounted for
        when calculating WAFU. Instead, it is added to DI as an additional proxy demand
        to be accounted for in a supply demand balance.


2.23.   The unplanned outage performance commitment (PC) incentivises good asset
        health. This measure should be used as a means of assessing asset health
        (primarily for non-infrastructure or above ground water assets), for water
        abstraction and water treatment activities. As part of calculating their
        unplanned outage PC, since the 2019 price review (PR19), companies are
        required to measure their PWPC. By introducing unplanned outage at PR19, and
        the underlying PWPC, Ofwat acknowledged that asset capability over a 7-day
        peak is important to understand and measure and use as a planning tool.


2.24.   Ofwat's unplanned outage PC guidance states: "PWPC is equivalent to the
        maximum volume of water which can be put into supply and sustained over a
        period of one week measured in megalitres per day (Ml/d). This should be at
        least as great as the highest historic performance that has been sustained for
        any seven-day period in the last ﬁve years (unless a change to assets or process
        can be evidenced) but could be higher. This should be supported by physical
        tests to demonstrate capability undertaken at least once every ﬁve years. It is
        expected that this value should be reviewed annually and periodically as
        modiﬁcations to assets and processes are completed which impact capacity." 21
        The guidance explains in further detail that these ﬁve yearly tests should
        evidence actual output or capacity and be based on a risk-based approach for
        each works, ensuring that the testing does not extend to seven days.22 The
        guidance also explicitly states that PWPC does not account for seasonal changes
        in yield (most commonly observed at groundwater sources) and allowed
        abstraction volumes (most commonly observed at river sources) which are
        weather dependent and not an indicator of asset health.23


2.25.   In some circumstances, one water company can supply water to another
        company in large quantities. This is known as a bulk supply.24 Bulk supplies are
        sometimes referred to as "water trades" as they are a way for water companies to
        trade water and may help companies to address potential gaps between the




21
   The methodology for calculating PWPC is set out by Ofwat's Reporting Guidance link.
22
   Final consistent reporting guidance - unplanned outage, p5.
23
   Final consistent reporting guidance - unplanned outage, p5.
24
   Ofwat, Negotiating bulk supplies

                                                    15
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        supply and demand of water resources. 25 Transporting the bulk supply from one
        company to another company involves an "interconnection", often in the form of
        a pipe. 26


2.26.   A temporary usage ban (TUB) (commonly known as a "hosepipe ban") is a
        demand-side measure used by water companies that temporarily restricts water
        use by customers. Water companies may impose TUBs when water supplies are
        low and drought triggers are reached.

2.1.10 Hydraulic modelling
2.27.   Hydraulic models illustrate effects of changing demand and climatic conditions
        on water distribution systems – as such modelling is a key element of identifying
        areas of supply risk under current and future scenarios, predicting pressures
        and identifying bottlenecks. It also plays a role in demonstrating the
        effectiveness of proposed solutions to reduce these risks.27 This is essential for
        optioneering processes, including in demonstrating optimal solutions for
        considerations in the water resource management planning and price review
        processes. 28


2.28. Hydraulic modelling can be used to identify high-leakage areas, develop
        targeted pressure management plans, and optimise infrastructure
        investments. 29 It assists in both day-to-day operations and future planning by
        identifying areas of limited capacity, identifying zones of inefﬁciency and
        potential concerns, and identifying areas that may become overloaded in the
        near future.30 In addition, the interrogation of hydraulic models is a key enabler
        for optimum, real-time decision-making during an incident or event. 31


2.29. The standard approach at most UK water companies is to have a rolling
        programme of hydraulic model updates aimed at ensuring all models are
        reviewed once every ﬁve years. A recalibration / rebuild is not always required
        every ﬁve years (for example, in a rural area with no investment / signiﬁcant




25
   Ofwat, Negotiating bulk supplies
26
   Ofwat, Negotiating bulk supplies

                                   on South East Water Limited



           network changes or abandonments) but an audit every ﬁve years will determine
           this. 32

2.1.11 Root cause analysis
2.30. Root cause analysis (RCA) is a systemic and structured problem solving
      approach that adopts a proactive, rather than reactive, management of issues
           by preventing recurring issues. RCA is commonly used in the industry to get to
           the underlying cause of problems or trigger events, to provide insight for the
           management of future risks helping to identify both operational and capital
           interventions that would eliminate, reduce or effectively mitigate risks. Further,
           a UKWIR report published in 2023, highlighted industry practice, developed a
           minimum data standard for various activities and made the case strongly for
           thorough RCA activities following each supply interruption. It states:


                   "When the threshold event criteria are met and an RCA is required, the
                   responsible person should initiate the process as soon as is reasonably
                   practicable…The investigative team should compile detailed information
                   regarding the system, its components processes and other asset lifecycle
                   insights, relevant to the event…As a general principle the greater the
                   impact of the event has on health, safety, environment, reputation, asset
                   availability or cost; the more advanced and complex the RCA
                   methodology utilised should be…


                   Following the identiﬁcation of root causes using the selected RCA
                   process, the team will then need to develop corrective interventions to
                   address them. It is important in this stage for participants to stay
                   creative so that all possible intervention approaches are listed for each
                   root cause before they are vetted… After reﬂective thinking about the
                   connection between the trigger event and root cause(s), the potential
                   intervention options should be incorporated into the water company
                   intervention delivery processes, and then subsequently evaluated to
                   ensure:
                       •   the prevention of ITS [interruption to supply] event recurrence
                       •   the re-establishment of the water company management system
                           control




32
     Aqua, Hydraulic Modelling Technical Note, 18 September 2025, p.2.

                                                     17
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



                     •   the organisational strategic priorities and other objectives are
                         being met." 33


2.31.   Although this particular report was published outside of the relevant period for
        this investigation, it consolidates long-standing, industry practice. In any event,
        we would expect, a mature asset management company to have a clearly
        deﬁned root cause analysis process in response to incidents. We would expect
        this to utilise supporting tools / software, often linked to hydraulic models (see
        Section 2.1.10), where required, to facilitate standardised and holistic root
        cause analysis activities. As evident from the citation above, the root cause
        analysis is not limited to identifying the trigger event as the cause of a supply
        interruption or the generalised or immediate causes of an incident, but
        identifying the underlying systemic issues, such as why and how the assets,
        their design, maintenance status/condition and operational response planning
        have contributed to such an interruption occurring and actions needed to
        improve performance in the future. Correcting only an immediate cause may
        eliminate a symptom of a problem, but not the problem itself.

2.1.12           Climate change
2.32.   Climate change is one of the biggest challenges to the long-term sustainability
        of the water sector in England and Wales.


2.33.   Ofwat published a climate change policy statement in 2008, which set the
        expectation that companies would seek "to understand and plan for the impact
        of climate change on both the supply of and demand for water resources." 34 It
        set out an expectation that companies ensure that their assets continue to be ﬁt
        for purpose in a changing climate and use their expertise and detailed
        knowledge of their asset base to make sure their long-term approaches to asset
        management stand up to future climate change scenarios, and (in the context
        of ﬂooding in the summer of 2007) understand the resilience of their assets. In
        October 2019, as part of Ofwat's 'Time to act, together' strategy, we set out that
        we want to drive the sector to meet long-term challenges such as climate
        change to ensure current and future customers have access to high-quality,
        reliable and affordable services, as well as a thriving environment. 35 This set the




33
   UKWIR, Identifying the root cause of interruptions to supply, IDENTIFYING THE ROOT CAUSE OF

        expectation that all companies would have a sufﬁcient understanding of their
        assets so "they can efﬁciently target their maintenance and replacement
        activities, be smarter in how they operate them to reduce risks, be proactive in
        mitigating the impacts of climate change, reduce leakage and service failure
        risks to customers." 36


2.34.   Pursuant to the Climate Change Act 2008, the Government asked Ofwat and
        water companies (that serve over 50,000 billed premises) 37 to report on their
        assessment of how climate change is likely to affect them and the associated
        risks. Ofwat published Climate Change Adaptation Reports in 2011, 2016, 2022
        and 2025, in which it discusses, among other things, the shift in the UK weather
        patterns and the need to develop and maintain resilient systems for water
        supply. 38 It was also noted that much of the funding to provide resilient systems
        and services is included in normal business (‘base’) operating costs.



2.1.13 Covid-19
2.35.   The Covid-19 pandemic was an unprecedented worldwide event, with water
        customers in England and Wales conﬁned to their homes during national
        lockdowns in 2020 and 2021 and a shift to a working/studying from home and/or
        hybrid working models, with less time spent at workplaces, schools and other
        venues. During the lockdowns this resulted in an unprecedented change in
        water usage patterns, with more water usage by households and less usage by
        many businesses. Ofwat refreshed its 'Time to act, together' strategy in January
        2024 to note the unforeseen pressures due to the Covid-19 pandemic.


2.36. Ofwat and the water companies (via Water UK) worked collaboratively to
        understand the impact of Covid-19 on the industry. As part of this collaboration,
        they commissioned a Frontier Economics report on the economic impacts of
        Covid-19 on the water sector, which was published in December 2020. The



36
   Time-to-act-together-Ofwats-strategy-1.pdf, p.31.
37
   Including South East Water.
38
   E.g. Ofwats-3rd-Climate-Change-Adaptation-Report.pdf, p. 5-6: "…the UK Meteorological Office has
updated its climate change projections (UKCP18)4 . This sets out that the UK climate will continue to be
characterised by warmer, wetter winters and hotter, drier summers." "We are already seeing drier
summers, more frequent and intense rainfall, more variable river flows and biological changes in water
bodies. The sector has already experienced a number of extreme weather events; for example, the ‘Beast
from the East’ in 2018, flooding from storms like Storm Ciara and Storm Christoph in 2020, and surface
water flooding in London in July 2021. The heatwave of August 2020 increased pressure on water supplies
particularly in the southeast, with prolonged increases in temperature having the potential to adversely
impact unique water environments, such as chalk streams. It is further estimated that there is a 25%
chance of the worst drought in recorded history within the next 30 years."

                                                   19
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        report noted that the pandemic led to an increase in household water
        consumption known as per capita consumption (PCC) and, combined with hot
        weather in 2020, an increase in PCC and water production costs for most
        companies.


2.37.   In 2020, water companies performed poorly with respect to PCC and supply
        interruptions, with no company achieving its PCC target in 2020-21. The Service
        Delivery Report 2020-21 published by Ofwat explained that "measures
        introduced to combat the spread of Covid-19 – such as increased working from
        home, closure of schools and the leisure and hospitality sectors – impacted how
        much and when customers used water. To varying degrees, companies saw non-
        household water use decrease and household water use increase; long periods
        of hot and dry weather during spring 2020 also increased demand; and
        companies’ ability to enter customers’ homes to offer water saving support and
        install meters was restricted" and that "high demand for water during spring
        2020 exceeded some companies’ capacity to supply customers and resulted in
        additional interruptions to supply. These factors contribute[d] to some of the
        poorest supply interruption ﬁgures since the freeze-thaw event in 2017-18."


2.38. Ofwat commissioned a further report by Europe Economics, published in March
      2024, indicating the water sector has seen an increase of 1.8% in PCC because of
      working from home since the Covid-19 pandemic. The pandemic also impacted
      water use reduction measures, including the installation of water meters. Some
      companies either continued with, or adapted their water reduction plans, or
      caught up after restrictions were lifted. However, several companies fell behind
      during this period and have not caught up as a result or abandoned aspects of
      their reduction scheme entirely. Europe Economics found that the rise in PCC
        during 2020-21 was largely due to "Covid shock" and only a minority of the rise
        could be attributed to the variations in water usage reduction programmes.



2.2 Regulatory framework
2.39. Section 2.2 details aspects of the regulatory framework applicable to water
      supply management which are relevant to this investigation.

2.2.1 Ofwat's price review process
2.40. Ofwat's price review process sets the investment and service package that
        customers will receive, including the price controls that limit what companies




                                             20
          Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                   on South East Water Limited



             can charge their customers. 39 Ofwat undertakes this price review every ﬁve
             years, with the period covered referred to as an Asset Management Plan (AMP)
             period. Most recently, PR19 set Ofwat's expectations of companies' delivery for
             the 2020-25 period (AMP7) and the 2024 price review (PR24) for the 2025-30
             period (AMP8).


2.41.        For each price review, Ofwat requires water companies in England and Wales to
             develop business plans setting out the investments they will deliver during the
             next AMP period to meet their statutory and licence obligations in an efﬁcient
             and economic manner, including service levels for various aspects of their
             delivery for customers. Through its price review process, Ofwat tests and
             challenges companies' business plans to ensure the service improvements they
             propose are suitably stretching, and to ensure efﬁcient investment. Types of
             funding relevant to this investigation are:


         •     Base expenditure – routine, year-on-year expenditure to deliver a base level of
               good service to customers and the environment and maintain the long-term
               capability of assets.
         •     Enhancement expenditure – generally where there is a permanent increase or
               step change in the current level of service to a new ‘base’ level and/or the
               provision to new customers of the current service level. It may be driven by
               new statutory obligations, by improving service quality and resilience, or by
               providing new solutions for water provision in drought conditions.

2.42.        Although Ofwat sets the initial total funding, it is up to individual companies to
             prioritise and determine how this funding is allocated and whether additional
             funding (subject to cost sharing with customers) from their shareholders is
             required to meet their legal obligations and business needs.


2.43.        Ofwat's price review also sets the performance levels each company should be
             seeking to achieve during the AMP for a set of speciﬁc outcomes, known as PCs.


         •     Supply interruptions are deﬁned as when properties are without a continuous
               supply of water. Water supply interruptions is a common PC that measures the
               average number of minutes lost per customer for the whole customer base for
               interruptions that last three hours or more. This PC is intended to incentivise a
               company to minimise the number and duration of supply interruptions that
               customers experience and to improve the reliability of water supply. The



39
     Price reviews - Ofwat

                                                  21
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



              three-hour benchmark that reﬂects companies cannot reasonably prevent all
              supply interruptions. However, when they do occur, we expect companies to
              minimise impact on customers by ﬁxing them quickly and communicating
              well. It provides a measure not only of companies' asset management
              capabilities but their response and recovery abilities. It has been a common
              PC for more than 10 years since PR14.
        •     Leakage is the loss of water from the network system that has not been
              delivered to homes, businesses or used in their operations; it can be described
              as the water which the companies cannot account for. Leakage was a
              common PC for all water companies within the PR19 period.
        •     Mains repairs, speciﬁcally, the need to repair water main bursts was subject
              to a performance commitment level (PCL) in PR19 and focuses on the number
              of reactive repairs to burst mains per thousand kilometres of the company's
              entire water main network. For PR24, Ofwat changed the name of this PC to
              ‘Repairs to burst mains’. This is to make it clearer that the performance
              commitment speciﬁcally covers repairs to burst pipes, rather than other types
              of mains repairs, proactive renewals, or replacements.

2.44.       Through the price review, companies are set PCLs for each PC. Companies are
            incentivised to achieve these PCLs through outcome delivery incentives (ODIs).
            In broad terms, companies can secure ﬁnancial beneﬁts by outperforming their
            PCLs or incur underperformance payments where they do not. 40 Ofwat's price
            review determinations also set the maximum level of ODI payments that
            companies can incur on speciﬁc PCs.


2.45.       Companies are required to report to Ofwat annually on the delivery of their PCLs,
            including supply interruptions and leakage, via their Annual Performance Report
            (APR). 41 The APRs inform Ofwat's decisions on in-period and end of period
            adjustments to the amount of money companies can recover from the
            customers dependent on their performance. Companies commission
            independent, third-party assurance of the data contained within their APRs, and
            the APRs are signed off by each company's Board.




40
   These payments are distinct from enforcement financial penalties that Ofwat may impose
under section 22A of the WIA91. The former is part of mechanisms used in the price review to incentivise
the achievement of agreed PCs; the latter are penalties for breaching statutory or licence
requirements that Ofwat enforces.
41
   The requirement to report to Ofwat for price review purposes is set out in Condition B, paragraph 9.2, and
Condition M of companies' Licences.

                                                     22
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



2.46.    For PR24 (2025-30), Ofwat also imposed price control deliverables (PCDs) which
         set out its expectation on improvements funded through enhancement
         expenditure including in areas such as resilience, metering, mains renewals,
         water demand and efﬁciency. All companies provide Ofwat with Delivery Plans
         on a six-monthly basis showing how they will meet their PCD targets and interim
         milestones. 42


2.47.    Companies can request a redetermination of Ofwat's price reviews by the
         Competition and Markets Authority (CMA). South East Water has requested a
         redetermination of the PR24 ﬁnal determination by the CMA, which Ofwat
         referred to the CMA on 18 March 2025 (CMA Redetermination). On 9 October
         2025 the CMA published its provisional determinations (CMA Provisional
         Determination).



2.2.2 Security and Emergency Measures Direction

2.48.    Section 208 WIA91 allows the Secretary of State and Welsh Ministers to issue
         directions for the purpose of mitigating the effects of any civil emergency, that
         is, a natural disaster or other emergency which, in their opinion, is or may be
         likely to disrupt water supplies (deﬁned in s208(7) WIA91). The SEMD 43 is the
         principal general direction issued under section 208 WIA91. The DWI is
         responsible for the regulation of the SEMD for companies who are wholly or
         mainly in England and Wales. The DWI exercises the functions of the Secretary
         of State and Welsh Ministers relating to enforcement of the companies' duties
         under the SEMD for the purpose of section 18 WIA91 (and related provisions).

2.49.    Paragraph 4(1) of the SEMD requires a company to make, keep under review and
         revise plans it considers necessary to ensure the continued exercise of its water
         supply function in an emergency. Plans must be prepared on the basis that the
         company must:
        • Ensure a minimum supply of water is available by alternative means during a
           civil emergency (paragraph 4(4)(b) SEMD).

      out these activities.


2.2.3 Standards applicable to water supply systems

2.51.       As we explain further in Section 2.3.1, the Secretary of State may publish
            regulations, including application by Ofwat, establishing standards relating to
            how a company performs its water supply obligations. During the recent review
            of the water industry, the former National Infrastructure Commission (NIC) 44
            made recommendations to government, including the need to develop resilience
            standards in UK infrastructure. The Independent Water Commission's Final
            Report published on 21 July 2025 (IWC Report) recommended implementing
            such statutory resilience standards for the water sector in the future, including
            the need to develop resilience standards in UK infrastructure.45 The UK
            Government's White Paper published on 20 January 2026 indicates that it will
            develop statutory resilience standards and new powers to make legislation on
            sufﬁciency to protect the supply of water to customers. 46 However, no standards
            have yet been implemented.

Transformation Authority.

2.52.   In November 2025, Ofwat concluded its review into how companies demonstrate
        their overall competency in asset management. 47 While the review found that
        one way to do this is to be certiﬁed to International Organisation for
        Standardisation (ISO) 55001:2024, compliance with this ISO is not a
        requirement of companies' licences. 48

2.53.   Nevertheless, while there are no formal binding industry standards for the
        purpose of companies' compliance with its supply system obligations, there is
        publicly available information regarding the desirable approaches and good
        industry practice the companies should adopt. As early as December 2015,
        Ofwat deﬁned resilience as the ability to cope with, and recover from,
        disruption, and anticipate trends and variability in order to maintain services for
        people and protect the natural environment, now and in the future.49

2.54.   South East Water itself noted an industry resilience standard (commonly used
        from at least 2017), namely "the 4Rs": resistance; reliability; redundancy;
        response and recovery. 50

        regarding good practice in the water industry. PCLs or key performance
        indicators relating to aspects of supply system performance, including supply
        interruptions, were also set in the price review processes since 2011, 51 as also
        explained at Section 2.2.1. 52 As a result of supply interruption during extreme

2.2.4 The outcome of the Independent Water Commission Review

2.56. The Independent Water Commission (IWC), chaired by Sir Jon Cunliffe, has
      recently conducted a review of the water sector regulatory system in England
      and Wales. Following the publication of the IWC Report, the UK government has
      set out a new direction for the water sector with the formation of a new
      regulator in England. This marks an opportunity to reset the sector, so it delivers
      better outcomes for customers and the environment.

2.57.   Ofwat will now work with the government and other regulators to form this new
        body in England, combining Ofwat with the Drinking Water Inspectorate and
        including elements of the Environment Agency and Natural England. We will also
        continue to engage with the Welsh Government and contribute to discussions on
        the options for Wales.

2.58. Until these new arrangements are in place, Ofwat continues to have a duty to
      enforce current regulations to improve performance and deliver maximum value
      for customers, communities, and the environment.



2.3 Relevant legal framework

2.59. Section 2.3 provides details on the relevant legal framework that we have
      applied in this investigation.


2.3.1 Section 37 WIA91 general duty

2.60. Section 37 WIA91 is one of the principal duties imposed on undertakers. 55 It
      provides:

        "37 General duty to maintain water supply system etc.

        (1) It shall be the duty of every water undertaker to develop and maintain an
        efﬁcient and economical system of water supply within its area and to ensure
        that all such arrangements have been made—




55
   See section 24 WIA91. A contravention or a likely contravention of a principal duty, where it is sufficiently
serious to make it inappropriate for the company to continue to hold its appointment, is one of the grounds
on which a High Court could make a special administration order with respect to a water company.

                                                      26
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



                (a) for providing supplies of water to premises in that area and for
                    making such supplies available to persons who demand them; and

                (b) for maintaining, improving and extending the water undertaker’s
                water mains and other pipes,

          as are necessary for securing that the undertaker is and continues to be able to
          meet its obligations under this Part [III]."


2.61.    In effect, it therefore sets out the following duties of a water undertaker:
        • To develop and maintain an "efﬁcient and economical" system of water supply
           within its area;
        • To ensure that all such arrangements have been made for providing supplies
           of water to premises in that area and for making such supplies available to
           persons who demand them, as are necessary for securing that the undertaker
           is and continues to be able to meet its obligations under Part III WIA91; and
        • To ensure that all such arrangements have been made for maintaining,
           improving and extending the water undertaker’s water mains and other pipes,
           as are necessary for securing that the undertaker is and continues to be able
           to meet its obligations under Part III WIA91.

2.62.    Section 37 is enforceable by Ofwat under section 18 WIA91 (see further
         explanation relating to our enforcement powers from paragraphs 2.85 to 2.90).
         As set out above, it is linked with the obligations imposed on the companies
         under Part III WIA91. These obligations and the remedies available in respect of
         contraventions of those obligations are additional to and/or qualiﬁed by any
         duties imposed or remedies available by virtue of section 37 WIA91 itself or
         section 38 WIA91.

Efﬁcient and economical system of supply

2.63. As explained above, one of the duties imported by section 37 WIA91 is for the
      water company to develop and maintain an "efﬁcient and economical" system of
      water supply within its area. The term "efﬁcient and economical" is not deﬁned
      and we therefore interpret it according to its plain language / common sense
      meaning.

2.64.    The system of water supply must be provided economically and efﬁciently (i.e.
         using customers' money effectively and not wasting it), but it equally cannot be
         said to be economical or efﬁcient if it is not also ﬁt for purpose.

Section 38 WIA91
                                             27
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



2.65. Section 38 WIA91 enables the Secretary of State (Defra) to set standards of
      performance by way of regulations, including on application by Ofwat, 56 in order
      to clarify the extent to which breaches of obligations under Part III WIA91
      amount to a breach of the section 37 WIA91 duty or to supplement that duty.

2.66. To date, only the Water Supply and Sewerage Services (Customer Service
      Standards) Regulations 2008, commonly referred to as the Guaranteed Service
      Standards Scheme (GSS), were adopted pursuant to section 38 WIA91. The GSS
      sets a baseline level of service below which companies must not fall and
      mandates payments to be made to affected customers for contravening these
      service standards.57

Obligations under Part III WIA91

2.67.   Part III WIA91 sets out a number of speciﬁc obligations imposed on water
        companies. 58 These relate to supply of water to household customers
        (individuals) and/or non-household customers (broadly speaking – businesses).
        We discuss in more detail below those obligations that are relevant to this
        investigation.

2.68. Section 52 WIA91 establishes the domestic supply duty.59 This is a duty to
      provide a supply of water such that is "sufﬁcient" 60 for domestic purposes.61
      There are certain circumstances in which the water undertakers have powers to
      disconnect and/or reduce supply of water. These include the carrying out any
      necessary works, if it is reasonable for the disconnection to be made and

      which South East Water runs its water supply system is such that it creates a
      real risk it would not be able to comply with its duties with respect to water
      quality.


2.3.2 Licence Condition P
2.70.   Licence Condition P12 65 requires that, to enable it to carry out the regulated
        activities, a company must, at all times, act in a manner which is best
        calculated to ensure that it has in place adequate ﬁnancial resources and
        facilities, management resources and systems of planning and internal
        control. 66 Regulated activities are the functions of a water undertaker and
        include the duties imposed on a water undertaker, such as the duties under
        section 37 WIA91 and a duty under section 208(3) WIA91 to comply with the
        SEMD which was issued pursuant to that section. 67

   Except in an emergency or in the case of a reduction which is immaterial.

exceptional shortage of rain there is (a threat of) serious deficiency of supplies of water in the area or such
a deficiency as to pose a threat to flora and/or fauna in the relevant waters. A company may be allowed to
