2.71.        This Licence Condition P obligation is a particular focus of this investigation. In
             order to be able to fulﬁl this obligation, a company would reasonably need to
             have:
         •     A robust understanding of the regulated activity it is intended to deliver;
         •     Suitable operational practices to enable that activity to be carried out and
               monitored; and
         •     Suitable processes of governance control and assurance to scrutinise and
               challenge delivery performance and reporting.

2.72.        In addition to this obligation, each year a company is required to submit a ring-
             fencing certiﬁcate to Ofwat 68 to conﬁrm that, among other things, in the opinion
             of its Board, it will have sufﬁcient ﬁnancial and management resources and
             system of planning and internal control to enable it to carry out its regulated
             activities for at least the next 12 month period. Where a company's Board
             becomes aware of any circumstances that would mean it could no longer
             provide a Ring-fencing Certiﬁcate (RFC), it must inform Ofwat in writing. 69 To
             enable the Board to sign the RFC, companies also prepare a Risk and Compliance
             Statement (RCS). This is designed to identify risks across the organisation and
             assess the extent to which any risks identiﬁed might impact on the ability of the
             company to carry out its regulated activities.


2.73.        A company is also required by Licence Condition P3 to meet the Board
             Leadership, Transparency and Governance Objectives to ensure that:
         •     The company has an effective board with full responsibility for all aspects of
               the company’s business for the long term;
         •     The board establishes the company’s purpose, strategy and values, and is
               satisfied that these and its culture reflect the needs of those it serves;
         •     The board’s leadership and approach to transparency and governance ensures
               trust in the company and ensure accountability for its actions; and
         •     The board and its committees are competent, well run, and have sufficient
               independent membership, ensuring they can make high quality decisions that
               address diverse customer and stakeholder needs.


2.3.3 Practical implications of the legal framework and our
approach to the investigation
Application of section 37 WIA91




68
     Licence Condition P30.
69
     Licence Condition P32.

                                                  30
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



2.74.   Providing reliable supplies of safe drinking water is a fundamental legal
        obligation of all water companies, reﬂected in their general duty under
        section 37 WIA91 to maintain a water supply system.


2.75.   As mentioned above, apart from GSS, no regulations setting out standards
        applicable to the section 37 WIA91 duty have been put in place to date. We have
        therefore applied the wording of this section, and other relevant provisions,
        based on its common-sense construction, to the facts of this case to assess
        South East Water's conduct and we did so in accordance with what we would
        expect from a reasonable company. We carefully and conscientiously considered
        the evidence available to us in this case and took into account the performance
        of other companies as well as industry practice.


2.76.   We explain our approach in further detail below, including a list of indicative
        behaviours/good practice which we consider a reasonable company must
        demonstrate in order to meet its duties under section 37 WIA91. This is a non-
        exhaustive list, which focuses on the aspects of supply systems relevant to this
        investigation.


2.77.   Water undertakers are required to develop and maintain an economical and
        efﬁcient water supply system. This means that its system must not only be
        delivered at proportionate cost but also be ﬁt for purpose: a system which, while
        low cost, cannot be sufﬁciently relied upon to meet the undertaker's supply
        obligations cannot be said to be economic and efﬁcient. Monitoring and
        maintenance of supply system assets, such that this system operates
        economically and efﬁciently, is a key aspect of this duty and applies to all
        components of a supply system such as boreholes, trunk mains and service
        reservoirs. We expect a reasonable water company to undertake not only
        reactive, but also proactive maintenance. We also expect the companies to have
        adequate operational response to issues with their supply systems, such as
        supply interruptions, to ensure that the system continues to operate
        economically and efﬁciently and minimise their impact on the customers.


2.78.   Whether something is economical or efﬁcient will ultimately be the matter of
        fact in the speciﬁc circumstances of each investigation, judged on the balance
        of evidence.


2.79.   We also expect the companies to have arrangements in place for providing water
        supplies and maintaining, improving and extending water mains and other pipes
        such that the company is both meeting its obligations in Part III WIA91 and that
        it is able to continue to do so (this involves determining whether a company is
                                             31
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



            capable of meeting its obligations in the future and/or whether there is a
            realistic risk of non-compliance).


2.80. We expect a reasonable company to take ownership of and be proactive in
            ensuring its compliance with section 37 WIA91. A reasonable company would
            invest to improve its water supply system in order to be able to comply with its
            duties and, where such investment is not possible, ensure that its existing
            assets are performing as well as they can through maintenance and operational
            response. Evidence as to whether or not a company appropriately sets and/or
            meets its maintenance and operational internal goals is relevant for the purpose
            of assessing a company's compliance with section 37 WIA91. We also consider
            the wider factual background such as developments with respect to climate
            change and Covid-19, to assess what a reasonable company ought to have
            known about regulatory expectations and risks to their water supply systems.


2.81.       In light of the above, we expect a reasonable company to, as a minimum, do the
            following in order to comply with its section 37 WIA91 duties:


        •     Maintain key assets through proactive planned preventative maintenance and
              regular inspection to ensure supply resilience, particularly those assets which
              are single points of failure. We would expect that a reasonable company would
              adequately assess the impact that a planned outage would have on demand,
              consider any existing assets that may be out of service, and maintain
              sufﬁcient operational resources to avoid or reduce the severity of any loss of
              supply. For high demand or critical periods (i.e. summer and winter) we would
              expect a 'hands off' process when planning for maintenance with a clear,
              robust and auditable escalation process to ensure that the cumulative, real-
              world risk, interdependencies and consequence of ongoing works is assessed
              and either mitigated or triggers a "do not work" instruction.
        •     For service reservoirs, a maximum ten-year inspection cycle and aim for a
              year-round normal operation volume target of 80-85% of the total volume of
              the service reservoir.
        •     Maintain supply demand balance and headroom at a level that would ensure a
              secure and reliable water supply to premises in all of its areas. A reasonable
              company would maximise storage and therefore resilience in periods of
              historically high demand or leakage. Prior to predicted extreme weather
              events, a reasonable company would increase storage in reservoirs and
              increase detection and repair activity to resolve leaks on the network.
        •     While the above should reduce the risk of supply interruptions, we do
              recognise it is impossible to eliminate interruptions entirely, therefore in the
              event that customers' supply is affected, a reasonable company would respond

                                                32
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



              promptly to deploy alternative water supplies, communicate clearly and
              effectively with customers and follow pre-established emergency plans to
              support vulnerable customers and sites.

Application of Licence Condition P12

2.82.       As explained above, when assessing a company's compliance with Licence
            Condition P12 we consider whether it acted in a manner best calculated to
            ensure it has in place sufﬁcient ﬁnancial and management resources and
            systems of planning and internal control in order to carry out its functions and
            duties (including its duties under section 37 WIA91 and the SEMD).


2.83. A reasonable company would have systems and processes in place to identify
      and effectively minimise risks and to develop plans to mitigate the impact of
            those risks. We do not apply Licence Condition P12 in a vacuum but also consider
            the wider regulatory landscape and factual context (such as developments and
            work done by Ofwat and companies in relation to climate change, extreme
            weather incidents and Covid-19) that should have informed, and should
            continue to inform, the way a company ensures it is meeting its obligations.


2.84.       In the context of compliance with SEMD and section 37 WIA91 in particular, we
            set out below examples of behaviours indicating that a company is complying
            with Licence Condition P12. A reasonable company would:


        •     Establish and implement effective programmes for the routine inspection and
              maintenance of key assets by specialist teams, including clear remediation
              plans for deteriorating assets, planning for critical or high demand periods
              and holistically assessing system-wide risks.
        •     Use all available planning tools (area plans, PWPC, hydraulic models, root
              cause analysis). For hydraulic models, a reasonable company would review
              them every ﬁve years to ascertain whether any updates are required. A
              reasonable company would also ensure that it has a good understanding of
              root cause analysis and develop internal processes to conduct it after every
              incident and as part of its investment optioneering, including within price
              reviews.
        •     Mitigate known risks including risks posed by extreme weather and climate
              change.
        •     Respond effectively to supply interruptions and would "over-prepare" at the
              outset of an incident, rather than be reactive. It would reﬂect the support
              required for vulnerable sites within its emergency plan, ensure operational
              staff had the right information and controls (including knowing when to cease


                                                33
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



              planned maintenance in light of emerging events) and deploy sufﬁcient
              resources for its operational response.
        •     Learn lessons from past incidents, adjusting its emergency plans when
              vulnerabilities are identiﬁed and implementing recommendations from
              regulators, including DWI and Ofwat.
        •     Have adequate reporting metrics and appropriate oversight by the Executive
              and Board to understand a company's compliance with its general duty under
              section 37 WIA91.


2.4 Ofwat's powers and duties
2.85. Breaches of the general duty under section 37 WIA91 and licence conditions are
      enforceable by Ofwat under section 18 WIA91. If it is satisﬁed that there is, or
      there is likely to be, a breach, Ofwat is under a duty to impose an enforcement
            order. The purpose of an enforcement order is to secure compliance with the
            relevant obligation.


2.86. Section 19 WIA91 provides limited exceptions to the duty to enforce where: (i)
      the contravention is of a trivial nature; (ii) the company has given a formal
      undertaking to take remedial steps which we consider appropriate to secure
      compliance and is complying with that undertaking; or (iii) the duties imposed
      on Ofwat under Part I WIA91 preclude the making of the order (these duties are
      explained further below). Should a company fail to comply with an undertaking,
      Ofwat is under a further duty to pursue an enforcement order to ensure the
      company’s compliance. We provide further information on our approach to
      enforcement orders in Section 5.


2.87.       If Ofwat is satisﬁed that a company has contravened or is contravening any
            condition of its licence or a statutory requirement, it may impose a ﬁnancial
            penalty under section 22A(1) and (2) WIA91. We will consider several factors in
            determining the appropriate level of the penalty in the circumstances of each
            breach, ensuring that the amount does not exceed 10% of a company's relevant
            turnover in the preceding year. We explain our approach to calculating the
            ﬁnancial penalties and the relevant statutory provisions more fully in Section 6.

2.88. Ofwat will consider regulatory settlement where our powers allow us to do so.
            Regulatory settlement may be accepted in lieu of all, or part of, a ﬁnancial
            penalty. It can include ﬁnancial and/or non-ﬁnancial commitments from the
            company such as a redress package for customers or remediation of a failure.
            On a case-by-case basis, we will decide whether the content of any settlement


                                                34
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



            proposed by a company is acceptable. We are generally open to companies
            engaging in proactive discussions with us about potential settlement, at any
            stage of an investigation.


2.89. Before making an enforcement, order or conﬁrming a provisional enforcement
      order 70 and/or imposing a penalty, 71 Ofwat is required to consider whether it
            would be more appropriate to proceed under the Competition Act 1998 (CA98).


2.90. Section 2 WIA91 sets out the general duties that Ofwat must comply with in
            exercising and performing its powers and other duties. These include acting in a
            manner it considers best calculated to:


        •     further the consumer objective, i.e. to protect the interests of consumers;
        •     secure that the functions of a water undertaker, the activities are properly
              carried out;
        •     secure that water undertakers are able to ﬁnance the proper carrying out of
              their functions;
        •     further the resilience objective, i.e. secure long-term resilience of water
              supply systems as regards environmental pressures, population growth and
              changes in consumer behaviour and that undertakers take steps to be able to
              meet the need for the supply of water in the long term, including by promoting
              long-term planning and investment and measures to manage water resources
              in sustainable ways and to increase efﬁciency of water use / reduce demand
              for water.

2.91.       Under section 2(4) WIA91, Ofwat is also required to have regard to the principles
            of best regulatory practice (including that regulatory activities should be
            transparent, accountable, proportionate, consistent and targeted only at cases
            in which action is needed). Under section 108 of the Deregulation Act 2015, in
            carrying out our enforcement duty under section 18 WIA91, we must have regard
            to the desirability of promoting economic growth, and in particular in a way
            which ensures that regulatory action is only taken where needed; and any action
            is proportionate. We are also required to have regard to any guidance that the
            Secretary of State may issue in relation to the application of this duty. The
            current guidance 72 states that the growth duty does not legitimise non-
            compliance with other duties or objectives, and its purpose is not to achieve or




70

   Sections 22A(13) and 22A(14) WIA91.

reasonable, not dissuasive to investment and take account of willingness to
comply.

3. Our investigation and ﬁndings
3.1. Our investigation
3.1.       This section sets out the steps we have taken throughout the investigation and
           describes the level of engagement by South East Water.


3.2.       On 15 November 2023, we opened an investigation into South East Water's water
           supply system resilience. This was prompted by South East Water's poor
           performance in relation to its supply interruptions performance commitment 73
           and its supply resilience performance during several events from winter of 2018
           to the summer of 2023. This also followed a meeting between Ofwat’s Chair and
           Chief Executive and South East Water's Board, in which the Board was asked to
           explain the company's poor performance in the preceding 18 months, South East
           Water provided a response to our initial queries on 31 August 2023 (August 2023
           Response). 74


3.3.       On 15 November 2023, we also served South East Water with a formal notice
           under section 203 WIA91 (First s203 Notice) to gather further information from
           the company. In that notice, we explained that based on its August 2023
           Response, it appeared that South East Water may have contravened, be
           contravening, or be likely to contravene, the following:
       •    The requirements of its duty under section 37 WIA91 to develop and maintain
            an efﬁcient and economical system of water supply within its area and/or to
            ensure that all such arrangements for providing supplies of water and
            maintaining, improving and extending water mains/other pipes have been
            made as necessary for securing that the company is and continues to be able
            to meet its other relevant obligations; and/or
       •    Paragraph 12 of Licence Condition P in relation to the adequacy of its
            resources and systems of planning and internal control to enable it to carry
            out its regulated activities, particularly in relation to its obligations under
            section 37 WIA91 and the SEMD.

3.4.       In the First s203 Notice, Ofwat raised concerns about South East Water's supply
           system resilience. We asked South East Water to provide details of:




73
   A performance commitment or PC are performance levels each company should be seeking to achieve
during the AMP for a set of specific outcomes, see paragraph 2.43.
74
   South East Water's response on 31 August 2023 to Ofwat's meeting on 25 July 2023 and the follow up
letter on 27 July 2023.

                                                  37
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



       •     whether South East Water's systems of planning are sufficient to maintain
             supply system resilience, 75 headroom 76 and capacity to meet high demand
             events;
       •     the effectiveness of South East Water's management of financial resources
             regarding the delivery of PR19 investment projects to maintain supply during
             high demand events; and
       •     the availability and output from the company's water treatment works and
             South East Water's decision-making process during high demand periods.

3.5.       We requested information in response to six supply interruption events (Events),
           from February 2018 to June 2023:
       •     'Beast from the East' freeze/thaw event in February and March 2018
             (2018 Freeze Thaw Event);
       •     A period of high demand because of hot weather in August 2020
             (2020 High Demand Event);
       •     A loss of supply event as a result of Storm Eunice in February 2022
             (2022 Storm Eunice Event)
       •     A period of high demand because of hot weather in July 2022
             (2022 High Demand Event);
       •     A further freeze/thaw event between 15 December and 25 December 2022
             (2022 Freeze Thaw Event); and
       •     A period of high demand because of hot weather in June 2023
             (2023 High Demand Event).

3.6.       The Events in the relevant period for this investigation i.e. since 15 November
           2018 (Relevant Period) affected a total of 286,645 customers,77 with many of the
           events affecting the same areas in South East Water's Eastern region (see
           Appendix 10). When we refer to Events within the Relevant Period we exclude
           the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, however, the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event is relevant to
           this investigation as it is a signiﬁcant supply interruption event that prompted
           an industry-wide review by Ofwat where we stated: "The freeze and rapid thaw
           earlier this year was forecast and was not unprecedented. A number of water




75
   For the purpose of the First S203 Notice supply resilience included both asset resilience and systems
resilience. Asset resilience referred to the ability of companies' assets to function reliably and resist shocks
and stresses. This included asset health, the ability of assets to withstand variable operating conditions
and maintaining sufficient headroom and capacity to meet demand or capital investment to make a step
change in service levels. Systems Resilience included a system's ability to respond to emergencies, endure
and recover from asset failures and variable operating conditions.
76
   When we use the term 'headroom' in this document, we mean the difference between the amount of
water a company has available to supply (water available for use) and the volume of water it expects to
introduce into its network (distribution input) to meet demand.
77
   South East Water, Fourth s203 Response, 21 August 2025, p.12.

                                                      38
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited



             companies showed what can be done to serve customers in the face of bad
             weather. But too many companies were caught off guard and let people down,
             causing real hardship as a result. Our report shows there is no excuse for this
             level of failure. Four companies [including South East Water] have three months
             to satisfy us that they will be ready to perform at the level customers deserve the
             next time there is bad weather. We will take action if they don’t rise to this
             challenge. We expect every water company to take action on the back of this
             review and the industry as a whole to transform how it works together in
             situations like this". 78 As such, South East Water was put on notice regarding the
             importance of supply system resilience and its operational response to supply
             interruption events and it should have taken action to implement learnt lessons
             from the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event.


3.7.         As part of our First s203 Notice, we also informed the company that, given the
             seriousness of our concerns, we strongly encouraged it to take urgent steps to
             improve the resilience of its supply systems to prevent future supply
             interruptions and to provide to us with an action plan setting out how South East
             Water intended to address the alleged contraventions identiﬁed within the
             scope of the investigation, including appropriate timeframes and a clear
             explanation about how each step would contribute to addressing the root cause
             of supply system resilience risks in its Eastern region. 79


3.8.         On 20 December 2023, South East Water responded to the First s203 Notice
             (First s203 Response). Our assessment of the First s203 Response, highlighted
             several areas of concern, including issues with:
         •     Asset resilience, that is, the ability of companies' assets to function reliably
               and resist shocks and stresses. This includes asset health, the ability of assets
               to withstand variable operating conditions and maintain sufficient headroom
               and capacity to meet demand or capital investment to make a step change in
               service levels.
         •     Systems resilience, which includes a system's ability to respond to
               emergencies, endure and recover from asset failures and variable operating
               conditions, including power resilience risks.
         •     The company's approach to managing supply demand balance, incident
               response and single points of failure across its network.

3.2. About South East Water
3.2.1 Overview of South East Water's water supply system
3.27.        This section provides an overview of South East Water's water supply system that
             is relevant to the issues considered within this investigation, in particular its
             supply interruption performance.


3.28. South East Water is a water only company supplying 2.3 million customers, the
      majority of which are household customers. 84 It provides water services in three
             distinct areas: Western, 85 Sussex and Kent (Sussex and Kent together form the



84
     South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 3.13.
85
     Comprised of WRZ4 Bracknell and WRZ5 Franham.

                                                    43
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



          Eastern region). South East Water stated that its Western region is "one of the
          top-performing areas in the country with (among other things) minimal supply
          interruptions and customer complaints." 86 The Western region is not within the
          scope of this investigation, however, we note that while it was also affected by
          the extreme weather events discussed in this draft decision, this did not result
          in its customers experiencing severe supply interruptions. As such, our
          investigation makes no ﬁndings with respect to the Western region and to the
          extent that speciﬁc actions by South East Water are company-wide, we ﬁnd that
          it should have taken appropriate action with respect to the speciﬁc
          vulnerabilities (described below in paragraph 3.29) that exist in its Eastern
          region to mitigate against the effect of supply interruptions.


3.29. Our investigation has focused on South East Water's Eastern region as its
      customers have been disproportionately affected by supply interruptions
      compared with its Western region and other companies in the south east of
      England (see paragraphs 3.41, 3.45 and 3.46). South East Water claims that this
      is due to several factors that, while not unique to South East Water in of
      themselves, have a combined "unique" effect on its Eastern region: 87
      •     First, the south east of England is one of the most-water stressed regions in
            the UK, but, unlike other companies, South East Water says that there is no
            ability to abstract more water in its region. 88
      •     Second, because of its rural nature, it has not beneﬁted from the de-
            industralisation "windfall" where there are assets remaining after industry has
            ceased; instead it has seen "constant domestic growth", which has led to
            housing and population increases.89
      •     Third, South East Water widespread use of metering has led to a reduction in
            average water use but not in peak water use, which has increased. 90
      •     Fourth, the Eastern region is comprised of more than 250 small water sources
            that are not linked together through the supply network.91 This is due to
            historical mergers over time and impacts on the region's interconnectivity. It
            also means that, unlike other companies, it cannot create a grid. 92
      •     Fifth, its topography and linear nature, characterised by hills and valleys,
            which requires its long network to have several re-pumping stages to move

3.30. Finally, South East Water noted a further factor, namely, changing usage
      patterns due to Covid-19. 94 In its CMA Redetermination, South East Water shared
      data relating to rail usage, which suggested that the train lines which serve
        South East Water's region have not reported the same post-pandemic increases

        across most WRZs post-Covid-19, the totals remain generally below headroom
        forecast in South East Water's WRMP and most WRZs are now at a level similar to
        2016-17. We consider further in Section 3.3.2 whether Covid-19 should be
        considered to have a unique or lasting impact on South East Water's supply
        system.

3.32.   The Eastern region has over 220 separate production and transmission sites,
        including WTWs, treated water service reservoirs and booster pumps, which are
        utilised to maintain supplies to customers. There is limited interconnectivity
        between the Relevant WRZs.96 South East Water admits that both Kent and
        Sussex face limited connectivity and capacity headroom challenges which it
        states leads "to greater exposure to supply interruptions where there are large
        ﬂuctuations in customer demand." 97


3.33. The Sussex region of South East Water's network is a rural area made up of three
        WRZs: Tunbridge Wells (WRZ1), Haywards Heath (WRZ2) and Eastbourne (WRZ3).
        South East Water noted that the Sussex network, due to its linear nature, has
        several single points of failure, that is, parts of the system that have the
        potential, in case of a failure, to stop an entire system from working. It stated
        that single points of failure "exist where there are constraints that make
        providing additional redundancy difﬁcult or cost prohibitive, where historically
        system failure probability is extremely low, and the consequences and severity

3.43.   South East Water identiﬁed a number of actions to improve its supply

3.2.3 South East Water's planning processes
3.49. This section provides an overview of South East Water's planning processes that
      it uses, amongst other things, to manage supply resilience risks and its water
        resource management more generally. We have reviewed its WRMPs, corporate
        risk register, Executive and Board reporting processes and its area plans for the
        Relevant WRZs.

Water resource management planning process

3.50. The WIA91145 requires water companies to prepare and maintain a WRMP which
      sets out companies' intended approach to maintain a supply demand balance
        surplus for at least the next 25 years 146 and achieve a secure supply of water for
        customers and a protected and enhanced environment. 147 WRMPs should be
        prepared at least every ﬁve years, with annual reviews that include clear
        information regarding WAFU.148


3.51.   Part of the WRMP includes ensuring opportunities to supply other water
        companies or regional groups if a surplus of water exists. WRMPs should have no
        planning deﬁcits over the planning period and if there is a surplus, or there are
        additional sources available, companies should consider supply options with
        neighbouring companies or regional groups to see if this surplus is available for
        trading. 149 South East Water has stated that it is becoming less common to see a
        supply demand surplus for its area. 150


3.52.   Companies report on progress in delivering their WRMPs in annual reviews
        which they submit to Ofwat and the Environment Agency for review. Annual
        reviews include information on maintaining actual supply demand balance
        against the forecast provided in the WRMP, meeting forecasts for demand
        reductions (such as reducing leakage), and delivering supply schemes which
        contribute to the supply demand balance. Companies must also report on risks
        to delivery against WRMP forecasts, how these risks impact the ability to




145
    See sections 37A to 37D WIA91.
146
    Defra, Water Resources 2023-2024: Analysis of the Water Industry’s Annual Water Resources
Performance, 7 October 2024, link.
147
    Water resources planning guideline - GOV.UK.
148
    Water resources planning guideline - GOV.UK. We note that a water company must also produce a WRMP
if it has been directed to do so by the Secretary of State for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (if wholly
or mainly in England) and if there has been a material change in circumstances, for example one identified
through the annual review.
149
    Water resources planning guideline - GOV.UK.
150
    South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 2.21.

                                                    53
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        maintain a supply demand surplus, and interventions required as a result. In
        more material cases, this may result in an update or change to the companies'
        WRMP. If companies' WRMP performance is poor it results in a joint regulator
        letter requesting further action and progress reports.


3.53. As part of the WRMP, companies assess supply and demand forecasts.
        Companies' initial forecasts (Baseline Plan) include an assessment of WAFU,
        which represents the supply available in a speciﬁed design event (i.e. a drought
        with a 0.5% or 0.2% annual exceedance probability) from resources and
        transfers external to the WRZ. This is the supply available after outage and
        losses have been accounted for, as well as future factors to supply availability
        such as climate change and environmental abstraction reductions. WAFU
        typically declines over the planning horizon as a result of the future factors
        considered. Baseline demand forecasts include an assessment of demand from
        customers (household, that is, PCC, and non-household), plus losses such as
        leakage, in a specified design event. The baseline demand forecast also includes
        future factors affecting demand, such as population and industry growth, and
        therefore baseline demand forecasts typically rise over the planning horizon.


3.54.   The ﬁnal planning forecasts involved in the WRMP process (Final Plan) include
        an assessment of baseline WAFU and demand forecasts, plus new supply and
        demand side options proposed to be implemented over the planning horizon. As
        such, the Final Plan WAFU forecasts are typically higher than the Baseline Plan,
        and demand forecasts typically lower. We consider the Final Plan WAFU
        forecasts to be the most appropriate supply forecast against which to compare a
        company's actual demand. This compares how the company has planned to
        deliver sufﬁcient capacity and a resilience system with the actual demands
        experienced.


3.55. Companies also assess supply and demand forecasts based on different types of
      scenarios, including 'baseline dry year annual average' (DYAA) and 'baseline dry
        year critical period (peak week) demand' (DYCP). Companies set out in their
        Final Plan which scenario is critical to their plans, depending on any
        constrictions to their supply demand balances. We consider DYCP to be most
        aligned with company's planning for extreme events as opposed to the DYAA
        scenario which averages dry conditions across the year.


3.56. Throughout this document, we refer to the following WRMPs prepared by South
      East Water: WRMP14 for the period 2015-40, WRMP19 for the period 2020-45 and
        WRMP24 for the period 2025-50. Below we provide information regarding the
        WRMPs published and maintained by South East Water, in particular, noting its
                                             54
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



        management of supply system resilience risks. We explore these more fully with
        respect to planning in Section 3.3.2.


3.57.   In WRMP14 (2015–40) South East Water referenced operating in an area of
        "serious water stress" and noted that weather patterns were becoming less
        predictable, citing recent weather events.151


3.58. In WRMP19 (2020–45) South East Water planned for several transfers from
        different water zones to increase connectivity in its Eastern network. 152 It also
        noted that "[i]n preparing our WRMP19, [South East Water] have undertaken a
        wholesale review and update demand forecast methodology and generating new
        models including population and property forecasts." 153


3.59. In Ofwat's response to South East Water's draft WRMP19 consultation, we
      highlighted our concern that it "has not demonstrated that there is an
      appropriate balance between supply and demand type options, consistent with a
      twin track approach [i.e. improve water supply resilience through both
      increased supply and reduced demand]." 154

3.60. In WRMP24 (2025–50), South East Water used hydraulic modelling to present a
      sub-zonal supply demand balance approach. Stating "[t]his work will be

      a sub water resource zone level in the future, to maintain WRZ integrity." 155

      supply system means "maintaining the balance between supply and demand so
      that there is enough water to cope with short-term shocks", for example,
        drought or long-term challenges such as climate change. This included "all the
        operational infrastructure associated with the abstraction, treatment and
        distribution of water supplies to the region's households." 156

Corporate Risk Register

3.61.   To manage risks across its WRZs, South East Water has a Corporate Risk Register
        (CRR), which uses a process the company refers to as the Corporate Risk
        Methodology 157 to set out the process by which risks are identiﬁed and reported
        to the Executive Team and Board. The CRR also records mitigating actions and
        monitors and records progress on their delivery 158 and is the tool that South East
        Water "primarily" uses to manage risks to resilience. 159 South East Water has
        used the CRR in its current form during the Relevant Period and continues to do
        so. 160 A subset of the CRR is used by the company's Operations team to manage
        operational risks, which are reviewed quarterly by the Operations Senior
        Management Team. 161


3.62. The Corporate Risk Methodology involves South East Water assigning risks a
      score on a risk matrix which uses the combination of the likelihood of the risk
      occurring in the next twelve months, and the impact if the risk were to occur.
      Selecting a combination of likelihood and impact generates a score between one
      (low risk) and 25 (extreme risk), with each risk recorded in the matrix assigned a
        number on this basis.162 Board reporting of these risks is undertaken annually
        and only risks scored between 16 and 25, considered key risks, are reviewed. 163


3.63. All senior managers and heads of department within South East Water are
      responsible for identifying risks relevant to their area of activity and deﬁning
        and implementing mitigations. 164 Each senior manager in South East Water has
        ownership of the CRR for their area, with managers meeting quarterly to review
        risks. 165 According to the Corporate Risk Methodology, the CRR is reviewed
        regularly by South East Water's Executive to ensure risks are consistently
        assessed and documented, and actions to reduce risks are monitored.166 Senior

        managers are also encouraged to update the risk register as required between

3.64. We recognise that not every risk becomes an issue or requires positive steps to
      be taken in mitigation, nor that every risk is foreseen. When we refer to relevant
      risks identiﬁed in the CRR throughout this draft decision we are doing so to
        demonstrate when South East Water was aware of speciﬁc risks. We then
        consider what a reasonable company, having identiﬁed such a risk, might be
        expected to do as a proportionate response to that risk in order to comply with
        its duties under section 37 WIA91 and Licence Condition P (recognising that
        there may be a range of reasonable options available), and whether South East
        Water's response was consistent with an approach to be expected of a
        reasonable company.

Executive and Board Reporting

3.65. During the Relevant Period, the Board reviewed monthly, quarterly, and annual
      reports on performance which highlight risks and business or operational issues
      as they arise. This includes impact areas for risk, action taken and target risk
      score. 168 Every year, South East Water presents an annual risk report to its Board
        which focuses on catastrophic risks but also outlines "top" medium, high and
        extreme risks.169


3.66. We note, in particular, that on 25 July 2023, at the time that Ofwat was making
      initial enquiries with South East Water's Board, the Board was briefed on its
      "duty under s37 WIA91." 170 It was told that this duty "refers not only to investment
      but also to processes and management resources" with links to Licence
        Condition P. It was told that this duty included water resources planning and
        drought management, asset management and investment in the water supply
        system, including investment in growth and resilience. It also noted its SEMD
        obligations regarding interruptions in terms of emergency planning, incident
        management and alternative water supplies.

              planned works to be undertaken.

3.3 Detailed consideration of the evidence and our
ﬁndings
3.70. As noted above in Section 3.1, we have obtained numerous pieces of evidence in
      the course of this investigation, which we have carefully reviewed and
            considered. In Sections 3.3.1-3.3.3, we detail the relevant evidence and our
            ﬁndings regarding South East Water's approach to maintenance of key assets;
            planning and investment; and operational response to and preparedness for the
            Events and consider the extent to which this was effective in maintaining an
            efﬁcient and economical supply system as required by section 37 WIA91 and
            whether South East Water maintained adequate ﬁnancial resources and
            facilities, management resources and systems of planning and internal control
            under Licence Condition P12.

3.3.1 Maintenance of key assets
3.71.       Asset health checks and maintenance are essential to maintaining resilient
            supply systems and ensuring that an organisation is prepared to respond to
            unplanned events. Good asset management practice requires a clear and up-to-
            date understanding of how sites are performing, as well as timely action to
            address issues and maintain required levels of performance. However, the
            evidence provided to us by South East Water shows signiﬁcant weaknesses in its
            approach to asset monitoring and maintenance in the Eastern region. These
            weaknesses are particularly concerning given the known vulnerabilities in that
            region and raise concerns about whether the company’s arrangements are
            sufﬁcient to manage asset risks and maintain reliable services for customers.

General approach to maintenance
3.72.       Below we set out the evidence we considered with respect to the company's
            general approach to asset maintenance as set out in its Area Plans, Operational
            Method Statements (OMS) system and statements that it has made publicly and

        through our assessment of the control room logs, and discussion with

3.78.   In April 2022, the South East Water Board was told that a key driver most likely to
        affect its 'Water 2050: Strategic Direction Statement' was "deteriorating asset
        health".183


3.79.   South East Water has stated that its planned preventative maintenance activity
        does not require a site shut down or reduced output as many of the assets have
        duty / standby arrangements so activity can be carried out on equipment
        without impacting the site operation; however, more involved activities will
        require site shutdowns and are therefore planned outside of forecasted peak
        demand periods. 184 It has also explained that as seasonal variations in river ﬂows
        and limits, under the Environment Agency's permitting regime, makes it more
        difﬁcult to maintain its assets in summer (June, July, August),185 means it is
        more likely to be appropriate to undertake remedial work in winter (December,
        January, February).186 This is because during summer, river ﬂows are lower so
        water upstream may be released to increase ﬂows, allowing continued
        abstraction within the limits of permits.187 In winter, river ﬂows are generally
        higher which mean more headroom is available so that, if any unexpected
        issues arise, they should be addressed without causing supply interruptions. 188
        South East Water refers to this as "routine winter maintenance" 189 and noted that
        this was "typical" practice despite the fact a large part of its maintenance
        programme takes place between autumn and late spring when customers use
        less water and it can take sites ofﬂine while continuing to meet demand. 190 In its
        Representations, South East Water stated that "March is generally a period of
        low demand, and improving weather. We therefore use the time to undertake
        maintenance at supply and reservoir sites and storage in these areas are
        reduced." 191

3.80. In response to a query, South East Water provided a version of the OMS system
        (issued on 15 January 2024). The OMS is a work system and centralised process

3.81.   Prior to the beginning of the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event in February, Barcombe
        WTW was out for maintenance (see Appendix 1), as part of South East Water's
        stated practice of routine winter maintenance. From as early as 16 February
        2018, the Met ofﬁce provided an early warning of the potential for very cold
        weather and snow for the UK, yet, an incident team was only deployed by South
        East Water on 26 February 2018 and emergency plan was put in place on 27
        February 2018. Freezing conditions continued into March 2018, with the Met
        Ofﬁce issuing frequent risk speciﬁc and regional weather warnings. In its
        subsequent review of the incident (2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan), South
        East Water itself recognised that it needed to make Barcombe WTW more able to
        undertake planned maintenance without causing additional supply risk. 193
        However, Barcombe WTW again was out of service as a result of a planned
        outage on 7 December 2022 in the lead up to the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event
        (paragraph A.5.6). In its Representations, South East Water stated that given the
        signiﬁcant level of capital maintenance at Barcombe WTW and due to the size of
        the site and the two independent treatment streams, it is "programming in
        activities that limit the capacity of the works to times when customer demand is
        lower, and this is a multiple year maintenance scheme…it is therefore
        unsurprising that parts of the works can be out of service when extreme weather
        occurs". 194


3.82. Following the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, Ofwat published a report, noting "some
      companies were not able to manage the increase in water use because parts of
      their distribution networks were closed for planned maintenance or
        refurbishment works. This put greater pressure on supplies and lead to a greater
        number of customers being without supply for a longer period. In some cases,
        work was sped up or deferred so that the key operational assets, such as
        treatment works, came back online. But this was not always possible. We
        appreciate that assets (such as treatment works or service reservoirs) need to

3.83. Following the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, the DWI noted that companies typically
      use the winter period to carry out intrusive maintenance onsite, as summer
        demand is usually higher, however, as weather patterns becomes more
        unpredictable with climate change, the DWI recommended that there is
        sufﬁcient resilience, within the available asset base, to provide a continual
        piped supply all year round. 196 It put forward recommendations for all
        companies:
        •    Review the capacity of its supply networks to withstand signiﬁcant, but not
             unforeseeable, weather related and other supply interruption challenges.
        •    Review local reasonable worst-case scenarios which should include
             reviewing the potential scale of an incident and the worst-case scenario in
             response to an incident. 197

3.84.   Other key assets, alongside Barcombe WTW, have also been taken out of supply
        during winter periods in the lead up to several Events as part of planned routine
        maintenance. For example, during the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, Saints Hill WTW
        and Tunbridge WTW were also out of service (paragraph A.5.6). South East Water
        should have also learnt the lesson from the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event about
        having key sites out for maintenance during winter; however, it did not, as it was
        also a feature that delayed South East Water's operational response to the 2022
        Freeze Thaw Event (paragraph A.3.1).


3.85. As stated above, South East Water acknowledged that summer is a period of
        higher risk for planned maintenance activity due to high demand. However,
        during the 2023 High Demand Event, which took place in June, three key assets
        were out of service due to maintenance: St Michael's, Hollingbourne and
        Aylesford service reservoirs. South East Water says that the maintenance at the
        ﬁrst two service reservoirs was required for water quality purposes by the DWI;
        Aylesford service reservoir was out of service because of remedial work due to
        sinkholes since 2021. 198

3.96. The average age of South East Water's individual service reservoirs or water
      tower compartments 229 is 65 years.230 As part of PR09, Ofwat requested asset
           inventory and health proﬁle information from all companies. Asset condition was
           categorised by Ofwat's information request according to their functionality and
           other factors, with the following being the three worst ranking categories:
       •     Grade three condition requires a review of the condition of the asset in the
             medium term, and is where an asset can be deﬁned as being functionally
             sound, but with appearance significantly affected by deterioration, structure
             is marginal in its capacity to prevent leakage, and mechanical and electrical
             plant and components functioning adequately but with some reduced
             efficiency and minor failures.231
       •     Grade four condition requires a major overhaul/replacement in the medium
             term and applies where assets are considered as deteriorated, with a
             significant effect on performance due to leakage or other structural problems,
             and where, although mechanical and electrical plant and components are
             functioning, significant maintenance to remain operational is required. 232
       •     Grade ﬁve condition requires a major overhaul/replacement in the short term
             and is the worst condition classification, which applies where assets are
             considered to have serious structural problems affecting performance, as well
             as the life of the mechanical, electric plant and components being
             exceeded.233

       •     South East Water provided documents explaining its asset stock and condition
             proﬁle from PR09 which highlighted that, at the time, 44.8% of its service
             reservoirs and 39.9% of its water towers were in grade three condition 234 and
             0.56% of South East Water's service reservoirs were at condition four and
             ﬁve across both Western and Eastern regions. This asset inventory and health
             profile was again requested by Ofwat at PR24. 235 It showed that 28% of its
             service reservoir assets were at grade three condition, and 44.1% of South
             East Water's service reservoirs and 47.2% of its water towers were in either
             grade four or grade ﬁve condition. 236

3.100. Between 2017–22, South East Water identiﬁed a risk on its CRR in relation to
       drinking water quality. The risk was that its service reservoirs could be failing
        water quality standards due to insufﬁcient inspection and maintenance
        opportunities which are required to maintain a minimum level of resilience. This
        was recorded as a high risk on its CRR in 2017 where it remained until 2022
        (when it was amalgamated with the risk that ingress at service reservoir sites
        could lead to contamination, which was also categorised as high risk). 248

3.101. During the Relevant Period, South East Water's target for its service reservoir
       maintenance programme was to maintain a maximum inspection frequency of
       10 years for all service reservoirs. 249 From 2016 to 2021, the average number of
       South East Water's service reservoirs that fell outside the 10-year inspection
        and maintenance was 44 per year; 250 this amounts to almost 20% of South East
        Water's total number of service reservoirs. South East Water stated in its
        Second s203 Response that, while a number of sites remain outside the required
        frequency of internal inspection, this does not inherently mean that they
        present a signiﬁcant increase in water quality risk as enhanced water quality
        monitoring is completed at all sites outside the 10-year frequency to verify the
        quality of water in supply. 251 However, on 15 February 2022, the DWI served
        South East Water with a notice to ensure that all of its service reservoirs were
        inspected within 10 years. 252 The notice stated "there is or has been a signiﬁcant
        risk of supplying water from South East Water tanks and service reservoir assets
        (and associated assets as applicable) that could constitute a potential danger to
        human health or could be unwholesome".253

3.102. As part of its Further Updated Action Plan, South East Water planned that 24
       inspections would be completed by March 2025 for its 2024-25 reservoir
        inspection programme, however, only 13 of these 24 inspections were
        completed in August 2025 and the work remains "ongoing". 254 This indicates
        that the risk identiﬁed by the DWI in relation to drinking water quality, and the
        potential resulting impact that could have on supply, is ongoing.

3.103. Maintenance of service reservoirs was consistently recorded on the CRR as a
          medium risk from 2017 until 2024. 255 South East Water identiﬁed that it may not
          be able to take complex service reservoirs out of service for maintenance
          without customer disruption. South East Water noted the reason for this in its
          Area Plans for the Relevant WRZs: 256
      •    Some service reservoirs are single cell which means that the service reservoir
           can either only be out of service for a short duration of time, or that an
           alternative supply is required or that the service reservoir should not be taken
           out of service at all.
      •    Some of the service reservoirs are located in sites of special scientiﬁc interest
           (SSSIs) which restricts access for maintenance activities.
      •    Some of its service reservoirs (predominately in WRZ8) cannot be fully drained
           down for maintenance as they can cause ﬂooding. Eight service reservoirs 257
           that were listed in the DWI's notice dated February 2022 are in WRZ8. 258

3.105. In the DWI report following the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, it noted that "several
       companies had taken the opportunity prior to the predicted event to increase
        storage in all treated water reservoirs. They had also taken the opportunity to
        increase detection and repair activity to resolve leaks on the network prior to the
        event, but also during the event, so that issues were dealt with swiftly. The
        Inspectorate considers this best practice and recommends that companies
        consider this when entering periods of uncertainty". 261

3.106. It is imperative for South East Water to maintain adequate storage levels for
       several reasons. First, South East Water has limited localised storage compared
        to other companies, which increases the risk of supply interruption during
        extreme demand surges as its storage systems were originally designed and
        developed to support smaller local WTWs.


3.107. Second, South East Water has explained that its patterns of water consumption
       vary seasonally with weather patterns and with different regional impacts.
        Demand patterns also vary each day (with demands higher in the morning and
        evening, and lower demands overnight), as well as varying each week (as there
        tends to be more demand at weekends when there are more people at home). 262
        These daily diurnal patterns can generally be accommodated within its treated
        water storage systems, but prolonged seasonal demand patterns can only be
        met through increased production capacity and/or storage.263


3.108. Third, one of the known vulnerabilities (paragraph 3.29) in its Eastern region
       area is topography and its linear network that requires several re-pumping
       stages to move water via interim service reservoirs and booster pumps. 264
        [CONFIDENTIAL]

3.112. To supplement the evidence regarding maintenance activities at service
        reservoirs, we analysed storage levels at South East Water's service reservoirs
        over the period 2022 to 2024. This included in the lead up to the 2022 Storm
        Eunice Event, 2022 High Demand Event, 2022 Freeze Thaw Event and 2023 High
        Demand Event. We assessed the trends in stock levels at individual reservoirs,
        water resource zone and company level. The daily average ﬁgures, per cell, were
        plotted against the relevant events and the average service reservoir level
        percentages across three time periods: 7 days prior to each event, 31 days prior
        to each event and 62 days prior to each event for each WRZ (Appendix 9, Figures
        9.1 to 9.4). Resilience in this context refers to potable water stored to act as a
        buffer against supply shocks. This assessment did not consider potential sub-
        zonal interconnectivity issues, although these have been considered elsewhere
        in this decision. The analysis in Appendix 9 was designed to look at broad trends
        to consider the overall picture of how South East Water maintains storage levels
        at service reservoirs and its level of preparedness in the lead up to the Events
        during the Relevant Period (Storage Levels Analysis). We consider that a
        reasonable company would maximise storage and therefore resilience in periods
        of historically high demand or leakage. As outlined above, this includes having
        'hands-off' periods for all but essential maintenance during summer and winter.

3.118. South East Water argued that we were inconsistent in our application of the 80–
          85% target as it considers that Ofwat has presented the target as, both, a year-
          round target and a level that is mainly relevant ahead of periods of high demand
          or severe weather. 283 Further, it claimed that treating 80–85% as a permanent
          target misunderstands the role of storage as a buffer. 284 South East Water noted
          that any target, including that applied in the Storage Levels Analysis, should
          account for diurnal patterns (see paragraph 2.10) which mean storage levels
          ﬂuctuate signiﬁcantly over the day as demand changes, with variations differing
          by reservoir and in some cases fell below 20% of capacity, particularly during
          critical demand periods. 285

3.120. At Figure 9.5, Appendix 9, we used South East Water's total usable storage per
       WRZ and company-wide to assess this against the single highest recorded daily
       DI ﬁgure from 2016 to 2024 to provide a picture of the observed worst case
       scenario for demand based on this previous history and to estimate the number
       of resilience days/hours in the event of no outputs from WTWs. We found that all
       but one WRZ has a resilience time of greater than 24 hours – a ﬁgure often
       adopted as soft lower storage target by water companies. Indeed, South East
       Water in its Area Plans, identiﬁed as an "aspiration" that there should be no less
       than 24 hours storage and no more than 36 hours storage available, to provide a
          buffer to customer impact for reactive and planned works to be undertaken (see
          paragraphs 3.68 and 3.69). In addition to this, three zones that were impacted
          by the Events in the Relevant Period (WRZs 2, 3 and 7) have more than the usual
          industry target of 36 hours storage.

Trunk mains
3.122. We have considered evidence below regarding the way South East Water
       monitors and maintains its trunk mains, including strategic valves.

Condition of trunk mains

3.123. Since December 2017 and throughout the Relevant Period, South East Water has
       identiﬁed and recorded failures of trunk mains and associated risks on its CRR
        as either "high" or "extreme". South East Water noted that the risks associated
        with trunk main failures, include an increased demand through bursts/leaks or
        failure to meet supply interruption PCL. 292 At the time of including these risks on
        the CRR, one of the causes was identiﬁed as lack of monitoring to pre-empt
        damage or issues on trunk mains and the consequence being signiﬁcant
        interruption minutes and service reservoirs emptying. 293

3.124. For risks that are classiﬁed as extreme or high in its CRR, South East Water has
       risk reviews which take place at least monthly. There is also a regular proactive
       review of deterioration modelling (at least every ﬁve years for the business plan)
       and identifying the most high-risk mains for replacement based on high
       likelihood of high consequence of burst. 294 The mains that are identiﬁed as high
       risk are the mains that are put forward for South East Water's annual mains
        renewal programme. 295 Additionally, South East Water uses Maximo to log and
        track the planned preventative maintenance that needs to be completed on
        mains and other assets.296

3.125. South East Water notes that strategic trunk main failure is a recurring entry on
        its CRR simply because of the fundamental nature of trunk mains. Given that
        trunk mains are assets through which water is transmitted into sub-zones,
        South East Water considers that any failure of a trunk main would inherently be
        "catastrophic".297 South East Water's target for trunk mains failure is a risk score
        of 17, which falls into the "high" category. It considers that this score, provided
        all mitigating actions are completed, will reduce the likelihood of failure from
        "possible" to "unlikely." 298

3.126. South East Water has identiﬁed that there is a link between mains bursts and
       extreme weather, particularly during freeze thaws. South East Water noted that
        this trend is especially apparent in its Eastern region as, given its particular
        geological environment, extreme weather causes increased rates of mains
        bursts due to ground movements.299

3.127. During the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, a rapid thaw led to an increase in South
       East Water's mains bursting and South East Water experienced an increase in
       demand of over 20% requiring an extra 105Ml/d of water. 18Ml/d of the extra
       demand was due to bursts on its network with the remaining 87Ml/d a result of
       customer pipes and taps. 300


3.128. Given that it is [CONFIDENTIAL] (paragraph 3.34), Barcombe WTW and its

       Concerns raised included the risk of reduced outage due to the inability to
       undertake the required maintenance and repairs. 301

Maintenance of trunk mains

3.135. South East Water has shared details of how it manages the risk of "strategic"
        mains failure and it stated that it had an "asset needs request process" in place
        which is a reactive way of raising maintenance and repairs from across the
        business. Strategic mains can be raised this way for replacement or for further
        investigation, usually when several bursts occur along the same section of
        main which indicates a deterioration of the main, i.e. that it is past its asset life
        and replacement is the only option. 310 Such requests may require a change to
        the corporate plan programme and fall outside agreed funding and therefore
        require a programme re-prioritisation to accommodate any reactive
        replacement. 311


3.136. In contrast, South East Water said it proactively inspects valves (which are vital
       for maintaining pressure and ﬂow in a water distribution system, see paragraph
       2.17) typically annually or up to a three yearly basis depending on priority. 312
       South East Water provided details of the reactive and proactive maintenance
       activities in relation to valves during the Events in the Relevant Period and
        recorded in Maximo. South East Water explained that jobs appeared "50 days
        overdue" because they are raised in batches at the start of each year.313 There
        were four instances in which more than 250 planned preventative maintenance
        workorders were closed in a single day.

Boreholes
3.142. South East Water is particularly reliant on ground water abstraction to meet
       customer demand. 322 There are a total of 285 boreholes and wells presently
       within its supply system in both its Eastern and Western regions. 323

       a borehole maintenance programme.324 The programme is based on the
       principle that timely maintenance reduces outage risk and extends asset life.325
        The frequency of maintenance for boreholes is largely determined by the
        hydrogeological environment, and in particular the groundwater quality regime

Non-operational boreholes replacement programme

3.148. South East Water classiﬁes boreholes as non-operational when they have been
        out of service for more than three months and no remedial work is currently
        underway.338 The company notes several possible reasons for a borehole
        becoming non-operational, including aggressive water chemistry, structural
        failure of the borehole, the absence of an abstraction licence, poor raw water
        quality, and inadequate or low yield.339

3.149. South East Water had concerns about non-operational boreholes from as early
       as 2019, as identiﬁed in its Area Plans for WRZs 1, 2, 3 and 8. 340 It highlighted
        whether non-operational boreholes were required for operational and/or
        monitoring purposes. Non-operational boreholes play an important role in
        monitoring as they provide essential data on groundwater levels, water quality,
        and asset condition to support effective resource management and future
        resilience. As of August 2024, South East Water had a total of 43 non-
        operational boreholes within its entire network. 341
