Ofwat's ﬁndings on asset maintenance
3.156. Below we set out our ﬁndings relating to South East Water's monitoring and
       maintenance of its water supply system and its compliance with section 37
        WIA91 and Condition P12.

General approach to maintenance
3.157. As set out in Section 2 above, under section 37 WIA91, water undertakers are
       required to develop and maintain an economical and efﬁcient supply system,
        that is – a supply system that is ﬁt for purpose. A system delivered and
        maintained at a proportionate cost, but which cannot be relied on to meet an
        undertaker's supply, [water quality] and other obligations, is not economic and
        efﬁcient. Monitoring and maintenance of supply system assets, such that this
        system operates economically and efﬁciently, is a key aspect of this duty and
        applies to all components of a supply system such as boreholes, trunk mains and
        service reservoirs. Water undertakers are also required to have arrangements in
        place to provide water supplies and maintain, improve and extent water mains
        such that they are able to continue to meet their supply, [water quality] and
        other Part III WIA91 obligations, i.e. to ensure there is not a real risk they would
        not be able to comply with these obligations.


3.158. The evidence shows that throughout the Relevant Period; South East Water has
       not been as proactive in terms of maintaining its assets as a reasonable water
       company should have been. South East Water has not updated its Area Plans
       since 2019 and 2020, and we have seen no evidence that it has delivered on the
       mitigating actions it identiﬁed in the Area Plans. While the introduction of AIPOS
       may assist in prioritisation and risk management may assist in future, there is
       limited evidence of a proactive approach to maintenance prior to this. This is
        evident as from as from as early as its CRR Board reports in 2018 South East
        Water identiﬁed a "lack of proactive monitoring of asset availability" 358 this
        continued to be a feature of its Area Plans in 2019 and 2020 with "very little"
        ability to monitor performance of its sites, assets and network. This continued
        into January 2022 with staff raising concerns about lack of knowledge of the
        network and South East Water itself identifying the need for further root cause
        analysis to support its understanding of its network. We examine the importance
        of root cause analysis, including with respect to asset health and maintenance
        in Section 3.3.2.



358
    South East Water, Key Risk Actions Control, Undated, Appendix 192, First s203 Response, p.5863; South
East Water, Annual Board Risk Review 2019, Appendix 113, p.2468 – 2480; South East Water, Board Annual
Risk Report, December 2018, p.3, 4 and 9, Second s203 Response, 22 July 2024.

                                                   89
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited




3.159. We note South East Water's differing performance in its Eastern compared to its
        Western region, however, disagree that this exonerates its maintenance
        approach. South East Water's Eastern region presents particular known
        vulnerabilities which do not exist in its Western region (paragraph 3.28) and the
        evidence below regarding maintenance of its boreholes, service reservoirs and
        trunk mains maintenance indicates that the company should adopt a different
        approach to asset management for its Eastern region.

3.160. South East Water's general approach to maintenance is out of step with
        accepted industry practice, which is not to schedule planned maintenance
        works during the summer (June, July, August) and winter (December, January,
        February). South East Water has itself acknowledged that it is typical to
        undertake these maintenance activities in autumn and spring and suggests that
        March is its preferred time for maintenance when there is low demand. Further,
        it Ofwat's 2018 Freeze Thaw Event report stated it "is best to schedule these
        works so that they are able to maintain supply to customers in the event of
        severe weather" 359 and the DWI's 2022 Freeze Thaw Event report recommended
        sufﬁcient resilience, within the available asset base, to provide a continual
        piped supply all year round. 360


3.161. At paragraph 3.85, there was evidence of South East Water undertaking
       maintenance during summer 2023 that it stated was "required for water quality
       purposes by the DWI". We note that the DWI notice for inspection of
       [CONFIDENTIAL] service reservoir was issued on 15 February 2022. 361 There
       was no speciﬁc requirement to complete this work during summer. It is up to
       the company to plan its inspections to be within the ten-year programme and to
        schedule them in a way that avoids peak demand periods which could impact on
        stored volumes available.


3.162. South East Water said that it undertakes maintenance during winter periods to
       minimise risk of disruption to supply. South East Water has explained that due to
        seasonal variations in river ﬂow, with river ﬂows generally higher in the winter, it
        undertakes most of its planned maintenance during the winter period due to
        more headroom availability, which it considers reduces the risk of supply




359
    Ofwat, Out in the cold – water companies' response to the Beast from the East, 19 June 2018, link, p.18.
360
    DWI, Consolidated Review of the Widespread Loss of Supplies Arising from the Freeze/Thaw Event
Affecting England in December 2022, 17 May 2023, link, p.6.
361
    Notice of regulation: South East Water Limited - 10+ Years Reservoir Inspection Programme - Drinking
Water Inspectorate

                                                     90
          Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                   on South East Water Limited



           interruptions if unexpected problems arise. We disagree with South East Water's
           assessment that winter should not be classiﬁed as a peak period. While
           customer demand may be lower than summer, there is greater risk of
           leakage/unaccounted for water which increases during winter. As such
           distribution input is often comparable between these two periods.


3.163. Further, any essential winter maintenance must be adjusted to consider actual
       weather conditions. Having experienced the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, South East
           Water should have considered this risk during the following winter periods. To
           clarify, we are not seeking that there be a total ban on maintenance during peak
           periods, however, we do consider that a reasonable company would deploy a
           critical or 'hands off' process at these times. We note that South East Water's
           OMS system (see paragraph 3.80) does not include any reference or guidance to
           consideration of critical demand or weather-related periods of water stress.
           While the OMS appears to be an adequate permitting system for individual
           tasks, it does not track cumulative risk levels and interdependencies between
           potential planned maintenance activities, particularly in times of water stress. A
           reasonable company would have a robust and auditable process to ensure that
           the cumulative, real-world risk, interdependencies and consequence of ongoing
           works is assessed and either mitigated or factored into a "do not work"
           instruction. If maintenance during peak periods was critical, we would expect
           that a reasonable company would holistically assess the impact that the planned
           outage would have on demand, consider the headroom available and any
           existing assets that may be out of service, and maintain sufﬁcient operational
           resources to avoid or reduce the severity of loss of supply events.

3.164. Despite South East Water's claim that "it has undertaken signiﬁcant
           maintenance and proposed ambitious plans to ensure security and reliability of
           the site's supply," 362 Barcombe WTW illustrates that this is not the case as
           maintenance of [CONFIDENTIAL] like the No.2 contact tank has been
           repeatedly delayed, temporary, or reactive, and long-standing issues with the
           bankside storage have left the site vulnerable to outages and water quality risks.


3.165. Since 2007, South East Water has faced ongoing issues with the Barcombe No.2
       contact tank. Although planned maintenance prior to the 2022 Freeze Thaw
           Event addressed the 2018 DWI notice (paragraph 3.89), the company had been
           aware of problems with the tank for many years. While further issues were

      South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 6.131.

                                   on South East Water Limited



           identiﬁed through its ten-year inspection programme, we would have expected
           an accelerated inspection schedule [CONFIDENTIAL], given the known and
           recurring problems. We acknowledge the need to balance short-term supply
           risks with longer-term maintenance, but the contact tank nonetheless took
           considerable time to rectify. We acknowledge South East Water’s rationale for
           the lengthy repair of the contact tank and commend its decision to prioritise
           upgrades to the treatment processes, allowing the tank to be bypassed rather

           recognise the signiﬁcant resources invested to achieve this. However, the nearly

           the works is exceptionally long for a [CONFIDENTIAL] WTW with known water
           quality risks.


3.166. South East Water stated in its 2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan that it would
       review how to make Barcombe WTW more able to undertake planned
       maintenance without causing additional supply risk. However, it said that "a
       request for an allowance to mitigate asset failure at Barcombe WTW in PR19…was
       rejected by Ofwat". 363 We outline the history of price review funding requests at
       Section 3.3.2, which were either rejected because of lack of evidence or
       because allowances had been previously been made. Further, we note that
       irrespective of the outcome of price review processes, it is the responsibility of
       South East Water to ensure that it meets its legal obligations. South East Water
       stated that for any Event where long-range weather forecasts indicated extreme
       weather, it restored Barcombe WTW to full capacity within a short space of
           time. 364


3.167. In terms of Barcombe's bankside storage, we are concerned that it only provides
       20% of its designed storage (out of a total of seven days) due to sludge and silt
       build up which, according to South East Water, provides two/three days of
           supply (paragraph 3.91). We consider that this lack of effective storage, puts
           Barcombe WTW at greater risk of outage as there is no redundancy in the event
           of a pollution event on the River Ouse. Ultimately, if the River Ouse was to be
           affected by a pollution incident, the bankside storage reservoir would be the
           only alternative water supply to Barcombe WTW. South East Water considers the
           two/three day supply that the bankside storage gives is adequate in a pollution
           event (paragraph 3.91) but we do not agree with this due to the large population
           Barcombe WTW serves. It has the capability to provide greater storage and, as

                                   on South East Water Limited



           such, it would be economical and efﬁcient for South East Water to maximise
           storage available. Additionally, due to the current state of the bankside storage
           there is no guarantee any storage would be available in the peak summer

3.168. We consider that South East Water should have built resilience within Barcombe
           WTW and its bankside storage via an ongoing dredging and maintenance
           programme. South East Water has known about the issues of siltation and algal
           blooms since 2019, yet it took two years for the Jacobs' study to be

           work as part of an enhancement scheme of PR24. In its Representations, South
           East Water questioned what timescales it should have set when it carried out
           the study, emphasising that the delay in commissioning the study did not cause
           any harm or risk. 365 While we acknowledge that no direct harm was caused by
           this delay, we would expect that, as Barcombe WTW is [CONFIDENTIAL], and
           considering the known issues of siltation and algal blooms, South East Water
           should have commissioned the Jacobs study as soon as practically possible.


3.169. Further, as South East Water acknowledged during the PR24 ﬁnal determination
       process that desilting the bankside storage (paragraph 3.91) was base
           expenditure that it should have been funded to complete throughout the PR19
           period (which overlaps with the Relevant Period), South East Water should have
           been conducting a dredging and maintenance programme throughout the
           Relevant Period.


3.170. As part of its Representations, South East Water stated that Ofwat "rejected" a
           scheme to provide a fully independent No.3 works at Barcombe WTW (paragraph
           3.93). For clariﬁcation, Ofwat did not reject the scheme itself, it challenged the
           enhancement costs as there were concerns over base cost overlap; a lack of
           evidence to justify the need for enhancement funding as it was considered that
           the works was driven by growth (which should be covered through base
           funding); and concerns over cost inefﬁciency as there was a lack of external
           benchmarking and third party assurance evidence. While we challenged 20% on
           the base cost overlap issue and challenged 10% for the cost efﬁciency issue,
           South East Water were allowed 70% of its enhancement request for the

        independent No.3 works at draft determination stage, this was then progressed
        to 100% at the ﬁnal determination stage. 366

Service reservoirs
3.171. We consider that South East Water has not inspected and maintained its service
       reservoirs in an effective and timely manner, leading to deterioration in service
        reservoir condition and an increase to risk of loss of supply and to water quality.

3.172. Ofwat notes that the PR09 and PR24 data on service reservoir asset condition
       cannot be directly compared (paragraphs 3.96-3.98), however, it does show a
        deterioration of assets over both periods. We consider this downward trend
        would have been prevented through regular inspection and maintenance
        activities. As detailed below, South East Water continues to be repeatedly
        behind its targets in relation to inspections of service reservoirs thus risking the
        further deterioration of the condition of these assets.

3.173. As set out in our summary of evidence above, between 2016-21 the average
        number of South East Water's service reservoirs that fell outside the 10-year
        inspection target was 44 per year. This represents almost 20% of South East
        Water's service reservoirs across both Eastern and Western regions. Eight
        service reservoirs listed in the DWI notice are located in WRZ8, a zone known to
        struggle during periods of prolonged demand and could not be drained down
        due to risk of ﬂooding according to the WRZ8 Area Plan (paragraph 3.103). There
        are only 17 service reservoirs in total in WRZ8, which means just under half of
        the service reservoirs in WRZ8 carry the risk of inoperability with signiﬁcant risk
        of loss of supply to customers. The evidence also shows that South East Water
        has failed to meet its targets as required by the DWI notice dated 15 February
        2022 for its reservoir inspection programme, completing only 13 of 24
        inspections by March 2025 for the 2024-25 period by August 2025 (paragraph
        3.102). As noted above, DWI's enforcement powers are separate from Ofwat's.
        However, in the context of our investigation, these missed targets are further
        evidence of South East Water not acting promptly to address the issues with its
        inspection frequency.

3.174. The company's failure to inspect its service reservoirs at least every 10 years in
       accordance with their legal obligations, which would have identiﬁed areas for
        maintenance, during the Relevant Period has contributed to the degradation in




366
   PR24-final-determinations-Expenditure-allowances-V2.pdf, p.225 – .p226; Final determinations models
- Ofwat, "Water – Resilience: Enhancement Expenditure Model – Redacted, worksheet tab 'SEW_WTW4."

                                                 94
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited



           condition of its reservoirs and the resulting increase in the risks to water quality
           and loss of supply.


3.175. In its Representations, South East Water stated that, despite having several
           service reservoirs outside of the 10-year inspection programme, there is no
           evidence to suggest that these service reservoirs present a signiﬁcant increase
           in water quality risk (whereby water of satisfactory quality could not be provided
           to the customers and this would therefore have a knock on effect on supply). It
           is difﬁcult to see how South East Water can make this statement given that risks
           to drinking water quality were identiﬁed by South East Water itself. It recorded
           as a high risk on its CRR, from 2017 until 2022, that service reservoirs could fail
           water quality standards due to insufﬁcient inspection and maintenance
           opportunities. Further, the 2022 DWI notice referenced potential risks to human
           health (paragraph 3.101).

3.176. We acknowledge that there is no guarantee that regular inspections would have
       prevented supply interruptions from occurring, however, we do consider routine
           inspection, and resulting maintenance would have enabled South East Water to
           have put in place measures to increase resilience. For example, internal
           inspections identify sediment build up and action can be taken to prevent them
           acting in an unexpected manner (e.g. uncontrolled draining) during extreme
           weather events. Lack of inspection and maintenance creates the real risk that
           service reservoirs will become inoperable, out for extended periods and put
           pressure on the WRZ assets. While South East Water does use ROVs to complete
           some internal inspections, this is only on vessels that cannot be readily be taken
           out of supply.367 We consider that this technology could be used more widely and
           frequently.

3.177. In addition, infrequent inspections and inadequate preventive maintenance can
           increase the risk to the structural integrity of the service reservoirs. The risk of
           restriction in usable storage and the resulting supply risk are particularly
           pronounced given the known vulnerabilities in the Eastern region, where there
           are many single cell reservoirs which were recognised as a risk by South East
           Water (paragraph 3.103).

3.178. In its Area Plans, South East Water stated that removing certain service
           reservoirs from supply is difﬁcult and this is why some have not been inspected
           for over ten years (paragraph 3.103). We note that the difﬁculties around
           removing these assets from supply are not unique to South East Water and




367
      South East Water, Second s203 Notice, 31 July 2024, p.49.

                                                       95
          Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                   on South East Water Limited



             common across the industry whereby the majority of water companies have
             speciﬁc programmes and/or teams aimed at resolving these issues through, for
             example, the installation of bypasses, pressure sustaining/reducing valves,
             control valves or in some cases temporary bowsers/tanks to enable removal from
             service to varying degrees. As such we do not consider difﬁculty of removing
             reservoirs from supply as a sufﬁcient justiﬁcation for not inspecting service
             reservoirs at least once every 10-years. We consider that a service reservoir
             asset cannot be considered fully operable if it cannot be removed from supply,
             whether as part of a planned maintenance schedule or reactively, for example,
             following a water quality sample failure. 368 If service reservoirs cannot be
             drained, in exceptional circumstances (i.e. as a last resort), industry research
             has recognised already in 2017 that there are methods and technologies which
             allow limited maintenance and/or refurbishment work while a structure remains
             in service. 369 Moreover, we would expect water companies to have detailed
             plans, per cell, for removal from supply and also during operation. This would
             include:


         •     Site overview and access information (access codes, required keys, site
               alarms, contact information for the control room).
         •     Distribution valve schedules that enable removal from supply, often by
               bringing a bypass main into service.
         •     Logistical plans detailing any limitations when tankering to the reservoir in
               question during an emergency (access, ofﬂoading etc).
         •     Where access is restricted, for instance, due to SSSI sites, we expect
               companies to be proactive and to work to resolve these issues by seeking
               taking active steps to ﬁnd alternative solutions.

3.179. With all of this in mind, the difﬁculties that South East Water state it
       experiences regarding service reservoir inspection and maintenance are not
       unique, we would have expected South East Water, throughout the Relevant
             Period, to have identiﬁed solutions that allow for appropriate proactive
             maintenance, for example using tankers to drain reservoirs or other measures
             adopted by companies outlined above.

3.180. We are concerned that only 75% of South East Water’s service reservoirs in the
       Relevant WRZs zones have live telemetry. As such, we consider that South East
       Water lacks oversight of the storage levels at its service reservoirs. This further




368
      UKWIR, Treated Water Storage Assets: Good Practice for Operation and Management, Version 2, 2017.
369
      UKWIR, Treated Water Storage Assets: Good Practice for Operation and Management, Version 2, 2017.

                                                     96
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



      demonstrates that the operational data that South East Water hold is
      insufﬁcient to conﬁdently manage its water supply system. This not only raises
      concerns of how it can effectively respond to high or low alarm levels, but also
      how it is able to plan/respond to a potential high demand incident if it is not
      aware of low storage levels.

Storage levels at service reservoirs

3.181. As explained above, we have also analysed the storage levels to supplement our
       understanding/assessment of evidence relating to how South East Water
      maintains its service reservoirs. We found that in short- and medium-term
      periods, prior to certain Events in the Relevant Period, South East Water failed to
      increase storage levels at service reservoirs (see Appendix 9). For the 2022
      Storm Eunice Event and the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, the storage levels were
      lower than the 80-85% target in Relevant WRZs, which we consider concerning
      given the known history of water stress and a prior event occurring during a
      similar period (i.e. the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event). As such, we would have
      expected South East Water to maximise its storage levels and WTW outputs in
      the lead up to critical periods.


3.182. In the lead up to a peak demand period, particularly over the summer period, we
       would expect companies to make every effort to maximise the storage of its
       service reservoirs. This could be achieved through cancelling or expediting
       planned maintenance or increase outputs from WTWs. The analysis at Appendix
       9, regarding the 2022 High Demand Event, shows that stock levels in all of the
       Relevant WRZs were lower than the target. Having experienced the level of
       disruption South East Water’s networks did the previous summer, good practice
      for the company would have been to focus on what actions might be undertaken
      to achieve an improved supply interruption outcome for its customers in
      subsequent periods of hot weather in the lead up to the 2023 High Demand
      Event – such as maximising storage levels at service reservoirs as part of a
      preparedness plan; however, this did not occur as is evident in Figure 9.4 in
      Appendix 9.


3.183. This level of storage left South East Water's operational teams and control room
       under prepared, from a water resilience point of view, to meet the demand. As
       outlined at paragraph 2.10, 80-85% should be what companies aim to achieve as
      a target level for average percentage full for service reservoirs. By aiming to
      maximise storage levels to this target, there would have been an adequate
      buffer to deal with demand increases and supply shocks. South East Water
      stated that we applied the 80–85% storage target inconsistently, treating it as


                                           97
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



         both a year-round and event-speciﬁc benchmark, and for failing to account for
         the buffering role of storage and normal diurnal ﬂuctuations, and thereby
         undermining the validity of the Storage Levels Analysis. As set out at paragraph
         2.10, this storage target is widely accepted and documented by UKWIR. It is
         generally used as a benchmark throughout the year by most water companies
         and is particularly important when assessing readiness ahead of critical periods,
         such as high demand or severe weather. These aims are not mutually exclusive;
         we would expect companies to internally aim for the 80-85% target even
         accounting for diurnal patterns. The Storage Levels Analysis examines daily
         average of 15 minute interval data for each service reservoir to look at the
         broader context in which it operates to consider whether South East Water's
         average daily service reservoir storage meets the 80-85% storage target over a
         longer period than a single day. To consider diurnal patterns, we also conducted
         the identical Storage Levels Analysis used the static "4am Snapshot" data point
         provided by South East Water for each reservoir encompassing a single 4am
         datapoint, per day, per reservoir cell. We would expect storage levels at 4am to
         be at the highest point, as reservoirs have had a chance to reﬁll overnight.
         However, the difference in storage levels at 4am for the week prior to each Event
         only increased by between 2.1% and 6.4% compared with the daily average and
         none of the Relevant WRZs met the 80-85% target in the lead up to the Events.
         We would have expected a bigger difference and for service reservoirs to be able
         to meet the 80-85% target overnight (i.e. at 4am).


3.184. South East Water considers that the Storage Levels Analysis fails to account for
       planned maintenance, winter operating practices, linked system operation,
       operational characteristics of and capacity of certain reservoirs, and constraints
       from third-party bulk water supplies, leading to a misleading interpretation of
         storage levels as poor preparedness across the 2022 and 2023 events. We do not
         agree with the arguments outlined at paragraph 3.119, for the reasons set out
         below:
     •    Regarding the linked system operation and the effect planned maintenance at
          Barcombe WTW had on the linked system during the 2022 Storm Eunice Event,
          although the Barcombe WTW outage affected downstream storage, reservoir
          levels still regularly fell below 50% in late January and early February (before
          Storm Eunice hit). To re-iterate our point at paragraphs 3.79 and 3.80,
          carrying out planned winter maintenance does not align with Ofwat and DWI
          guidance on ensuring year-round resilience, particularly given lessons from
          the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, Barcombe WTW’s [CONFIDENTIAL] and its
          limited bankside storage capacity. Similarly, it does not align with the 4Rs
          industry approach to resilience (paragraph 2.54).


                                            98
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



      •   Regarding the operational characteristics of certain service reservoirs that run
          downstream, in the 62 days before the 2022 High Demand Event, several
          reservoirs downstream of Cranbrook service reservoir experienced storage
          levels below 50–60% for sustained periods. While we recognise that turnover
          and operational management processes are in place for this section of
          network, and that it intentionally operates at possibly lower levels to support
          downstream service reservoirs, the data shows repeated low storage levels in
          June 2022, with multiple downstream cells below 50% at the same time as
          Cranbrook fell below 20%. This is signiﬁcantly lower than the 80-85% target.
          We would expect a reasonable company to change its usual operating practice
          in advance of hot weather and raise service reservoir levels, particularly in
          light of the maintenance activity referred to at paragraph 3.119. This is not the
          level of preparedness that we would expect from a reasonable company
          particularly in the face of known vulnerabilities identiﬁed in the Eastern
          region. While South East Water points to factors such as a burst trunk main
          and borehole failure to be "outside of its control", we consider maintaining
          asset health and maintenance to be the responsibility of a reasonable
          company, as is a timely response when trunk mains and boreholes fail.
      •   Regarding constraints from third party bulk supplies, while we acknowledge
          that South East Water receives more bulk supplies than any other water
          company, the management (operationally and contractually) of these bulk
          supplies is part of South East Water's duty to maintain supply for its
          customers.
      •   Regarding whether Ofwat had considered South East Water's winter operating
          practices where storage is typically held lower to maintain water quality, we
          examined 4am data to take into account such practices and consider that
          service reservoirs should be able to meet the 80-85% target overnight – they
          did not (paragraph 3.183). Even if a company accepts this as a risk during
          normal operating conditions for "water quality purposes", then it should start
          to increase storage levels in the 1-2 days before a forecasted event. As
          outlined at paragraph 2.81, we expect a reasonable company to have a 'hands
          off' period for maintenance during critical or high periods (i.e. summer and
          winter, or during extreme weather).
      •   Regarding whether Ofwat considered the capacity of each service reservoir,
          we have undertaken further analysis to consider the weighted volume. 370 We
          conducted identical Storage Levels Analysis assigning the total capacity (m3)
          per cell to reﬂect the percentage contribution each cell made to the total




370
    Provided to South East Water in the spreadsheet 'res15_period_averages_UPDATED – Volume
Weighted'.

                                                 99
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



        storage capacity per WRZ, both for visible assets (with operational telemetry)
        and for all cells. The impact on the overall analysis described above was small.

3.185. Taking into account our calculated maximum aggregated storage capacity,
       South East Water has a possible maximum resilience time of 32 hours (worst
      case scenario); while this number is based upon all the service reservoirs being
      full, this demonstrates that South East Water's total possible aggregated service
      reservoir storage capacity of 948Ml is a healthy amount of potable storage given
      the maximum demand it is likely to experience in extreme circumstances is
      708Ml/d. As such, we consider that if planned maintenance schedules had been
      cancelled or expedited during peak periods, WTWs had outputs maximised and
      borehole yield had been maximised, at a WRZ level, South East Water would have
      had adequate storage levels to meet demand and provide a buffer to overcome
      supply shocks during the Relevant Period. While we acknowledge that sub-zonal
      factors and interconnectivity have a bearing on effective network management,
      this challenge is a universal one and should be part of a company's planning,
      investment and problem solving following root cause analysis. We examine
      South East Water's approach to these issues in Section 3.3.2.


3.186. South East Water considers that Ofwat's focus on storage levels, as a measure of
       resilience is misplaced, as its Western region shows interruptions are driven by
       limited headroom and interconnectivity, not storage. We disagree with this, as
       multiple factors inﬂuence performance, with South East Water attributing
       differences between Eastern and Western regions to borehole yields, bulk
       transfers, and trunk main reliability. While we accept that interruptions are
       affected by headroom and interconnectivity, these are known vulnerabilities
      that the company should have been taking steps to mitigate and address
      including as part of incident preparedness (Section 3.3.3). In any event,
      headroom and interconnectivity are not the only drivers. We consider that asset
      availability (including increasing and prioritising borehole maintenance and
      yield as well as service reservoir storage) are also important in providing
      resilience for customers. This is evident in Appendix 9, which shows similar, if
      not worse, maintenance of storage levels in the Western region (WRZ4 and
      WRZ5), yet it does not experience a similar level of supply interruption as the
      Eastern region. This suggests that South East Water should have been focussed
      on asset maintenance within its Eastern region due to its known vulnerabilities
      and should have taken active steps in light of these characteristics.

Trunk mains
3.187. As outlined above South East Water has been aware of the risk that failure of
       trunk mains poses to its supply since 2017. Throughout the Relevant Period at an

                                          100
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited



           Executive level, South East Water has identiﬁed this as a speciﬁc risk to its
           Eastern region, and the number of supply interruption minutes the company has
           accrued. The need to maintain trunk mains is particularly important in the
           Eastern region given the known vulnerabilities (paragraph 3.29) regarding the
           extent to which properties on South East Water's network rely on single sources
           of supply. This means that proactive maintenance is vital in South East Water's
           Eastern region.

3.188. As discussed in our summary of evidence, South East Water's mains are
       increasingly ageing and therefore more susceptible to bursts. South East
           Water's performance improved against the burst mains PCL over the PR19
           (2020-25) period, however we note that this refers to mains generally, not
           speciﬁcally to trunk mains. 371 South East Water is aware that it has an
           increasingly ageing asset population (paragraph 3.132), yet it continues to
           implement a reactive approach to repair and maintenance that is dependent on
           several burst occurring along the same section of main before raising a main for
           replacement. A reasonable company, particularly in light of the known
           vulnerabilities identiﬁed by South East Water, would have an effective
           preventative maintenance plan and specialist trunk mains teams. 372 We consider
           that South East Water should be doing more with respect to proactive
           maintenance of its trunk mains acting in an economical and efﬁcient way. Its
           steps to introduce dedicated proactive maintenance crews (for 2024-30) are
           positive and should be brought forward. We also consider that adequate
           resourcing and planning should have been put in place by South East Water
           prior to PR24, at a minimum as part of its PR19 base expenditure, to ensure it
           had sufﬁcient resources to meet both reactive and preventative maintenance of
           trunk mains.

3.189. Proactive asset management looks to learn lessons from previous bursts and
       model risk to identify future problem areas which could then be prioritised for
           replacement. South East Water's "asset needs request" approach to its mains
           replacement, whereby it relies on several bursts occurring along the same
           section of main to indicate deterioration highlights that its approach to
           monitoring and therefore its understanding of asset condition is poor. Further, it
           introduces more risk of jeopardising customer supply as it relies on repeated
           failure of aged and deteriorating assets meaning customers are more likely to
           experience interruptions to supply or reduced pressure. Deploying proactive




371
      2020-2025 Water Company Performance Report, p.28: PowerPoint Presentation
372
      UKWIR, Trunk Mains Preventative Maintenance to Reduce Major Incident Risk, 2020/21.

                                                    101
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited



           maintenance would ensure that assets do not deteriorate such that they need to
           be replaced.


3.190. In its Representations, South East Water says that a more efﬁcient and practical
           means of addressing trunk main failure risk would be to install interconnector
           mains. 373 It claims it has not been "fully funded", we note that South East Water's
           section 37 WIA91 applies irrespective of its funding situation as discussed
           further at Section 3.3.2. Ofwat considers that South East Water has been
           previously funded for much of what was claimed in PR24 to address
           interconnectivity. Additionally, as outlined in paragraph 3.86, the CMA
           Provisional Determination has only allowed South East Water an additional
           £0.1 million in relation to its interconnector mains as opposed to the
           £38.4 million that South East Water requested. If the CMA conﬁrms this in the
           ﬁnal determination, then proactive maintenance will continue to be important
           for South East Water's trunk mains in the future. In any event, we have found
           that proactive maintenance activities could have assisted and improved South
           East Water's trunk mains maintenance throughout the Relevant Period and at
           least since June 2020, when South East Water expressed the desire to reduce its
           vulnerability to reactive maintenance and prioritise planned maintenance on all
           assets.

3.191. In contrast, South East Water's approach to maintaining strategic valves is
       considered by Ofwat to be an appropriate service level agreement (paragraph
       3.136) and in line with the rest of the industry. However, we are concerned about
       how this is managed in practice – South East Water raises large batches of
       workorders on a single date and closes these workorders in batches. We
       therefore do not consider South East Water has sufﬁcient real-time information
       about the result and status of its maintenance activities.

Boreholes
3.192. As discussed in the evidence above, South East Water's borehole maintenance
           programme covers about 12 sites a year, which South East Water cites is
           sufﬁcient to keep pace with current requirements. Its inspection programme,
           has on average, resulted in operational boreholes being inspected between
           every eight to 21 years). We consider that that its maintenance programme and
           frequency of inspections is not sufﬁcient or adequate. By not inspecting and
           maintaining boreholes, the company increases the risk of borehole outage
           increases and causes asset life to decrease; this in turn affects supply




373
      South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 6.86.

                                                    102
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



         resilience, especially in the Eastern region, where supply resilience is already at
         risk due to its known vulnerabilities.

3.193. The example of [CONFIDENTIAL] borehole 2 discussed above (paragraph
         3.145) shows that South East Water undertook certain schemes to optimise its
         borehole yield, but this work was only undertaken reactively in response to the
         issues experienced from the 2023 High Demand Event, despite being aware that
         this borehole needed repair since October 2022. South East Water should have
         proactively conducted maintenance and optimised its borehole yield to mitigate
         risk rather than waiting until the risk was realised when an incident occurred.


3.194. We consider that lack of proactive maintenance (including through
         replacement) can lead to and, in the case of South East Water's Eastern region,
         has at least contributed to boreholes becoming non-operational. This was the
         case for example for Saints Hill borehole 1, which had suffered long term
         turbidity issues and was identiﬁed as a candidate for replacement following a
         survey but which was classiﬁed as a non-operational borehole in August 2024, or
         Coggins Mill borehole 4, which had experienced a reduction in borehole yield
         and subsequently became non-operational as of August 2024.


3.195. Non-operational boreholes and boreholes with persistent issues increase the
       risks to WTW performance, in periods of high demand or limited output, e.g.
         during extreme weather, and/or when coupled with other assets
         underperforming or being out of service at the same time. This happened for
         South East Water's Eastern region in the case of the following boreholes:
     •     As discussed at paragraph 3.151, South East Water had known that Saints Hill
           borehole 1, which is now categorised as non-operational, had been a borehole
           of concern since at least 2019 due to its lack of yield. However, during the 2022
           Freeze Thaw Event, borehole 5 was out of service for maintenance despite the
           issues with borehole 1 remaining unaddressed. This meant that two boreholes
           were not available, reducing yield which South East Water acknowledged
           reduced the deployable output ﬂow at Saints Hill WTW during this Event. If, as
           part of a planned proactive replacement programme, South East Water had
           replaced borehole 1, the output ﬂow at Saints Hill WTW would not have been
           reduced or would have been reduced to a lesser degree.
     •     Similarly, Coggins Mill borehole 4 and Bewl borehole 3 had known yield issues
           since 2019. South East Water identiﬁed that Coggins Mill borehole 4 would not
           be able to be reinstated due to water chemistry and that Bewl borehole 3 had
           been out of service for years. Both Coggins Mill WTW and Bewl WTW had limited
           site output as there was a lack of raw water availability during the 2023 High
           Demand Event due to the boreholes being out of rehabilitation. These


                                             103
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



          boreholes had not yet been classed as non-operational during this incident
          and, as such, we consider that if the yield of these boreholes had been
          increased by maintaining as soon as issues were identiﬁed in 2019, the WTWs
          at Coggins Mill and Bewl would have had more raw water availability,
          increasing both sites output and preventing a decrease in supply.

3.196. While we acknowledge South East Water's comment that Bewl WTW obtains the
        majority of its raw water from Bewl reservoir, and receives a transfer of up to
        [CONFIDENTIAL] (although the average is [CONFIDENTIAL]) from Southern
        Water,374 we do not consider that this negates the need for an additional
        borehole/replacement of borehole 3 at Bewl WTW (paragraph 3.154). This is
        because site output was limited at Bewl WTW during the 2023 High Demand
        Event due to borehole 3 being out for rehabilitation, affecting raw water
        availability. If anything, this demonstrates the importance of boreholes in terms
        of supply resilience in the Eastern region and that South East Water should be
        optimising as much yield as possible, and building redundancy (one of the "4Rs"
        discussed at paragraph 2.54) into its system to the extent that it is economical
        and efﬁcient to do so, especially as borehole 3 at Bewl WTW has been a cause of
        concern since 2019. We note that, as part of the Further Updated Action Plan,
        South East Water is currently increasing the output at Bewl WTW during AMP8,
        although this is subject to the CMA Redetermination. 375


3.197. While do not suggest that all South East Water's non-operational boreholes need
       to be replaced to ensure maximum deployable output. Rather, at a minimum the
       company should be replacing those boreholes in the Eastern region with long-
       term issues and/or that were identiﬁed as contributing to supply issues during
       extreme weather to ensure multiple points of abstraction are available at each
        WTW at any given time, including where it needs to take boreholes out of service
        for maintenance. Having multiple points of abstraction from the same source is
        industry practice, without this ﬂexibility/backup, the resilience of boreholes is at
        risk, affecting the company's preparedness for extreme weather events.


3.198. In any event, while South East Water acknowledged that a borehole replacement
       programme was necessary, no boreholes were scheduled for replacement in
        2015–20 and 2025–30. South East Water told us this was due to budget
        concerns, explaining that it does use its base expenditure to fund borehole




374
   South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 6.70-6.71.
375
   As part of the CMA Provisional Redetermination, the CMA has made no further allowances for works to

                                                  104

         works, but the budget for this is small and would take away funding from other
         areas of the network. While we understand that South East Water considers
         boreholes to not be the best use of its base expenditure, our ﬁndings above
         demonstrate the importance of having additional supply resilience to a region
         which has known vulnerabilities (paragraphs 3.29 and 3.31).

Summary of ﬁndings and conclusions on section 37 WIA91
3.199. We ﬁnd that South East Water has contravened its section 37 WIA91 duty, as it
         has not been maintaining an economic and efﬁcient system of supply within its
         Eastern region. By failing to adequately maintain its assets, it has failed to
         ensure that it has arrangements in place in its Eastern region, for providing
         supplies of water to meet demand and maintaining and improving its mains,
         such that it is able to continue to meet its domestic supply and [water quality]
         obligations. More speciﬁcally, South East Water has:
     •     Failed to ensure its service reservoirs were adequately and regularly
           inspected (at least every 10 years) and cleaned/maintained. This has led to
           degradation in condition of its reservoirs and an increase in the risks to water
           quality and loss of supply.
     •     Failed to maintain storage levels at service reservoirs during periods of high
           demand. We found that key service reservoirs entered critical periods with low
           storage levels.
     •     Failed to adequately maintain its trunk mains by undertaking sufﬁcient
           preventative maintenance and over-relying on reactive maintenance. Trunk
           mains have been identiﬁed as a factor that has at least contributed to supply
           interruptions in the Eastern region.
     •     Failed to undertake the necessary inspection and maintenance of boreholes,
           which led to boreholes becoming non-operational and increased the risk of
           outages at boreholes, contributing to supply interruptions. On several
           occasions, boreholes were out of service or underperforming during high
           demand periods while other assets were taken out of service, leading to
           reduced yield. We also found that South East Water has failed to optimise the
           yield of its non-operational boreholes to provide additional supply resilience
           and delayed re-prioritising its borehole maintenance programme.
     •     Failed to plan for maintenance of key assets outside of critical or high
           demand periods. We found a lack of holistic risk assessment to ensure
           sufﬁcient headroom and anticipate the impact of outages on demand to allow
           for planned preventive maintenance without the need to shut down
           operations. We would expect that a reasonable company would adequately
           assess the impact that the outage would have on demand, consider any
           existing assets that may be out of service, and maintain sufﬁcient operational
           resources to avoid or reduce the severity of loss of supply events.


                                             105
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



3.200. These failings indicate that during the Relevant Period South East Water's
       supply system was not maintained both efﬁciently and economically, as it was
         not ﬁt for purpose and South East Water did not secure its ability to meet its Part
         III obligations and, in fact, it was not able to meet, or at least at a signiﬁcant risk
         of not meeting, its domestic supply and water quality obligations in the past as
         evidenced by persistent supply interruptions and enforcement action taken by
         the DWI with respect to water quality.


3.201. We ﬁnd that each of these failings amounts to a contravention of section 37
       WIA91 in and of itself. In addition, we have considered these failings together,
       and they reveal consistent themes and wider issues in the way South East Water
         manages its supply system. South East Water's WTWs rely on multiple assets
         working to abstract, treat, store and distribute water to its customers. As
         discussed above, the fact that the company is failing to properly maintain its
         asset base and plan its maintenance activities, meant that during extreme
         weather multiple assets were out of service or not performing sufﬁciently, which
         contributed to supply interruptions in the particularly vulnerable Eastern region.


3.202. We consider these contraventions to be ongoing, as South East Water has not
       taken sufﬁcient steps to bring itself back into compliance with section 37 WIA91
       and there is a continuing, and real, risk that it will not be able to meet its
       domestic supply and water quality obligations.


Summary of ﬁndings and conclusions on Licence Condition P12
3.203. As set out above, under Licence Condition P12, water undertakers are required
         to act in the manner best calculated to ensure they have in place adequate
         systems of planning and internal control (as well as ﬁnancial and management
         resources) to enable it to carry out their functions as undertakers. This includes
         meeting the duties imposed on the undertakers.


3.204. The evidence we have summarised above, and our ﬁndings on section 37 WIA91,
         show a lack of adequate systems of planning around maintenance activities and
         a lack of systems of internal control to monitor and appropriate resources
         ensure compliance with section 37 WIA91. As such, we found that, in
         contravention of Licence Condition P12, South East Water:


     •     Failed to ensure it had adequate ﬁnancial and management resources and
           systems of planning and internal control to:
            a. plan to maintain key assets through regular and frequent maintenance
                and inspection programmes. Speciﬁcally, its failures to:

                                              106
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



                  i. proactively optimise its borehole yield through maintenance and
                     replacement. This is evident in South East Water's conscious
                     decision not to invest in these programmes over a ten year period
                     even though it acknowledges that this would improve resilience.
                     While we understand South East Water's budget concerns relating
                     to its borehole maintenance programme, our ﬁndings have shown
                     the importance of boreholes in providing additional supply
                     resilience for its Eastern region's known vulnerabilities;
                 ii. regularly inspect service reservoirs, even though South East Water's
                     Board was aware of the issues and risks with these assets it failed to
                     ensure that appropriate systems to ensure maintenance of service
                     reservoirs were in place and to have a clear remediation plan for
                     assets that were in a deteriorating condition; and
                iii. have a structured planned preventative maintenance programme
                     for trunk mains and strategic valves, which enable it to accurately
                     track the progress of maintenance activities.
            b. adequately plan maintenance of key assets outside of peak periods
               and/or adequately assess the impact that the outages would have on
               demand by considering any existing assets that may be out of service,
               and maintain sufficient operational resources to avoid or reduce the
               severity of supply interruption incidents; and
            c. ensure storage levels were at its highest possible capacity leading into
               critical or high demand periods, such as summer and winter, taking
               account of minimum storage levels required to effectively operate service
               reservoirs and booster pumping stations.

3.205. We found that South East Water's Executive Team and Board failed to:
     •    ensure sufﬁcient reporting metrics to give them a clear picture of whether
          South East Water was properly complying with its general duty under section
          37 WIA91; and
     •    appropriately monitor the company's supply system resilience risks, properly
          scrutinising its compliance with its legal and regulatory obligations and
          ensuring that appropriate action was taken in response to those risks.


3.206. We consider a company's internally set targets and policies to be relevant
         evidence when considering its systems of planning under Licence Condition P12
         and we expect a company to have in place systems of internal control that
         effectively follow-up on the internal actions and principles identiﬁed in
         documents such as South East Water's Area Plans, in addition to complying with
         relevant DWI notices, which we do not consider was done with respect to service


                                            107
          Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                   on South East Water Limited



           reservoirs. We note that South East Water considers the criteria within its Area
           Plans as "long-term ideal conditions". 376 Nevertheless, the Area Plans show
           knowledge of issues since at least 2019 and we ﬁnd that it failed to take
           sufﬁcient action within the Relevant Period to address the known risk of failing
           to adequately maintain key assets by adopting a proactive, planned preventative
           maintenance programme and following through with respect to the mitigating
           actions it identiﬁed. A reasonable company would be taking proactive steps to
           maintain its asset base with a clear remediation plan consistent with its general
           duty under section 37 WIA91. The evidence highlights its inability to proactively
           monitor asset availability and a lack of company knowledge of its network.

3.207. At a minimum, South East Water should have proactively monitored key assets
           (such as service reservoirs, trunk mains and boreholes), including having a
           rolling programme of preventative maintenance and checks to ensure
           completion in accordance with scheduled frequency and company policy. For
           example, greater visibility over strategic valve workorders rather than the
           majority being open and closed on a single day. We would expect it to have in
           place a rolling, dynamic schedule of maintenance activities, with optimised
           frequency of visits which includes borehole maintenance and replacement
           programmes, regular inspection and maintenance of service reservoirs, and
           proactive trunk mains maintenance and renewal.

           optioneering process to inform its investment decisions and to address the

3.209. We also examine its claims relating to climate change and Covid-19 and whether
           the resulting impact on its supply resilience was foreseeable by a reasonable
           company in South East Water's position. Lastly, we look at its investment choices
           and consider its claims that Ofwat has not "fully funded" schemes to improve its
           supply resilience.

WRMP and Headroom
3.210. Each year since 2022, South East Water has been sent joint regulator's WRMP
       annual review letter expressing concerns about the company's performance on
       supply demand balance, DO and DI.377


3.211. In this subsection, we explore South East Water's chosen planning approach for
       its WRMP DYCP demand, its justiﬁcation for using this approach and whether,
       using this approach, meant that its WAFU forecasts during the Relevant Period
       were reasonable. We also look at whether, despite its WRMP, there was an
       identiﬁed issue with headroom in its Eastern region.


3.212. South East Water uses Average Day Peak Week (ADPW) in planning for DYCP in
       its WRMP as it allows for service storage to balance daily fluctuations in DI. 378
       South East Water stated this is a more useful measure where there are
       limitations in peak supply outputs relative to demand due to the nature of water
       sources, abstraction licences, or treatment capacities. 379 This approach of using
       ADPW in planning for DYCP is contemplated by the Water Resources Planning
       Guidance (WRPG) during the Relevant Period, which references a 2006 UKWIR
       report which found eight companies surveyed used ADPW as their methodology
       for calculating DYCP. 380


3.213. Appendix 7 reflects South East Water's ADPW approach to WRMP planning: DYCP
       planning scenario is plotted, which the company used the ADPW approach to
       define. As an outturn demand comparison, distribution input is plotted but as an
       average weekly demand as a close comparator to ADPW, smoothing out short
       term daily fluctuations. Appendix 7 demonstrates that South East Water's Final
       Plan WAFU targets/forecasts, in terms of planning, were reasonable given the
       information available at the time.

3.214. However, this does not address the emerging risk of insufficient headroom
       within its planning and operational processes and the effect this may have on
       supply and demand during the Relevant Period. "Insufficient headroom" has
       been identified as a challenge by South East Water as early as 2018, when it was

Leakage
3.217. In this subsection, we explore South East Water’s leakage performance during
       the Relevant Period, drawing on its APR and Ofwat’s performance reports, and
          compare this performance against the leakage forecasts set out in its WRMP
          Final Plan for all Relevant WRZs.


3.218. Since 2022, South East Water has performed poorly with respect to leakage. It
       has identiﬁed several actions to manage and reduce leakage as part of its
          Service Commitment Plans that it provided to Ofwat in 2023 and 2024. South
          East Water has also been sent joint regulator's WRMP annual review letter that
          references poor performance in leakage each year since 2022. 390 However,
          annual leakage levels have continued to increase:
      •    South East Water was categorised in Ofwat's 2022–23 Water Company
           Performance Report as "lagging behind" and was reported to have seen a large
           deterioration in its three-year average leakage performance against its
           PCLs.391 Although, South East Water noted that this was the ﬁrst time in
           20 years that it had not met its leakage PCL.392
      •    The 2023-24 Water Company Performance Report found that South East
           Water's three-year average leakage levels were higher than the baseline set in
           2019-20. 393
      •    South East Water's APR shows that, for the period 2024-25, it reported an
           annual average leakage level of 10.89% above its 2019-20 baseline of 94.5Ml/d.
           This is the highest percentage increase in annual leakage for any company in
           the 2020-25 period, making South East Water the worst performer on
           leakage based on this metric.

3.219. Appendix 7 includes an analysis of South East Water's performance against its
       WRMP Final Plan leakage forecasts. It plots leakage and highlights where South
       East Water's leakage has exceeded the WRMP forecast targets. It shows that all
          Relevant WRZs (apart from WRZ3) experienced excess leakage. From 2022
          onwards, excess leakage increases for WRZ1 and WRZ2; this can also be seen

PWPC
3.220. In this subsection, we explore South East Water’s use and calculation of PWPC,
       drawing on its performance during the 2019-24 period in regards to DI and
          unplanned outage. We have examined whether South East Water has been using
          PWPC as a planning tool (as described at paragraphs 2.23-2.24) and whether it is
          calculating its PWPC correctly, which should be calculated using the highest
          historic performance for any seven-day period in the last ﬁve years. To do this,
          we plotted and analysed PWPC against DI and unplanned outage (as shown on
          the graphs included in Appendix 8). PWPC is only plotted on each graph from
          2019, when it was ﬁrst introduced as a measure by Ofwat. Appendix 8 shows
          that:
      •     Apart from Figure 8.1 (WRZ1), South East Water's PWPC (i.e. the capability of
            its assets) has remained stable throughout all Relevant WRZs, despite the
            peak demand challenge the company faces.
      •     South East Water has not made changes to its PWPC to reﬂect highest
            historical performance, as seen in Figure 8.4 for WRZ6 and Figure 8.5 for
            WRZ7. 394

3.221. As part of its Third Response, South East Water provided several spreadsheets
       which highlighted the PWPC for each of its water sources in all its WRZs. These
       spreadsheets indicate that South East Water is multiplying each WTW Ml/d by
       seven rather than ensuring that the PWPC is set at a level equal to or exceeding
       the maximum seven-day sustained output achieved in the preceding ﬁve
       year.395


3.222. In its Representations, South East Water considered that PWPC should be used
          as an asset health measure, 396 rather than a planning tool for calculating
          headroom.397 It stated that seasonal changes in yield and allowed abstraction
          volumes (which do not form part of PWPC) are relevant factors for production

          planning used by South East Water. 398 In response to further query about how it
          calculates PWPC, South East Water stated the "originally" there was a shadow
          reporting period where it used "the WRMP normal year PDO [peak deployable
          output]" and that "is why a formula [where the PWPC value is calculated by
          multiplying the PDO by seven] is included in the spreadsheets provided". 399 For

          does not need to extend to seven days" to support its assertion that its PWPC

          system constraints allow it, or an alternative as contemplated in Ofwat's
          guidance". 400

3.223. South East Water said that setting PWPC for certain WRZs can be difﬁcult
       because: 401
      •     WRZ3 is subject to network constraints, which means the outputs of certain
            sites cannot necessarily be fully utilised. Certain WTWs only supply a local
            service reservoir and local community. As such, the outputs of these sites can
            only be used if there is demand on the system. Without adequate demand,
            storage ﬁlls, and there is no capacity to use additional water.
      •     WRZ6 receives bulk supply from Southern Water at Burham, Matts Hill and
            Bottom Pond. The reporting PWPC is below the minimum daily DI because of
            the volume of bulk supplies received into the WRZ.
      •     WRZ7 has two WTWs, Goudhurst WTW (which is a groundwater source where
            output is impacted by groundwater levels) and Bewl WTW (which is subject to
            a bulk supply agreement with Southern Water). While [CONFIDENTIAL] is
            the tested peak site output at Bewl WTW (which is used for calculating PWPC),
            the site’s peak deployable output pursuant to its abstraction licence and bulk
            supply agreement with Southern Water is lower. As such, it cannot plan to use
            output at the higher level.

          management including leakage reduction and pressure management plans. In

3.225. South East Water has a team of nine modellers. 402 It stated that it consults its

        identify which customers have been affected to help it ascertain which of its
        customers may be entitled to a GSS payment. 403 South East Water further stated

        from demand-management activities (such as water efﬁciency programmes or
        leakage reduction). 405 As it forecasted that the overall demand would stay
        roughly the same, it concluded that updating its hydraulic models more
        frequently would not have a signiﬁcant impact. 406

       comprehensive update and recalibration. 407 During AMP7, South East Water

       would provide the data that is needed for the recalibration of its hydraulic
       models, including any future ongoing updates of the models.408

3.229. In July 2023, South East Water identiﬁed, at its Board meeting, that its hydraulic
        models needed to be rebuilt and maintained as they had not been fully updated
        since 2011. 412 As set out at paragraph 2.29, the standard approach at most UK
        water companies is to have a rolling programme of hydraulic model updates
        aimed at ensuring all models are reviewed once every ﬁve years, while a
        recalibration/rebuild is not always required, the ﬁve-year review will determine
        this. In South East Water's case this would mean that, as its models had not
        been fully updated since 2011, we would expect that these models would have
        been updated by 2016; however, as of May 2024, the average age of South East
        Water's models was 10.7 years. 413 The July 2023 Board meeting also discussed
        investing more in its hydraulic models to ensure that they were brought up to a
        good standard.414 This suggests that South East Water itself recognised that it
        was not reviewing and updating its models with sufﬁcient frequency. As of May
        2024, the most recent update to hydraulic models in the Relevant WRZs took
        place in 2017, with the majority last updated in 2010 or 2011. 415


3.230. As discussed at paragraph 3.226, South East Water said its smart networks and

       the recalibration and future ongoing updates of hydraulic models. As part of its
       PR24 submission, South East Water requested funding to carry out this work on
       all its mains modelling stock. 416 Further, as part of AMP8, it explained that it will
        recalibrate its hydraulic models over the 2025-30 period, with prioritisation for
        areas with higher supply resilience risks that were affected by high-demand
        events and/or in connection with signiﬁcant changes to the network
        conﬁguration. 417 South East Water stated that as the roll out of these two

        the outputs from the hydraulic models are used for planning, deﬁning

        information, tools and processes. 422 South East Water also noted that hydraulic
        models play a greater role in validating resilience investment options rather
        than carrying out root causes analyses.423 We examine South East Water's

        thorough root cause analysis. In this subsection we summarise the evidence
        showing whether South East Water has carried out adequate root cause analysis
        to justify its proposed actions in response to the Events in the Relevant Period
        and within its investment plans.


3.234. In response to the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, the DWI recommended that all
        companies should establish the root cause for the loss of supplies.424

3.235. In January 2022, South East Water’s Executive Team was informed of the need
       for more thorough root cause analysis of its asset failures and investment in
        network solutions to improve resilience and asset stability. 425 Since then, South
        East Water explained that, as part of its lessons learned, it has undertaken a
        comprehensive analysis of the root causes of the supply resilience issues for
        Events within the Relevant Period. 426 Our preliminary assessment of this
        evidence raised concerns that South East Water's lessons learnt focussed only
        on its customer and incident responses (i.e. post-event) rather than addressing
        the root cause of its supply system resilience issues to reduce the risk of an
        event happening again.


3.236. During the minded to process, South East Water asked us to provide clariﬁcation
       as to the deﬁnition of root cause analysis. We explained that root cause analysis
        is a process undertaken to identify the underlying or systemic, rather than the
        generalised or immediate, causes of an incident. 427 It is about demonstrating the
        causal issue of a problem so that it is possible to identify the best solution. 428 It
        is a vital process as correcting only an immediate cause may eliminate a
        symptom of a problem, but not the problem itself. 429 We also cited UKWIR's
        project 'Identifying the Root Cause of Interruptions to Supply' as setting out the
        industry practice for root cause analysis, including root cause analysis activities
        following supply interruptions (see Section 2.1.11). 430

3.243. South East Water considers that the severity and frequency of the impacts
       associated with the weather events experienced in AMP7 were not seen prior to
        2018 and not foreseeable in any modelling work for PR19.441 Yet, in its WRMP14 it
        stated it operated in an area of "serious water stress",442 noted that weather
        patterns are becoming less predictable and referenced previous incidents
        where weather had adverse impacts on its network. This included a 17-month
        period between October 2010 and February 2012 where the region saw
        exceptionally low rainfall meaning that by April 2012, groundwater levels were
        the lowest since records began in 1904. 443


3.244. Since 2016, South East Water participated in the Water UK's National Water
       Resources Long Term Planning Framework investigating resilience which found
       in the South East of England "a further risk from climate change impacts, which
       unless properly addressed through improved resilience, could lead to more
       frequent demand restrictions." 444 In 2018, South East Water identiﬁed risks
       associated with climate change on its CRR including not being able to meet
       "periods of high usage such as heatwaves." 445


3.245. Following the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, Ofwat published a report on the
       companies' response during this event. 446 We observed, among other things,
        that performance was not directly correlated with the severity of the weather.
        Instead, outcomes were inﬂuenced by factors within companies’ control, such
        as the quality (or absence) of major-incident plans. We also found poor
        coordination between companies and a failure to apply lessons from earlier
        events, including a previous freeze thaw incident in 2010-11. Our key ﬁndings
        included that companies underestimated the impact of extreme weather, relied

3.249. The 2022 Freeze Thaw Event was similar to the disruptions resulting from the

        was still room for improvement.455 Our ﬁndings again emphasised that extreme
        weather events "will be increasingly the norm in future", meaning companies
        need to better prepare. On a dedicated webpage, we noted that "such variable
        weather conditions will be increasingly the norm in future and have the
        potential to affect supply networks and bring widespread disruptions for
        customers." We also highlighted that the companies need to better understand
        and anticipate the risks of future climate change, including by ensuring that
        assets and infrastructure are in good health so that networks and services are
        resilient to future challenges (e.g. service reservoirs need to be maintained at
        appropriate levels in readiness for events) and alternative water supplies should
        be made readily available. South East Water and Southern Water were
        highlighted as particularly poor performers.

3.250. In addition to identifying risks associated with climate change on its CRR in 2018

        "principal risk" in its Annual Report 2021-22. 456 South East Water has also made
        comment on the fact that climate change has increased acute weather events
        such as ﬂoods. Flood waters can contaminate surface water or groundwater,
        affecting the raw water quality that is available.457 Flood waters can also
        inundate a treatment facility and wash out open tanks, damage mechanical
        equipment, render electrical power and controls useless, spoil ﬁnished water
        storage tanks and wash contaminants directly into the treatment process. 458
