Covid-19
3.255. South East Water stated that, since Covid-19, demand has signiﬁcantly
       increased due to the imposition of lockdowns (affecting how and where people
        used water, i.e. the PCC) and permanent changes of working patterns. 466


3.256. South East Water stated that pre-Covid-19, 68% of households were working
       outside of home, and at the time of the 2020 High Demand Event, 54% of
       households were working outside of home.467 It suggested this demand is a "new
       norm" that was not previously forecast in its WRMP. 468 To test this claim, at
        Appendix 7, we compare South East Water's outturn DI with the forecasted
        WAFU in its DYCP Baseline and Final Plans for WRMP19 (2020–45) and WRMP24
        (2025-50) for its Eastern region. This is to understand whether South East
        Water's demand during the Relevant Period was unexpectedly high compared to
        its WRMP forecasts and its pre-Covid-19 levels. As explained above, we have
        converted outturn DI into average week values to better reﬂect the ADPW
        scenario that South East Water utilises in its planning to avoid skews of short
        term daily high demand peaks. 469 We set out our conclusions based on this
        analysis in our ﬁndings section below.

Investment
3.257. This subsection outlines evidence relating to South East Water’s investment and
       price review funding for maintaining and enhancing its water supply system
       across successive price review periods.


3.258. As outlined in Section 2.2, Ofwat's price review establishes the funding
       companies are allowed to spend on operating and improving their networks
        (referred to as the cumulative wholesale totex allowance) over a ﬁve-year
        period. In their submissions for each price review, companies must set out the
        investment needed to meet their statutory duties, including section 37 WIA91,

          maintain their assets, and deliver the required levels of service. This includes
          providing detailed ﬁnancial modelling and evidence to justify their proposed
          costs and to show that their investment plans are both deliverable and
          affordable. Ofwat then reviews these proposals, assessing whether the costs are
          well-supported, comparing them across companies, and removing or adjusting
          any elements that appear inefﬁcient or lack sufﬁcient evidence. Following
          consultation and reﬁnement, Ofwat issues a Final Determination, which sets,
          among other things, the investment, service, and funding framework each
          company must deliver in the next AMP.


3.259. During PR14, South East Water had a total revenue 470 of £1.037 billion and was
          given a total base expenditure allowance of £785 million (2012–13 prices),471 3%

          Determination, that South East Water's supply demand situation was atypical,
          we were unconvinced by its proposed interventions which lacked independent
          benchmarking and assurance. Since the company did not address our concerns
          about regional network and zonal strategies, we did not approve additional
          enhancement expenditure relating to this which meant, based on the evidence
          provided, we allowed £155 million for core enhancements. 472

3.261. South East Water sought accelerated funding outside of the price review

        impact the same groups of customers repeatedly with the obvious implications
        that will bring". 482 Ofwat rejected the proposals because South East Water did
        not provide sufﬁcient evidence to justify the schemes.

        monitoring of base expenditure to ensure funding allocated to maintaining
        assets was spent on maintaining assets.486


3.263. Not only does the price review process require companies to invest in their
        networks to maintain service, meet regulatory standards, and address future
        challenges, Ofwat can assess whether companies are investing as planned,
        spending more than expected, or underspending and potentially delaying
        delivery during the annual end of year reporting process through companies
        APRs. This is achieved by comparing each company's cumulative wholesale
        totex allowance with how much the company has spent so far. An underspend
        could indicate efﬁcient delivery or a shortfall in planned delivery, while an
        overspend could indicate inefﬁcient delivery or the need to deliver more than
        planned. This comparison is then used to make ﬁnancial adjustments at the
        next price review, so that any savings or extra costs from spending more or less
        than planned are shared fairly between customers and the company. Table 3.2
        summarises South East Water's cumulative wholesale totex allowance against its
        cumulative actual wholesale totex for PR14 and PR19, highlighting whether it
        overspent or underspent during those price reviews.

                         totex allowance               wholesale totex          overspend

                         (2019-20 prices)              (2019-20 prices)

3.267. South East Water also considers that, in its experience, Ofwat has found it
       challenging to assess its network capacity enhancement schemes because
        there are no clear resilience standards, few suitable comparators, and the most
        effective solutions in its area are smaller local interconnectors and extra storage

        uncertainty over whether these schemes should be assessed through the WRMP
        or the price review.496 South East Water stated that recent WRMPs and price
        reviews have focused mainly on demand-reduction measures, such as metering
        and ODIs on per-capita consumption, with little allowance for supply-side
        investment, but expected reductions in peak demand have not materialised.497

Ofwat's ﬁndings on planning and investment and the effect on
South East Water's system resilience
3.268. South East Water did not implement effective internal controls to review or
       update the actions and principles set out in its Area Plans introduced in 2019
       and 2020. While the company describes its Area Plan criteria as “long-term ideal
       conditions,” the plans show it was aware of asset risks since at least 2019.
       Despite this, during the Relevant Period, it failed to take sufﬁcient action to
        mitigate these risks through a proactive, planned preventative maintenance
        programme (see Section 3.3.1) or by executing the remedial actions it had
        identiﬁed. A reasonable company would have maintained assets proactively,
        with clear remediation plans consistent with its general duty under section 37
        WIA91. The evidence below shows that South East Water lacked visibility of asset
        availability and did not have a sufﬁcient understanding of its water supply
        system.

WRMP and Headroom
3.269. Accurate forecasting of supply and demand is an important part of maintaining
       an efﬁcient and economical water supply system as it ensures that there is
        enough water in the supply system available for customers, even during periods
        of high demand. We consider that South East Water's planning of its WRMP as it
        relates to WAFU has been broadly adequate as Appendix 7 shows that South East
        Water's Final Plan WAFU targets/forecasts were reasonable given the
        information available at the time.




495
    South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 2.46.

    South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 2.46.

    South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 2.47.

                                                 129
        Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                 on South East Water Limited



3.270. However, we consider that South East Water's WRMP does not capture sub-zonal
       issues. While South East Water's approach of using ADPW in planning for DYCP is
        contemplated by the WRPG, the WRPG allows for ﬂexibility and it is for
        companies to select the methodology that is ﬁt for purpose for their water
        supply system. We accept that planning for peak demands is not the focus of the
        WRMP, however, this does not exempt a company from responsibility or the
        expectation to undertake adequate resilience planning against shorter duration
        peak events, such as daily peaks, or smaller geographical areas/sub-zones.
        South East Water could have improved its water resource modelling granularity
        or WRZ boundaries to identify sub-zonal issues within its WRMPs during the
        Relevant Period, particularly given the challenges posed by known
        vulnerabilities in relation to lack of connectivity and prevalence of single points
        of failure. These sub-zonal issues could have then been mitigated/managed by
        operational interventions, proactive maintenance or investment in period or
        through investment with needs evidenced through resilience business cases
        submitted in business plans. Indeed, in 2023, Ofwat suggested to South East
        Water that it should consider selectively splitting WRZs to better reﬂect future
        system conﬁguration and enable proposed schemes to demonstrate a zonal
        WAFU beneﬁt for WRMP purposes. 498 This targeted approach would improve
        alignment between future zonal integrity, supply demand modelling, and
        scheme justiﬁcation. 499 We consider better modelling and integrating with the
        WRMP may have helped identify sub-zonal issues with supply resilience before
        these risks materialised.


3.271. In relation to headroom, South East Water’s own analysis identiﬁes a clear
       regional imbalance between its Eastern and Western regions, with headroom in
       the Eastern region exhibiting a sustained downward trend. South East Water
        attributes this reduction to demand growth and underlying capacity constraints.
        South East Water was aware of these known vulnerabilities in the Eastern region
        prior to the Relevant Period and despite South East Water identifying insufﬁcient
        headroom as a risk from as early as 2018, it has still not addressed the lack of
        headroom even in light of the frequency and severity of supply interruptions that
        it experienced during the Relevant Period. Further, lack of proactive asset
        maintenance, and the failure to maintain adequate storage levels in advance of
        and during critical periods (as set out in Section 3.3.1) and South East Water's

       poor performance with respect to leakage (outlined below) have also eroded

Leakage
3.272. Accurate leakage forecasting ensures a company's water supply is used
       efﬁciently, resources are allocated wisely and operating and capital costs are
       kept as low as possible, maintaining an efﬁcient and economical water supply
       system. Reducing water leakage is also important for a company, as it helps
       alleviate demand by conserving a signiﬁcant amount of water that would
       otherwise be lost. It maximises the use of existing infrastructure, lowers
       operating costs, and ensures a reliable, efﬁcient, and economical water supply.


3.273. As at paragraph 3.271, from at least 2018, South East Water has been aware that
       it has insufﬁcient headroom, affecting supply in parts of its network, yet it did
       not meet its WRMP19 and WRMP24 leakage forecasts. We consider that if it had
       met those forecasts, it would have created more headroom, decreasing demand.
       This can be seen at Appendix 7, which shows that leakage for all Relevant WRZs
       had exceeded the WRMP forecast targets.


3.274. South East Water’s leakage performance, since 2022, has been consistently
       inadequate. This not only reinforces the need for South East Water to reduce
       leakage to relieve pressure on demand but also raises concerns about the
       effectiveness of its Executive and Board in monitoring performance against
       leakage PCLs and managing its supply demand balance. A reasonable company’s
       Board would be expected to have robust systems and processes in place to track
       progress against these commitments and take corrective action where PCLs are
       not being met.

PWPC
3.275. Updating and correctly calculating PWPC ensures that a company's water supply
       can meet peak demand efﬁciently, make the best use of existing assets, and
       avoid unnecessary expenditure, supporting an efﬁcient and economical supply.


3.276. The analysis at paragraph 3.220, indicates that South East Water has not
       materially increased PWPC irrespective of the peak demand challenges South
       East Water has faced and the increasing or decreasing amount of DI. The
       analysis also indicates that that South East Water has not been annually
       reviewing the values that are used to calculate PWPC, as it has not made
       changes to its PWPC to reﬂect its highest historical performance. This can also
       be seen throughout all Relevant WRZs as there is very little review or changes to
       reﬂect the actual DI ﬁgure (either up or down). This indicates that South East

                                          131
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



        Water has also not been testing each site every ﬁve years to provide reliable
        peak site capacity.


3.277. Further, it has not been calculating PWPC correctly by applying a multiplier of
        seven rather than the observed maximum weekly DI. These expectations are
        outlined in Ofwat's unplanned PC guidance which we detail at paragraphs 2.23-
        2.24. We note South East Water's submission about original shadow reporting in
        2017, however, the PWPC guidance was ﬁrst published on 27 March 2018 and the
        version that was in operation for 2020-25, did not direct companies to use peak
        deployable output. 500 We consider that South East Water's approach that
        multiples a daily ﬁgure is incorrect and does not account for any
        interdependencies or constraints as it assumes the maximum output can be
        sustained across seven days in a peak week scenario. We do not have any
        evidence that South East Water calculated PWPC correctly during the Relevant
        Period. 501 Further, South East Water's "ongoing" approach misinterprets the
        PWPC guidance as the reference to the risk-based "alternative" relates to testing
        requirements not the PWPC formula itself. If South East Water had calculated its
        PWPC ﬁgures correctly, it would have resulted in more accurate reporting which
        would have made PWPC a more useful planning tool.

3.278. South East Water's view that PWPC is an asset health measure (paragraph 3.222)
        is consistent with our guidance that the PWPC calculated to inform unplanned
        outage is an asset capability measure and that unplanned outage is the asset
        health indicator. However, a key element of production planning involves the
        capability and availability of a company's treatment and associated transmission
        assets. As such, we consider that PWPC should also be used as a planning tool as
        it allows companies to manage their water production and distribution during a
        week with the highest water usage to ensure that sufﬁcient water is available to
        meet the needs of customers while maintaining operational efﬁciency and asset
        health. In addition to this, PWPC is a useful metric when developing detailed
        annual production plans per site/region and this dovetails with efforts to target
        unplanned outage performance at certain sites. A reasonable company would
        understand, monitor, test and ensure it has the peak capacity available to meet

       demand across its regions and PWPC is simply one planning tool by which to do
       this.


3.279. In addition to this, South East Water's view that seasonal changes in yield and
       allowed abstraction volumes are relevant factors for production planning
       (paragraph 3.222) seems to contradict its assertion that it has raw water
       availability during periods of high demand. If it has enough raw water during
       peak demand periods, this would rule out the need to consider seasonal
       changes in yield and allowed abstraction measures as the key metric. We
       maintain that it should be focusing on the measure the availability of capacity,
       deploying tools such as PWPC.


3.280. We acknowledge South East Water's representations at paragraph 3.223 relating
       to the difﬁculty of setting PWPC for WRZ3 and WRZ6 due to network constraints
       and bulk supplies. However, these constraints are common to all companies and
       do not detract from PWPC's usefulness as a planning tool. We would expect if a
       bulk supply or known constraint, temporary or otherwise, exists, a reasonable
       company would adopt a documented and sensible approach for setting the
       PWPC at the site in question. Regarding South East Water's concerns that the
       limits on abstraction from its licence affects its ability to meet the peak outputs
       for WRZ7, South East Water has not explained how it is managing or mitigating
       this risk. Further, regardless of any difﬁculties at these three WRZs, South East
       Water should still be able to conﬁrm the peak demand and accurately calculate
       the PWPC available.

Hydraulic modelling
3.281. Maintaining hydraulic models is important for companies as models provide an
       accurate, up-to-date representation of their water distribution system, which is
       essential for maintaining an efﬁcient and economical water supply system.
       While we acknowledge the extensive programme that South East Water has
       scheduled for AMP8 in terms of recalibration of its hydraulic models (which in
       any case will not be completed before the end of AMP) and the roll out of the
       smart networks and smart metering programme, this does not detract from our
       ﬁndings about the adequacy and efﬁcacy of its hydraulic models during the
       Relevant Period.


3.282. We expect a reasonable company to be regularly maintaining their hydraulic
       models. This includes regular updates with latest GIS data, a set of logged data
       (ﬁeld and/or live telemetry), customer demand information as well as
       considering other factors such as asset abandonment, network rezoning, new
       housing, non-household developments and new assets. Hydraulic models should

                                           133
          Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                   on South East Water Limited



           be reviewed every ﬁve years – this is standard across the industry. As South East
           Water's model stock is, on average, ten years out of date, we consider that
           without ﬁve-yearly audits, it should not have used or relied upon those models
           to make investment decisions or for repair planning during incidents as it would
           result in inefﬁciencies and take longer to resolve.502 Further, as hydraulic
           modelling is a key element of identifying areas of supply risk in current and
           future scenarios, as well as predicting pressures and identifying bottlenecks, we
           would have expected South East Water to have updated its hydraulic models in
           2016 as it would have been better prepared when it was planning for its WRMP19
           and PR19, even if it assumed (erroneously) that demand measures would
           counterbalance any growth.


3.283. Many of its models remain substantially out of date, with some last updated in
       2017 and the majority not updated since 2010–11. This indicates a material gap
       between the company’s understanding of the need for updated modelling and
       its actual practice, raising concerns about the adequacy of its asset
       management. Having aged and degraded hydraulic models, made it difﬁcult for
       South East Water to identify sub-zonal interconnectivity schemes at WRMP24
       and PR24. This is because the models do not accurately represent current
       network conﬁguration and asset performance. This then affects the capability of
       the hydraulic models to detect localised sub-zonal constraints and assess the
       feasibility/beneﬁt of interconnections. In addition, despite acknowledging post-
       pandemic changes in demand (see more regarding our assessment of the
       impact of this in Covid-19 subsection below), the company chose to wait until
       PR24 to seek funding for its smart networks and smart metering programme and
       to make changes to its WRMP24 regarding sub-zonal issues (paragraph 3.270).
           Taken together, these factors raise concerns about the robustness of South East
           Water’s optioneering process. The natural conclusion from this is that hydraulic
           modelling options were not developed from a current or reliable evidence base,
           and that South East Water was not consistently identifying or prioritising optimal
           solutions in a timely manner.


3.284. After asking South East Water to provide examples of its hydraulic models being
       used during the Events within the Relevant Period, to ascertain how its hydraulic
       models are used in operational optioneering during incidents, we found that
           South East Water was using its models mostly to determine customer impact
           and the calculation of GSS payments. As such, we consider that South East

        Water does not appear to consistently run campaigns of scenario modelling to

Root cause analysis
3.285. Robust root cause analysis is essential for maintaining an efﬁcient and
       economical water supply. By conducting this analysis at the time of or shortly
        after supply interruption incidents and identifying underlying system
        vulnerabilities or need for changes to operational processes, rather than
        reacting to immediate triggers, such as extreme weather, companies can
        prevent repeat failures and reduce customer disruption. Root cause analysis
        also strengthens long-term planning and ensures that investment decisions are
        targeted, evidence-based, and aligned with regulatory expectations.


3.286. South East Water said that, as part of its lessons learned after the Events, it had
        undertaken a comprehensive analysis of the root causes of the supply resilience
        issues. This analysis informed both the short-term actions in its Action Plan and
        the investment proposals in its PR24 business plan. However, South East Water
        later acknowledged that its PR24 submission had to be reformulated and
        supplemented, indicating that its original submission lacked the clarity and
        robustness expected for a fully developed root cause analysis. From 2022, South
        East Water's Board knew it needed to do a more thorough root cause analysis to
        improve resilience, 503 but we consider this has been impeded by a lack of
        fundamental understanding of root cause analysis. We are also concerned that
        this comprehensive root cause analysis appears to have been carried out
        primarily for the PR24 process, rather than being an established and embedded
        part of South East Water’s routine systems and processes during the Relevant
        Period.


3.287. As part of the Fourth s203 Notice Response, South East Water provided various
       documents as evidence of its approach to root cause analysis, including the

      •    "Extreme weather" or "high demand" was identiﬁed as a root cause. 509 We
           consider extreme weather to be a trigger event rather than the root cause of
           the issue. In its Representations, it says "each event was different but the
           common issue to all these events was demand for water exceeded South East
           Water's supply capability and communities at higher elevations, on the end of
           these more vulnerable systems, were disproportionately impacted through
           supply interruptions".510 This fundamentally misunderstands the nature and
           purpose of a root cause analysis which would identify internal factors and/or
           known vulnerabilities within its water supply system that were, or could be,
           impacted by extreme weather in order to identify and implement mitigating
           actions to prevent reoccurrence or at the very least to minimise the impact of
           supply interruptions. Without a thorough assessment of root cause, we
           consider it likely that the known vulnerabilities in its Eastern region and/or
           deﬁciencies in South East Water planning and response are more
           appropriately characterised as one of the root causes of the Events than
           extreme weather.
      •    There is limited information on the outcome of monitoring and reviewing
           proposed actions to see if the actual beneﬁt (e.g. risk reduction and
           mitigation) has been delivered. While Ofwat has seen some evidence of this,
           for example, South East Water's review of 2022 Storm Eunice Event considers
           the actions from the 2020 High Demand Event, this review has occurred in
           response to another event. It does not appear that there is a formal process
           whereby monitoring and review of actions routinely happens. As such, we
           have limited evidence of South East Water actively monitoring the
           effectiveness of its solutions.

           clear working beyond the macro factors in its incident review conclusions
           section. We would expect factors such as failures in asset management or
           strategy, maintenance or management system and monitoring to be root
           causes. South East Water does show some reﬂection on underlying leakage
           factors in the recommendations, as it mentions ﬂow and pressure and refers
           to a revised leakage strategy, however, it is difﬁcult to map these actions
           against the root cause.
     •     Flooding at site: South East Water refers to a feasibility study for two water
           treatment works but it does not explain the reason for the ﬂooding or list the
           root cause(s) of the ﬂooding which may have led to the selected option to
           investigate ﬂood defences.
     •     Turbidity: South East Water mentions this is due to scouring. This does not
           address the 'why' question – it is unclear if the company has explored if there
           are any issues with the design of assets, underlying maintenance or operator
           related issues; or if there are any methodologies that need review for how this
           is undertaken; for example, ﬂushing regime, valve operations etc.

3.289. The above indicates that South East Water did not undertake meaningful root
       cause analysis during the Relevant Period, despite “identifying the root cause”
       being a stated component of its own six-stage methodology. Had such analysis
       been carried out, the company would have recognised internal factors as well as
       external and root causes rather than immediate causes or trigger events (such
       as high demand or extreme weather). For an investigation of the root cause of
       an event of this signiﬁcance, we would expect the documents to show
       consideration of broader organisational issues, such as, the need for
       improvements in business process control (e.g. training, incident management,
         operational control), which they did not. Further, South East Water's analysis
         moves directly to conclusions/recommendations (e.g. action plans, optioneering
         and capital programmes), without providing a summary of data/evidence it
         investigated and its analytical process, which make it difﬁcult to map the causal
         factors and proposed actions. This is further underscored by the fact that,
         during the minded-to process, South East Water sought clarification from us on
         the definition of root cause analysis. A reasonable company would ensure that it
         has a good understanding of this concept and develop internal processes to
         conduct effective root cause analysis. We conclude that South East Water does
         not understand how to do a thorough root cause analysis or have such internal
         processes in place.


3.290. We note that South East Water said that the actions in its Action Plan go some
       way to address issues identiﬁed in its root cause analysis (although further
         investment is needed in AMP8). Because of the ﬁndings outlined above, we are

                                            138
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



       not satisﬁed that South East Water has undertaken sufﬁcient root cause analysis
       to ensure that the actions within its Action Plan are targeted to the root cause of
       its supply interruptions and resilience issues. In December 2024, South East
       Water provided us with one WRZ1 case study, which begins to illustrate how its
       Action Plan will address some of the supply system resilience issues in WRZ1. We
       noted that in the ﬁve months (June-December) since receiving our original
       Action Plan Feedback, South East Water did not develop case studies for the
       other water resource zones in the Eastern region. We also note that this case
       study was not proactively developed in response to the issues experienced by
       South East Water but produced by South East Water in response to our
       investigation. We communicated to South East Water in our Clariﬁcation Letter
       on 6 May 2025 that we are not satisﬁed that the Action Plan alone, even with the
       additional case study, is sufﬁcient to address the root causes identiﬁed within
       this investigation.


3.291. A lack of robust root cause analysis undertaken by South East Water weakens
       the effectiveness of the measures proposed in its Action Plan and reduces
       conﬁdence that its investment proposals in PR24 were sufﬁcient and
       appropriately targeted. Without a clear understanding of the underlying reasons
       for past and ongoing performance issues, South East Water cannot implement
       the appropriate actions to tackle root causes, leading to reactive or incomplete
       solutions. This makes it difﬁcult to be conﬁdent that its proposed schemes and
       investment are aligned to the real drivers of poor performance, especially as root
       cause analysis is essential for optioneering and demonstrating expenditure
       cases as part of the price review process; something that has proved difﬁcult for
       South East Water (see the Investment subsection below). These challenges,
       compounded using outdated hydraulic models (see subsection above), limit
       South East Water's understanding of how its network operates and constraints
       within its network, making it more vulnerable.

Climate change
3.292. We have considered the Representations that South East Water made regarding
       climate change. These, however, do not change our view that it has contravened
       section 37 WIA91 or Licence Condition P12 for the following reasons.


3.293. The impacts of climate change were known to the industry (from Ofwat's
       publication of climate change policy statement in 2008 and climate change




                                           139
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited



           adaption reports in 2011 and 2016), 511 with the need to prepare for extreme
           weather events emphasised prior to the Relevant Period by Ofwat and by the
           DWI in their respective reviews of the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event. In this context,
           we consider that South East Water should have absorbed the lessons from the
           2018 Freeze Thaw Event and taken further action during the Relevant Period to
           address the growing risks posed by extreme weather and climate change. These
           risks were already recognised within WRMP14 and WRMP19 planning. Indeed, in
           WRMP14 South East Water itself acknowledged increasingly unpredictable
           weather patterns dating back to 2010. Moreover, through its participation in the
           Water UK's National Water Resources Long Term Planning Framework, its
           identiﬁcation of climate-related risks on its CRR from 2018, and the change of
           risk to "principal risk" in its 2021-22 Annual Report, it is clear that South East
           Water was aware of climate change as a material issue from at least the start of
           the Relevant Period.


3.294. While South East Water maintains that extreme weather in 2022 was
       "unforeseeable", Ofwat considers that while the extreme hot spells observed
       during AMP7, particularly in 2022, were severe, they represent a continuation
       and acceleration of known climate trends rather than an unforeseeable “step
       change”. We note that companies were already expected to adapt progressively
       to increasing climate risks and to plan for a range of climate outcomes, not just
       median projections. As such, we do not consider extreme weather experienced
       during the Relevant Period to be altogether unforeseeable, but rather a
       continuation of climate change impacts that were identiﬁed before the Relevant
       Period.

3.295. Although we consider a thorough root cause analysis is required, as outlined in
           the Root Cause Analysis subsection, we consider it likely that the disruptions
           during the Events in the Relevant Period stemmed from the failures with respect
           to maintenance outlined in section 3.3.1, poor planning in section 3.3.2 and the
           known vulnerabilities in the Eastern region, rather than from “extreme weather”
           as South East Water claims. This is demonstrated by the stronger performance
           of other companies in the South East, as well as South East Water’s own Western
           region, which operates under similar management, processes and weather
           conditions but did not experience comparable interruptions. These known
           vulnerabilities make robust preventative maintenance and effective operational
           response (examined in section 3.3.3) even more critical.




511
      Paragraphs 2.33 and 2.34.

                                                140
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited




3.296. We recognise that South East Water has conducted work to understand the
           impacts of climate change from AMP8 through to AMP12; however, this is
           forward facing only. Similarly, while large-scale works have been designed to
           mitigate growth and climate change risks, there have been delays to these
           works. 512 While it is positive that South East Water have worked to understand
           the impacts that climate change going forward, this does nothing to mitigate
           the effects that have already been experienced by customers. With climate
           change and water stress being a known issue to South East Water it is unclear
           why it has taken many years for modelling to be undertaken to understand the
           effects of climate change on its network.

Covid-19
3.297. We agree that the short-term impact of Covid-19 during the pandemic was
       unforeseeable, however, we have examined South East Water's claim regarding
       the pandemic introducing permanent changes to demand patterns. South East
       Water has consistently maintained that demand has signiﬁcantly increased in
       the period following Covid-19.


3.298. Our analysis at Appendix 7 shows that for WRZs 1, 2, 3 and 6, South East Water
       has not experienced demand peaks higher than its forecasted Final Plan WAFU
       for its WRMP19 (2020–45 planning horizon) and WRMP24 (2025-50). This
       includes during the years of Covid-19 lockdowns and the subsequent years. As
       such, we do not consider that the effects of Covid-19 impacted demand to the
       extent it challenged the forecasted Final Plan WAFU levels for these speciﬁc
       WRZs. Appendix 7 also shows that WRZs 1, 2, 3 and 6 had experienced similar
           demand peaks prior to 2020. This suggests that the demand following Covid-19
           had not materially changed in these WRZs. As such, for WRZs 1, 2, 3 and 6, this
           indicates South East Water should have had capacity to deliver secure supplies
           provided it delivered according to its plans and operated its network effectively.

3.299. In terms of WRZ7 and WRZ8, Appendix 7 shows that the peak demands
       experienced as a result of Covid-19 were of limited duration, and while this was
       higher than Final Plan WAFU, was not sustained over the long term. In addition
           to this, the regional PCC data provided by Europe Economics, which formed part




512

        of a report mentioned at paragraph 2.38, 513 demonstrates that while there was
        an increase in PCC in the year of the Covid-19 pandemic, this has been followed
        by successive reductions in the years from 2021 across all of South East Water's
        WRZs. While we recognise that the data from 2020-25 uses a new leakage
        reporting methodology, and is not directly comparable to 2015-20 data, the
        reduction in PCC in the last three years demonstrates that shifting consumption
        patterns, following the Covid-19 pandemic, has not resulted in a sustained
        increase in PCC. This is further supported by our PR24 PPC data which shows
        that PCC is lower than pre-Covid levels. For example, during the years of 2017 to
        the end of 2019, the annual PPC was between 146l/h/d and 148l/h/d; from 2023
        this has decreased to 143.4l/h/d. 514

Investment
3.300. Proper investment in maintaining and renewing key assets ensures the network
       remains reliable and resilient, allowing the company to meet regulatory
       obligations, manage risks, and provide a continuous and efﬁcient water supply.
       We disagree with South East Water's representations that it had received
       insufﬁcient funding in the past. In any event, these representations do not alter
       our ﬁndings regarding breach of section 37 WIA91 and Condition P12. We
       examine below whether South East Water has sought funding, in a well-
       evidenced and efﬁcient manner, and the way it deployed this funding to support
        the delivery of its legal obligations.


3.301. South East Water asserts with respect to funding that was historically disallowed
       by Ofwat at price review, that Ofwat has “not properly considered how its own
       actions may have contributed to or affected outcomes.” We do not accept this
        assertion. The history of successive price reviews, and the fact that South East
        Water sought no redeterminations for PR14 or PR19, indicates that the company
        regarded the funding it received in both periods as sufﬁcient to meet its legal
        obligations, including base expenditure. We note that the IWC Report makes
        recommendations for future price reviews, and that the CMA’s Redetermination
        will assess the adequacy of South East Water’s base expenditure for 2025–30;
        however, we are more concerned with its investment decisions during Relevant
        Period for the reasons below.

                                on South East Water Limited




3.302. While we acknowledge that Ofwat did not approve any of South East Water's
        schemes that formed part of Defra's Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project,
        South East Water did not provide sufﬁcient evidence to justify these schemes.
        For instance, the proposed delivery dates for these schemes remained
        unchanged so would not be "accelerated" – South East Water were effectively
        following the original timetable of delivery rather than delivering the schemes
        more quickly as the programme required.515 We also identiﬁed that some of the
        schemes either overlapped with activities that were already funded through
        base expenditure allowances or that several sub-zonal schemes, while
        potentially beneﬁcial at a very local level, did not generate a measurable
        increase in WAFU at the wider WRZ level 516 and did not meet WRMP
        requirements.517 Further, Ofwat has supported several other resilience schemes;
        for example, additional expenditure allowances were given in PR14 and PR19 to
        increase resilience, notably for work at Broad Oak reservoir.


3.303. The history of price review submissions, together with the Accelerated
        Infrastructure Delivery Proposal, contains instances where Ofwat rejected
        funding requests on the basis that allowances had already been provided for the
        investments concerned. This indicates that South East Water should have
        undertaken this work in earlier periods. Likewise, where Ofwat rejected schemes
        due to insufﬁcient evidence, South East Water ought to have carried out a more
        robust optioneering process and root cause analysis (as detailed above) to
        demonstrate the need for the speciﬁc investment or to explain why it had not
        already been delivered.


3.304. While its underspend in PR14 may partly reﬂect efﬁciencies, it may also stem
       from lower construction costs or from the deferral of enhancement investments,
       such as the Ardingly treatment works upgrade, alongside reduced levels of
        mains renewal. South East Water’s overspend in PR19, by contrast, reﬂects
        several factors, including its reprioritisation of short-term resilience measures
        for the Action Plan in this investigation. The majority of the 41 schemes in South
        East Water's Further Updated Action Plan were already funded at PR14, PR19 or
        through base expenditure. This suggests that South East Water had already

      been funded for many of the schemes through earlier price reviews (PR14 and
      PR19) but did not deliver them at the time, instead deferring or re-presenting
      those investments in its Action Plan as remedial actions rather than timely,
      planned delivery. With respect of the 15 "new" schemes and the two accelerated
      schemes from PR24 with a combined value of £18.95 million, we note that
      [CONFIDENTIAL] and others should have been funded via base expenditure.
      Therefore, we do not consider all of these schemes to be "new" or additional
      redress.


3.305. South East Water argues that it has lacked funding for network capacity
       investment and claims that the regulatory framework regarding this is ﬂawed
      because network capacity analysis has not been a focal point since privatisation,
      when most assets were originally built with sufﬁcient capacity. We do not accept
      this argument as section 37 WIA91 requires water companies to maintain an
      efﬁcient and economical water supply system and to make all necessary
      arrangements to meet their obligations under Part III, which includes assessing
      and managing network capacity. We also note that other companies have
      developed grid network projects. Although South East Water asserts that such
      projects are not feasible in its area, their existence demonstrates that the
      regulatory regime does, in fact, take network capacity into account.

3.306. We do not agree with South East Water’s argument that its rural nature should
       prevent it from making effective enhancement cases during the price review
       process. Even if South East Water’s point were accepted, its rural nature,
       together with its known vulnerabilities in the Eastern region and the fact that
       Ofwat only partially funded resilience schemes in PR14 and PR19 for the reasons
       set out above, made it all the more important for the company to use its base
      expenditure allowance effectively during the Relevant Period to maintain an
      efﬁcient and economical water supply system.

3.307. In any event, as our evidence in Section 3.3.1 demonstrates, many of South East
      Water's failings relate to lack of basic monitoring and maintenance that is not
      dependent on capex funding. If South East Water had processes in place to
      address this, it would have been in a better position to engage with Ofwat's
      business planning process.

Summary of ﬁnding and conclusions on section 37 WIA91
3.308. Our ﬁndings show that despite South East Water being aware of the known
      vulnerabilities in its Eastern region it has failed to maintain sufﬁcient headroom
      or meet its WRMP leakage forecasts or accurately use its PWPC as a planning
      tool. We found its hydraulic models were out-of-date which further adversely

                                          144
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



         impacted its ability to understand the effects of the likely demand on its supply
         system and plan for peak periods. Similarly, it did not undertake root cause
         analysis to identify the root cause beyond the trigger events (such as "high
         demand" or "extreme weather"). These failures have affected South East Water's
         ability to appropriately forecast, plan for and measure demand, resulting in its
         assets not being able to effectively cope with periods of high demand, reducing
         its ability to maintain an efﬁcient and economical water supply.

3.309. Contrary to South East Water's representations, we found that the extreme
         weather events in 2022 represented a continuation and acceleration of known
         climate trends that South East Water was aware of (or should have been due to
         messaging by Ofwat and the DWI). As such the Events within the Relevant Period
         were not unforeseeable. We also disagree with South East Water's
         representations regarding demand driven changed working patterns since
         Covid-19. Our ﬁndings show that the effect of Covid-19 was of limited duration
         and did not have a sustained impact on PCC. Similarly, in response to South East
         Water's claims that it has been "underfunded", we consider that it has been
         appropriately funded and that Ofwat has only rejected schemes on the basis
         that allowances had already been provided for the investments concerned or
         due to insufﬁcient evidence. Again, investment optioneering would have been
         aided by a proper approach to planning, including root cause analysis.


3.310. Our ﬁndings demonstrate that while South East Water has failed to properly
       investigate the underlying root cause of supply interruptions and plan for
       extreme weather events, including by failing to maintain sufﬁcient headroom;
       met its leakage targets; and plan for sub-zonal issues, during the Relevant
         Period. Planning is an integral part of developing and maintaining a water supply
         system and forms part of the arrangements necessary for meeting obligations
         under Part III WIA91. We therefore consider that South East Water has
         contravened section 37 WIA91 as it has failed to put appropriate planning
         processes in place in order to maintain its water supply system and meet its
         domestic supply obligation. Given the issues raised in our investigation, we
         consider it is a real risk that it will not be able provide sufﬁcient domestic supply
         in the future and therefore this contravention is ongoing.


3.311. We found that, in contravention of section 37 WIA91, South East Water:
     •     Failed to maintain its sufﬁcient headroom in its Eastern region. We found that
           South East Water had known from at least 2018, that it had insufﬁcient
           headroom and this was affecting supply in its Eastern region. By failing to
           maintain key assets, failing to maintain adequate storage levels in advance of
           and during critical periods and not meeting its WRMP leakage forecasts, all of

                                              145
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



           which would have eroded headroom, South East Water failed to manage its
           supply demand balance which resulted in the company being with less
           headroom and less able to respond than we would expect a reasonable
           company to have be able to in the lead up to, and during the Events in the
           Relevant Period.
     •     Failed to calculate an accurate PWPC and use it as a planning tool to manage
           its water production and distribution during a week with the highest water
           usage to ensure that sufﬁcient water is available to meet the needs of
           customers while maintaining operational efﬁciency and asset health.
     •     Failed to audit and update its hydraulic models every ﬁve years in line with
           standard industry practice in order of priority based on risk. It has also failed
           to maintain and develop a rolling maintenance programme for its hydraulic
           models based on the results of that audit. This breach has been ongoing since
           2016 and throughout the Relevant Period and negatively impacted South East
           Water's ability to assess supply demand balance at sub-zonal levels and
           engineer options for investment and during operational interventions during
           the Events within the Relevant Period.
     •     Failed to undertake adequate root cause analysis, including the vital step of
           identifying possible causal factors and root cause(s) in response to supply
           interruption incidents but also more generally as part of its optioneering
           processes.
     •     Failed to mitigate the risks posed by extreme weather and climate change
           that became apparent before and during the Relevant Period. We would have
           expected a reasonable water company to have implemented actions and
           recommendations from the DWI and Ofwat's review of water companies'
           response to the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event.


Summary of ﬁndings and conclusions on Licence Condition P12
3.312. Referring to the evidence in Sections 3.3.2, we do not consider that South East
       Water has in place adequate systems of planning and internal control – it should
       have ensured its operational staff used the tools at their disposal to plan for the
         delivery of its duties, including by updating its Area Plans and hydraulic models,
         and effectively using PWPC and root cause analysis. We consider that South East
         Water's planning and use of investment was not best calculated to ensure it had
         adequate ﬁnancial and management resources in place in breach of Licence
         Condition P12. By not providing sufﬁcient reason for investment in previous price
         reviews, optioneering using out dated hydraulic models and by not using its
         base expenditure to maintain its assets (see Section 3.3.1 and Section 3.3.2),
         South East Water failed to meet its regulatory requirement to maintain its assets
         effectively which has affected its supply resilience to sufﬁciently cope with
         periods of high demand.

                                             146
           Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                    on South East Water Limited




3.313. We found that, in contravention of Licence Condition P12, South East Water and
           speciﬁcally its Board and Executive Team:
       •   Failed to monitor supply resilience risks the company's supply system
           resilience risks, properly scrutinising its compliance with its legal and
           regulatory obligations and ensure that appropriate action was taken in
           response to those risks.
       •   Failed to ensure appropriate systems of planning and internal control to
           monitor the delivery of mitigating actions included in Area Plans introduced in
           2019 and 2020 for its WRZs, and to update these plans.
       •   Failed to have adequate systems of planning and internal control and
           management processes in place to monitor performance against leakage to
           meet PCLs and managing its headroom and supply demand balance. This
           includes a failure by its Board and Executive Team to ensure sufﬁcient reporting
           metrics to give them a clear picture of whether South East Water was properly
           complying with its general duty under section 37 WIA91.
       •   Failed to calculate PWPC correctly and use it as a planning tool since it was
           introduced by Ofwat in 2019.
       •   Failed to use accurate and up-to-date hydraulic models to plan for
           maintenance programme, operational response to incidents and undertake
           effective optioneering, including for investment.
       •   Failed to effectively use root cause analysis to inform its planned maintenance
           and its investment decisions, including within the price review process, to
           identify the most effective solution via effective optioneering.
       •   Failed to understand the likely impacts of extreme weather and climate change
           on its water supply system.

3.3.3 Operational response to and preparedness for the
Events
3.314. South East Water has stated that it manages supply system resilience risks
       through immediate operational response and the prioritisation of investment
       when supply interruption events occur. South East Water noted that the type of
           solution, and therefore the investment required, will depend on the nature of
           the risks and implications on deliverability and lead time. 518 Sections 3.3.1 and
           3.3.2 detail South East Water's reactive approach to maintenance of key assets
           and insufﬁcient planning and investment, which impacted on its supply
           resilience during the Relevant Period. Due to the known vulnerabilities within its



518
      South East Water, First s203 Response, 20 December 2023, p.21.

                                                     147
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



        Eastern region, we found that more needed to be done in these areas to provide
        South East Water's customers with resilience of supply.


3.315. The impact of this inaction by South East Water was most evident during
        extreme weather events, all of which tested its operational response, which
        proved insufﬁcient. We have identiﬁed a number of recurring themes/issues
        detailed below. South East Water stated that during the Relevant Period it
        planned its incident response according to SEMD guidance, designing its
        reasonable worst case scenario as approximately 34,500 customers. 519 In
        response to the DWI noting that the company regularly had events exceeding
        this ﬁgure (paragraph 3.83), as part of PR24, South East Water increased its
        SEMD worst case scenario to 100,000 customers to reﬂect the number of
        customers impacted by the Events in the Relevant Period (see Table 3.3). 520

Summary of the Events
3.316. The Events themselves are detailed more fully in Appendices 1–6, however we
       have summarised below the key areas where lessons should have been learnt by
        South East Water during the Relevant Period. Since 2018, South East Water's
        customers in the Eastern region have experienced multiple supply interruption
        incidents, with three of the Events occurring within a one-year period. 521 As
        outlined in Section 3.2.2, South East Water's performance against its supply
        interruption PCL was also consistently poor throughout the Relevant Period.


3.317. The 2018 Freeze Thaw Event, while outside of the Relevant Period, put the
       industry on notice about Ofwat's expectations regarding their operational
       response to supply interruption incidents (paragraphs 2.53 and 3.6). Ofwat
        required South East Water to provide an externally assured action plan (the

        2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan) that included updating emergency
        response plans to take into account lessons learnt; develop or update plans
        regarding the provision, deployment and delivery of alternative water supplies;
        improve their information regarding customers in vulnerable circumstances;
        develop or update a comprehensive crisis communications plan; consider their
        approach to proactively ﬁxing customer side leaks; and improve the quality of
        data so they can quickly and accurately predict and identify network issues; and
        update governance processes including considering whether the triggers for
        escalation are appropriate. 522

3.318. South East Water's 2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan contained 61 actions
       and, in September 2018, only 17 of those 61 actions were complete with the
        remaining actions being assigned future completion dates or marked as
        "ongoing." The actions were categorised as follows:
        •   Planning and preparation;
        •   Incident response, including alternative water supplies such as tankers;
        •   Communication with customers and key stakeholders;
        •   Customers in vulnerable circumstances; and
        •    Compensation.

3.319. The DWI wrote to all water companies in England and Wales about the
       widespread loss of supplies arising from the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event. It noted
       that South East Water was one of two companies reporting difﬁculties in
       meeting the minimum requirement of 10 litres per person per day under the
       SEMD guidance. 523 Some companies also used tankers in addition to bottle water
       as alternative supplies for customers. 524 The DWI noted, "This severe weather
        event has highlighted the challenges faced by the water industry in England and
        Wales when dealing with a water supply emergency that is affecting all, or a
        majority of, water companies. The capacity of companies to support others
        through existing mutual aid arrangements was severely hampered because
        every company was fully committed to managing its own".525 As such, the DWI
        recommended a review of requirements for provision of alternative supplies and

      •     Better stakeholder engagement with retailers and developers.

3.328. The DWI's report on the incident also noted the similarities between the two
       Events.547 The DWI noted that, when responding to the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event,

        matching a level of demand experienced during the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event. 548

3.333. [CONFIDENTIAL]


3.334. In August 2024, Ofwat and the Consumer Council for Water (CCW) published
       research conducted with a sample of South East Water household customers to
       understand how people and households were impacted when their water and
       wastewater services were disrupted as a result of the 2023 High Demand
       Event. 556 This research is not intended to be a statistical sample, it simply
       highlights how customers feel they have been impacted by South East Water's
       performance. Key ﬁndings included: 557
      •    All customers who participated in the research were disappointed by South
           East Water's management of the incident and in some cases, this amounted to
           a loss of trust in the company.
      •    South East Water's communication was poor; it was not proactive or honest
           and this was exacerbated by the tone of some of the communications. For
           example, an email from South East Water's CEO announcing a TUB was sent to
           customers, asking them to "use less water" when they were without supply.
           Customers felt this was insensitive and felt that they were being blamed for
           the problems they were experiencing. 558
      •    Participants did not feel supported by South East Water.

3.341. In its Representations in May 2025, South East Water stated that "despite the

3.342. South East Water included a number of actions relevant to its operational
       response and preparedness in its Action Plan developed in response to this
       investigation. These related to the improvement of bottled water planning, the
       review of PSR bottled water distribution, alternate water for schools and care
       homes, the introduction of the customer SMS communication tool and the
       reduction of leakage were all actions completed on time either by March 2024 or
        March 2025.576

Ofwat's ﬁnding on South East Water's operational response
3.343. In this section we set out our ﬁndings regarding South East Water’s incident
       preparedness and operational response.


3.344. Under paragraph 4(8) of the SEMD, one of South East Water's Regulated
        Activities is to regularly test the effectiveness of its incident plans to ensure they
        remain appropriate and to take steps to address any vulnerabilities identiﬁed.
        Throughout the Relevant Period, South East Water maintained a worst-case
        scenario planning at 34,500 customers. Despite the fact that all but one 577 of the
        Events during the Relevant Period exceeded that number (paragraph 3.315),
        with the prompting of the DWI, South East Water waited for its PR24 submission
        to amend its scenario planning to 100,000 customers. In 2022, DWI suggested
        companies should review local reasonable worst-case scenarios (paragraph
        3.83). Further, in 2022, the DWI found that, given its history of Events, South




574
    South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 3.48.
575
    South East Water, MTD Response, 27 May 2025, paragraph 3.33.
576
    South East Water, Fourth s203 Response, 21 August 2025, p.34 – p.47.
577
    The 2022 High Demand Event affected 34,105 customers.

                                                   157
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



       East Water should have conducted "a full asset and network resilience review
       which would look to ensure the future security of supply to consumers"
       (paragraph 3.324). The map in Appendix 10 highlights which areas of South East
       Water's supply system were impacted by multiple Events and South East Water
       itself has noted the similarities between Events (paragraphs 3.321 and 3.327; as
       did the DWI at paragraph 3.328). The summary of the Events and Appendices 1-6
       outlines that customers in the Eastern region were disproportionately affected
       and have experienced several outages. As such, we consider that South East
       Water failed to test the effectiveness of its scenario planning throughout the
       Relevant Period as it should have done more to prevent repeat incidents in its
       Eastern region.

Lessons learnt from the Events
3.345. We have found that South East Water has failed to effectively implement the
       lessons from previous events. Despite over 70% of the actions in the 2018 Freeze
       Thaw Event Action Plan having future completion dates, South East Water did
       not provide details of monitoring or implementation plans to ensure that the
       actions it suggested would be implemented effectively and to mitigate the
       impact of similar events in the future. From the evidence provided, South East
       Water did not holistically review the actions from the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event
       until four years later, following the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event. South East Water's
       review of the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event was, once again, only following requests
       by Ofwat and the DWI that all water companies review their performance. This is
       a reactive approach.

3.346. Further, South East Water's review of the 2022 High Demand Event was
       published in August 2023, almost a year after the event and after the 2023 High
       Demand Event. Therefore, any actions regarding drought and incident
       management from 2022 were not actioned in time for the 2023 High Demand
       Event, which is again a failure of South East Water's systems of planning and
       ability to learn from past events. This delay in actioning lessons learnt from
       Events within the Relevant Period indicates a failure to take steps to ensure a
       strong operational response to incidents, regularly test the effectiveness of
       incident plans and to take steps to address any vulnerabilities identiﬁed.


3.347. This failure to learn lessons and review incident plans, was compounded by
       South East Water's failure to undertake sufﬁcient root cause analysis to identify
       the underlying cause of supply interruptions, which would have identiﬁed
       appropriate action (as detailed in Section 3.3.2). South East Water maintains
       that "a comprehensive analysis of the root causes of the supply system
       resilience issues we encountered during high demand events in AMP7 (2020–25)

                                           158
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



        has already been carried out." 578 However, we have not seen evidence of this
        (Section 3.3.2). Further, we are concerned that South East Water's lessons
        learnt focus only on responding to an event once it has occurred (customer and
        incident responses), rather than addressing the root cause of supply system
        resilience issues within its supply system to reduce the risk of an event
        happening in the ﬁrst place and/or its impact. For example, hydraulic modelling
        could have been used to identify improvements in its water supply system and
        helping the company better plan for future periods of high demand. Our
        concerns with South East Water's approach to hydraulic modelling are outlined
        in Section 3.3.2.


3.348. While we note the steps that South East Water have taken in its Action Plan
        (paragraph 3.342) these do not seem to have had an effect on its operational
        response as recent events 579 have seen customers raise concerns regarding poor
        communication and failings in relation to bottled water supply. We note that this
        is subject of a separate investigation by Ofwat. South East Water failed to
        implement actions that would have improved its incident response during the
        Relevant Period; these are detailed further below. Our investigation looks at
        these actions over a ﬁve-year period and so we would expect to see some
        improvement in terms of operational preparedness and incident management.
        These are fundamental building-block activities that are ongoing and are
        outside of price review planning. South East Water should have in place
        appropriate processes and systems of control to ensure that its operational staff
        have the necessary information to identify potential issues and that it has the
        right culture and processes in place to ensure that remediation of these issues
        is prioritised.

TUBs and emergency triggers
3.349. As outlined at paragraph 3.46, South East Water was one of only two companies
       in the south east of England to issue two consecutive TUBs during the Relevant
        Period. This demonstrates South East Water's lack of preparedness in terms of
        its operational response to Events within the Relevant Period as it considers
        TUBs to be "a back stop option within an overall demand and customer




  South East Water, Response to Action Plan Feedback, 18 December 2024, p.19.
578

  Two events affecting Tunbridge Wells in November/December 2025 and January 2026: Where did it all go
579

wrong for South East Water? - BBC News

                                                 159
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



        messaging framework." 580 South East Water itself recognised its "high reliance
        on demand management is recognised as increasing deliverability risk." 581 This
        shows South East Water was relying on demand-side responses when we
        consider that it should have responded earlier to declining storage levels
        (Section 3.3.1) and taken steps to plan for and manage its headroom (Section
        3.3.2).


3.350. A reasonable company would "over-prepare" at the very outset of an incident,
       rather than responding to a worsening situation. We consider that a reasonable
       company would have ceased planned maintenance and convened its operational
        response earlier. Like the other companies identiﬁed by DWI who took action
        during the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, 582 South East Water should have increased
        storage in all service reservoirs and increased detection and repair activity
        during this period. For example, in the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event despite freezing
        temperatures from the beginning of the month, South East Water waited until
        15 December 2022 to convene its incident response team (paragraph A.5.5). It
        was only at the point that planned maintenance was ceased. We assess the
        adequacy of South East Water's maintenance escalation triggers at paragraph
        3.163. South East Water should consider clariﬁcation on what is expected of
        operational managers, and in particular at what level incidents should be
        escalated and subsequently managed, an undertaking agreed to upon its CEO
        meeting the Secretary of State in 2023 (paragraph 3.330).

3.351. In response to a rapidly developing shock, a reasonable water company, in
       combination with warnings from its associated network alarm limits, would
        trigger its incident response plan/preparedness. This should include any periods
        of winter freeze/thaw events typically associated with increase in water demand
        due to leakage from pipe bursts. Further, we would expect a reasonable water
        company to have implemented actions and recommendations from the DWI's
        and Ofwat's review of water companies' response to the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event.

    South East Water, Water resource management plan annual review and data return, June 2022,
Appendix 132 in South East Water's First s203 Response, p.3328. Although an action from the 2020 High
Demand Event, South East Water noted the need to consider how quickly it implemented temporary usage
bans.

    South East Water, Water Resources in South East regional plan and WRMP24 - Board Update, Appendix

3.352. Despite South East Water's representations that the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event was
       not prudent to predict the weather experienced in the Relevant Period, the DWI
        noted that other companies took action to ensure they had sufﬁcient water
        treatment and storage assets to produce water matching a demand similar to
        the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event and had taken the opportunity prior to predicted
        events to increase storage in treated water reservoirs and to increase detection
        and repair activity to resolve leaks on the network (paragraph 3.105). We
        consider these are actions that a reasonable company would have taken in
        South East Water's circumstances.


Alternative water supplies
3.353. Under paragraph 4(4)(b) of the SEMD, which for the purpose of this investigation
       forms part of its Regulated Activities, South East Water has an obligation to
        ensure a minimum supply of water is available by alternative means during an
        emergency. South East Water's response to the Events within the Relevant
        Period, demonstrates that it did not have a sufﬁcient operational response ready
        to provide alternative water supplies (including mutual aid, tankers and bottled
        water) to its customers.


3.354. One issue identiﬁed across all the Events was South East Water's overreliance on
       mutual aid from other water companies and suppliers such as WaterDirect.
        South East Water noted the need to supplement the service it received from
        WaterDirect with its own bottled water stock, as mutual aid was and is difﬁcult
        to facilitate in nationwide events such as the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event. 583 The
        DWI recommended a review of alternative water, including overreliance of
        mutual aid, after the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event (paragraph 3.319). Despite the
        DWI's recommendation, South East Water again identiﬁed overreliance on
        mutual aid from suppliers and other companies as an issue in the 2022 Storm
        Eunice Event (paragraph 3.324) and the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event (paragraph
        3.327). Paragraph 4(7) of the SEMD requires South East Water, as part of its
        Regulated Activities, to identify and assess any risks that may arise due to such
        dependencies to maintain its water supply during an emergency, including
        where it has arrangements with external suppliers of alternative water supplies.
        As such we would expect a reasonable company to ﬁnd alternatives rather than
        a continued overreliance on mutual aid.

    South East Water, 'Beast from the East' Incident Response South East Water Action Plan, September
2018, link, p.34.

3.355. Similarly, use of tankers by other companies was also noted by the DWI following
       the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event (paragraph 3.318 and 3.319). South East Water
        identiﬁed the need for additional tankers in its response to the 2018 Freeze
        Thaw Event (paragraphs 3.318 and 3.319), 2020 High Demand Event (paragraph
        3.322) and 2022 Storm Eunice Event (paragraph 3.323); however, in
        Representations that tankers are not the sole solution during incidents. 584 South
        East Water purchased two tankers as an action in its Service Commitment Plan
        given to Ofwat in response to its "lagging behind" company engagement in
        2023. 585 In its Representations, South East Water admitted that tankers had
        been useful. 586 South East Water submitted its PR24 resilience proposals
        included the purchase of new equipment and buildings (including ten
        additional tankers to limit its reliance on third party providers during incidents),
        which were to be funded within enhancement expenditure as part of the
        alternative water supply requirements under the revised SEMD worst case
        scenario (paragraph 3.344).

3.356. As part of its Further Updated Action Plan, South East Water stated that it would
        put in place dedicated mobile water tanks and order 10 new water tankers; this
        was completed in August 2025. 587


3.357. We agree with South East Water's representations that tankers are not a long-
       term resilience solution, 588 however, the use of water tankers to increase
       response capability by tankering water directly into service reservoirs or
       injecting water into the network are a short-term operational response
       measure. 589 Tankers can also be used to support water companies alternative
       water response by maintaining continuous supplies to critical infrastructure
        sites such as hospitals, care homes, and schools. While South East Water's steps
        to increase its number of tankers from two to 12 in August 2025 was positive,
        these tankers were not in place for the Events within the Relevant Period. While
        the DWI recommended that all companies review local reasonable worst-case
        scenario in 2023, we note that the SEMD requires regular testing of incident
        planning to ensure they remain appropriate and address any vulnerabilities,
        which means that South East Water had an ongoing requirement to do this.
        Given the nature of its supply system, South East Water should have considered

Vulnerable sites
3.359. Under paragraph 4(4)(d) of the SEMD, water undertakers, including South East
       Water, have a duty to maintain an emergency plan that must identify and
       prioritise its vulnerable customers and vulnerable sites within its area, and have
        regard to the needs of non-domestic and domestic users. 592 In the context of
        this investigation, this forms part of South East Water's Regulated Activities
        under Licence Condition P12. While an initial review of the emergency plan
        provided by South East Water suggested it was "broadly in line with good
        practice", 593 on closer examination we identiﬁed concerns about how that plan
        was operationalised, including those ﬁndings above relating to reviewing plans
        in light of past incidents, escalation triggers, incident management and
