                               on South East Water Limited




A0: Draft Enforcement Order
OFWAT HEREBY MAKES A FINAL ENFORCEMENT ORDER UNDER SECTION 18(1) WIA91 IN
RELATION TO SOUTH EAST WATER LIMITED AS SET OUT BELOW

[DRAFT] Enforcement Order in relation to South East Water

This document constitutes a [draft] final enforcement order (the Order) given by the
Water Services Authority (the Authority) to South East Water Limited (the Licensee)
under section 18(1) of the Water Industry Act 1991, as amended (WIA91).

Except as otherwise provided in this Order, words and expressions used in this Order
have the same meaning as in the WIA91.

On [DATE], the Authority gave notice under section 20 WIA91 of its proposal to make an
order under section 18(1) WIA91 in relation to the Licensee (the Notice).

The purpose of this Order is to secure compliance by the Licensee with its statutory
obligations under sectioapril n 37 WIA91, and its Instrument of Appointment (the
Licence).

As set out in the Notice:

     •   The Licensee has contravened its statutory obligations by failing to ensure
         that its water supply system was efficient and economical, and to make
         arrangements to supply water to premises and maintain its water mains and
         other pipes in accordance with section 37 WIA91; and
     •   The Licensee has contravened its Licence by failing to comply with the
         requirements of Licence Condition P12.




This Order takes effect as of [DATE].




                                          195
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



Requirements

Requirement 1: Maintain its water supply system

The Licensee shall ensure and be able to demonstrate that it is developing and
maintaining an efficient and economical system of water supply within its Eastern
region and that all such arrangements are made to supply water in a way that meets
demand and for maintaining, improving and extending its water mains and other pipes.

Requirement 2: Incident preparedness and operational response to supply
interruption incidents

The Licensee shall ensure and be able to demonstrate that it has adequate financial
and management resources and systems of planning and internal control to meet its
duties and functions, including those under section 37 WIA91 and the Security and
Emergency Measures (Water and Sewerage Undertakers and Water Supply Licensees)
Direction 2022 (SEMD), to meet the needs of its customers during an emergency or
water supply interruption incident.

In support of Requirements 1 and 2:

1   By [1-month from decision date] the Licensee shall submit to the Authority for
    approval the identity of a third party reviewer. The third party reviewer must be
    independent and have an appropriate engineering background (the 'Independent
    Reviewer') and the commercial arrangements and scope must make it clear that
    the Independent Reviewer is responsible for reporting to the Authority (not the
    Licensee). As part of obtaining the Authority's approval, which will not be
    unreasonably withheld, the Licensee shall provide contractual documents outlining
    the commercial arrangements and scope of the review to undertake an assessment
    of the Licensee's processes as set out below (the 'Independent Review').

2   The Independent Review, taking into account the findings and the Authority's
    expectations set out in the Authority's notice dated [DATE], will:
      i.   Review the Licensee's root cause analysis process and approach and make
           recommendations on any changes to that approach necessary to ensure
           that the company investigates and identifies the underlying causes for
           failings in compliance with section 37 WIA91 (including supply
           interruptions), including at a minimum consideration of whether, why and
           how the assets, their design, maintenance status / condition, and/or the
           company's planning and operational response policy and procedures, have
           contributed to these issues. To aid this exercise, the Independent Reviewer
           should consider as guiding principles the steps outlined by the Authority

                                          196
       Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                on South East Water Limited



            that a reasonable company would have taken at section 2.3.3 of this decision
            (Practical implications of the legal framework and our approach to the
            investigation);
      ii.   Review the Licensee's incident management procedures to consider
            appropriate use of all available operational tools and what is expected of
            operational managers, and in particular at what level incidents should be
            managed, including escalation triggers during critical periods. This should
            include a consideration of whether alternative water supplies (including
            bottled water, tankers, and mutual aid arrangements) are deployed
            effectively and efficiently. To aid this exercise, the Independent Reviewer
            should adopt the principle that it is better to "over-prepare" at the very
            outset of an incident, rather than responding to a worsening situation. It
            should also take account of the actions and recommendations from the
            DWI's and the Authority's review of the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event and their
            subsequent reviews of the Events within the Relevant Period;
     iii.   By [2-months from decision date] approve the scope and advise on the
            approach to be used in the root-and-branch review of hydraulic models
            outlined in paragraph 4 below;
     iv.    Conduct root cause analysis of the failings identified in the Authority's
            notice dated [DATE], including the particular failings which have
            contributed to the Events within the Relevant Period; and
      v.    Make recommendations for remediating the failings identified in the
            Authority's notice dated [DATE], taking into account deliverability,
            affordability and financeability.

3   The Licensee will take all reasonable steps to facilitate and support the Independent
    Review and the Independent Reviewer.

4   By [3-months from decision date] reflect the advice of the Independent Reviewer
    given at paragraph 2(iii) in its scope and approach to the recalibration and root-
    and-branch review of its entire hydraulic model stock, including a risk-based
    prioritisation of 'hotspots' in WRZs 1-3 and WRZs 6-8 to be completed by as soon as
    possible in 2027 so it can inform the PR29 process (the 'Hydraulic Model Review').

5   By [2-months from decision date] review its area for all vulnerable sites, including
    Special Educational Needs schools and care homes, and, if it has not already done
    so, proactively engage with these sites to establish what support is required in an
    emergency and to reflect these additional needs within the Licensee's emergency
    plan (the 'Vulnerable Sites Review').

6   By [5-months from decision date] or if more time is needed for the Independent
    Review another date to be agreed by the Authority and the Independent Reviewer

                                           197
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



    on reasonable notice (the 'Independent Review Completion Date'), provide the
    Authority with the outcome of the Independent Review, including a report by the
    Independent Reviewer as to the level of cooperation provided by the Licensee
    during the Independent Review process.

7   By [2-months from Independent Review Completion Date] provide the Authority
    with a draft plan as to how it will implement the recommendations from the
    Independent Review and specify actions and reasonable deadlines for remediating
    non-compliance, and any other identified issues which are likely to result in non-
    compliance, taking account of deliverability, affordability and financeability (the
    'Remediation Plan').

8   Without limiting the scope of paragraph 7, the Remediation Plan must include:
       i.  an update on the Vulnerable Sites Review and a plan for completion of the
           remainder of the review, including ongoing and regularly review the
           requirements for vulnerable sites within the Licensee's emergency plan.
      ii.  an update on the Hydraulic Model Review and plan for completion of the
           remainder of the review by 2027.
     iii.  a plan for a thorough lessons learned exercise to be completed no more than
           one month after each water supply interruption incident. This includes a
           thorough root cause analysis and implementation of lessons from previous
           incidents informed by the Independent Reviewer's assessment from the
           Independent Review. The Licensee should demonstrate that following
           lessons learned, all actions are assigned owners with specific and
           measurable deadlines which are reported on to Licensee's senior
           management and/or the Board.

9   By [3-months from Independent Review Completion Date] obtain the Authority's
    agreement, such agreement not to be unreasonably withheld, to the Remediation
    Plan.

Requirement 3: Oversight of compliance

The Licensee shall ensure and be able to demonstrate that it has in place processes to
ensure its senior management and Board actively review and challenge the company's
compliance with its section 37 WIA91 and SEMD obligations on a regular basis,
including when considering and signing-off the annual risk and compliance statements
and the ring-fencing certificates, and that they receive all the information they need to
be able to do this.

In support of Requirement 3, the Licensee shall:


                                          198
      Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                               on South East Water Limited



10 By [2-months from Independent Review Completion Date] present and obtain the
   Authority's agreement, such agreement not being unreasonably withheld, to a plan
   (the 'Management Plan') for putting in place measures to ensure that the necessary
   compliance information is provided to its senior management and Board and that
   this information will be used to review and challenge the company's compliance
   performance and to ensure that its water supply system is maintained. This
   includes updating, monitoring and reporting on its Area Plans and the Remediation
   Plan.

11 By [5-months from Independent Review Completion Date] ensure and be able to
   demonstrate that it has implemented the Management Plan and is using
   compliance information to monitor and address compliance issues within the
   company and to plan for the maintenance of its water supply system.

12 By [5-months from Independent Review Completion Date], develop and implement a
   programme for carrying out annual training of operational staff and senior
   management to ensure that they understand the compliance requirements of the
   section 37 WIA91 and SEMD and are familiar with the processes that need to be
   followed to report and escalate potential non-compliance within the company. The
   Licensee shall maintain a register of the implementation of this training (including
   dates and attendees) and provide the Authority with a copy of the training material
   used.

Requirement 4: Delivery of the plans

The Licensee must deliver the Remediation Plan and the Management Plan in
accordance with the deadlines and terms specified in those plans. The Authority
reserves the right to require amendments to be made to one or more of the agreed
plans should circumstances change.

Requirement 5: Reporting

The Licensee will report to the Authority on a six-monthly basis on its progress against
the requirements of this Order and publish on its website details of its progress and a
summary of the actions it has taken. The first report should be provided by 5pm on [the
date falling twelve months from the decision date].

A failure to comply with this Order may result in the Authority seeking an injunction or
other forms of relief from the court to applying to the court for a special administration
order against the Licensee.

A1: Timeline of the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event
A.3.1   On 16 February 2018 the Met ofﬁce provided an early warning of the potential for
        very cold weather and snow for the UK. On 23 February 2018, the Met Ofﬁce
        issued its ﬁrst yellow warning for snow for 26 February 2018. South East Water's
        region experienced below zero or near zero temperatures from 26 February 2018
        to 3 March 2018. On 3 March 2018 there was an increase in temperature of more
        than 10 degrees resulting in a rapid thaw of the snow and frozen ground. This
        led to an increase in South East Water's mains burst rate, with 216 bursts
        reported and repaired during the freeze thaw event. 615 South East Water
        experiences an increase in demand by over 20%. South East Water calculated
        that 105Ml/d extra was required, 18Ml/d due to bursts on its network and the
        remaining 87Ml/d was due to customer pipes and taps.


A.3.1   A full incident team was formed on 26 February 2018, and South East Water's
        emergency plan was implemented from 27 February 2018 after the Met Ofﬁce
        issued its ﬁrst amber warning signifying a high likelihood of further snow. South
        East Water's region experienced below zero or near zero temperatures from 26
        February 2018 to 3 March 2018. The Met Ofﬁce forecasted a gradual rise in
        temperatures and a slow thaw until 5 March 2018 but, nonetheless, on 2 March
        2018, SEW upgraded the incident to a level 4 (South East Water's most serious
        incident category) and escalated it to the Board. 616

A.3.1   Three areas in South East Water's Eastern region experienced supply
        interruptions for more than 12 hours. South East Water saw 26,705 customers
        lose tap water supply, with up to 6,000 of those customers having no water for
        more than 48 hours.617

A.3.1   Prior to this event occurring, Barcombe WTW had been partially closed for winter
        maintenance. Crowborough, Rotherﬁeld, Mayﬁeld and Wadhurst in particular
        were impacted as they are particularly reliant on Barcombe WTW which provides

A.3.1     During the ﬁrst day of warmer temperatures the water demand increased by
          over 20%, requiring an extra 105Ml/d of water which was the highest level of
          demand that South East Water has ever experienced in March.620


A.3.1     South East Water's extra demand was caused by loss of water due to damaged
          pipes. South East Water calculated that 18Ml/d extra demand was due to bursts
          on its network and the remaining 87Ml/d was due to losses from customer pipes
          and taps. 621

A.3.1     In June 2018, the DWI wrote to all water companies in England and Wales
          following the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event stating: "We recommend…all water
          companies review their levels of preparedness to cope with severe, but not
          unforeseeable, weather events". 623

A.3.1     At the same time, Ofwat published a review of the water companies' response to
          the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event.624 We found that South East Water was one of the
          four worst performing companies, identifying the following issues in particular:
      •   Need to improve forecast modelling as the full impact of the weather was not
          forecasted; 625
      •   Having a number of assets ofﬂine for refurbishment, reducing the overall output
          and resulting in outages for customers; 626 and
      •   Service reservoirs running dry, known as "dewatering", causing further
          escalation of supply interruptions. 627

A.3.1   In the report, we explained that we expected South East Water to publish an

A.3.1   Following Ofwat's review, South East Water conducted a lessons learned exercise
        to prepare an action plan (2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan) where it
        noted that key service reservoirs emptied as a result of the rapid increase in
        demand (by over 20%) that it had experienced. It prepared an action plan in
        which it identiﬁed 61 actions it committed to complete to improve its incident
        response and preparedness. The actions were categorised as follows:
        •   Planning and preparation;
        •   Incident response, including alternative water supplies such as tankers; 629
        •   Communication with customers and key stakeholders;
        •   Customers in vulnerable circumstances; and
        •   Compensation.

A.3.1   South East Water included in its 2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan details as
        to whether the actions were complete, ongoing, or had a speciﬁc due date. At
        the time of publication of the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan (September
        2018), 17 of the 61 actions were marked as completed. This included an action to
        review how to make Barcombe WTW more able to undertake planned
        maintenance without causing additional supply risk.630

A.3.1   Some of the key lessons learned included that South East Water's supplier,
        WaterDirect, could not cope with requests for water from multiple water
        companies affected by large weather events. 631

A.3.1   South East Water also stated that parts of its network are on higher ground and
        therefore it uses pumps to supply water to certain areas from the service
        reservoirs.632 This includes areas such as Loose (see Section 3.3.1) and Charing

A2: Timeline of the 2020 High Demand Event
A.2.1   During the 2020 High Demand Event a total of 43,394 customers were affected
        and South East Water paid a total of £869,670 in compensation to 9,264
        household customers. 638


A.2.2 In the summer of 2020, South East Water experienced a period of high demand
      with a particular impact in its Kent and Sussex regions. South East Water's
        region experienced temperatures of 34 degrees and the Met Ofﬁce
        characterised the event as one of the most signiﬁcant heatwaves to affect the
        South of England in the last sixty years. 639 In its Annual Review 2020 and the
        Summer Demand Event 2020 Incident Response Report, South East Water noted
        that due to a combination of warm weather and changes in water demand
        patterns associated with the impact of Covid 19, such as a focus of working from
        home and having to stay at home, South East Water's network experienced a
        signiﬁcant increase in demand.640 South East Water noted that the demand it
        experienced was signiﬁcantly above its summer average. 641


A.2.3 During the 2020 High Demand Event, daily demand began to increase upwards
      of 610Ml/d from 4 August 2020 reaching a peak demand of 695Ml/d on 7 August
      2020 which was the highest demand level on record for South East Water. 642
      Localised signiﬁcant increases in demand were seen particularly in WRZ2, in
      Sussex, which saw average peak week demand increase to 90.4Ml/d compared
      to 81.8Ml/d for the preceding 15 years.643 On 8 August 2020, South East Water
      escalated the incident to its Board.644 South East Water noted that customers
      lost supply in Kent due to a network valve found to be in a partially closed

        experienced a peak of 695Ml/d on Friday 7 August 2020 which is 11% higher than
        its TUB level. Despite this it was not until 9 August 2020 that an incident team
        was convened thus South East Water lost critical time to protect stocks.

        service reservoirs was also a component feature of the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event
        as noted in paragraph A.3.1.

      Event and 2018 Freeze Thaw Event resulted in loss of supply, experienced similar
        issues with alternative water supplies and affected a similar area, South East
        Water explored how it had implemented the previous lessons to improve its

       be completed until 2024. 653 South East Water included this in its Action Plan with
       an anticipated delivery date of March 2025, ultimately it was delivered in May
       2025. 654 South East Water stated that even if the additional booster pump had
       been installed prior to the 2020 High Demand Event, it would have not been
       effective in mitigating the impacts of the event due to the low levels of the
       feeding reservoir. 655

          and reduce the risk of a similar event occurring. These actions included
          reviewing the actions from the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan to identify
          whether South East Water could accelerate any of the schemes, reassess the
          stafﬁng arrangements for alternative water provisions, and implement TUBs
          more quickly. 656

A3: Timeline of the 2022 Storm Eunice Event

A.3.1   During the 2022 Storm Eunice Event, a total of 85,838 customers were affected
        and South East Water paid a total of £382,500 compensation to 3,827 household
        customers. 657 On 14 February 2022, the Met Ofﬁce issued a warning that Storm
        Eunice was expected on 18 February 2022. 658 In preparation, South East Water's
        production output was increased across sites with spare capacity from
        14 February 2022. On 18 February 2022, the storm caused widespread outage of
        UK Power Networks' (UKPN) power supplies which impacted 1.2 million homes
        and businesses in the south east of England. For South East Water, it impacted
        over 100 assets (ranging from remote borehole sites, booster pumping stations
        and major water treatment works) causing customers to lose supply. 659 Up to
        142,901 properties were impacted and Barcombe WTW experienced a
        [CONFIDENTIAL]. On 3 March 2022, a verbal update on 2022 Storm Eunice
        Event was given in the CEO report to the Board, noting that Sussex "was hit the
        hardest." 660

A.3.2 In the Storm Eunice Incident Review report to South East Water's Executive
      team in August 2022, the company identiﬁed a number of common issues which
      had also materialised with respect to the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event and the 2020
      High Demand Event, including systems resilience (the capability and headroom
      capacity of network assets), ineffective deployment of alternative supplies (such
      as tankers, bowsers and bottled water) and its emergency response and
      communication. 661 It received limited mutual aid, particularly for tanker
      provision. 662 While South East Water's preparatory and responsive efforts did

A4: Timeline of the 2022 High Demand Event
A.4.1   During the 2022 High Demand Event, 34,105 customers were affected and that
        South East Water paid £352,600 in compensation to 710 household customers. 667


A.4.2 South East Water noted that 2022 was characterised by "periods of successive
      months of dry weather". 668 As early as March and April of 2022, South East
      Water's region saw below average rainfall. June 2022 saw 56% less rainfall than
        average which meant, leading up to this event, groundwater levels had begun to
        decline.669 South East Water claim that it had an increased daily demand for
        water across all of its regions from a normal daily average. 670 The dry and hot
        weather had led to ground movement which also caused multiple mains
        bursts.671 In July 2022, a red warning (representing severe drought) was issued
        for the south east of England for prolonged period of high temperatures. 672

A.4.3 During this period, several of South East Water's service reservoirs experienced
      a total loss of storage, due to insufﬁcient headroom, including Charing Hill.673 On
      27 July 2022, South East Water made a recommendation to its Board to
      implement TUBs. 674 TUBs were not deemed necessary for the Western region
      which, although it was also subject to high demands, was well supported by the

A5: Timeline of the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event
A.5.1   During the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, 85,466 customers were affected and South
        East Water paid £3,723,545 in compensation to 24,763 household customers. 681


A.5.2 On 5 to 16 December 2022, Saints Hill WTW in Kent reduced ﬂow output due to
      planned borehole maintenance.


A.5.3 On 10 December 2022, Groomsbridge WTW was shut down impacting service
        reservoir levels across Kent. This was exacerbated by issues at Tonbridge WTW
        which was out of service from 17 November 2022.


A.5.4 On 11 December 2022, temperatures as low as -7 degrees affected the Sussex
        and Kent regions. The temperature did not rise above freezing until the daytime
        of 17 December, where a swing from -7 degrees to +13 degrees was recorded in
        less than 24 hours, causing a freeze thaw.682

A.5.5 During the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, demand exceeded supply in some areas of
      Sussex and Kent due to a spike in demand caused by increase leakage and burst
      mains. During the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event, there were 316 burst mains
      repaired. 683 From 1 to 17 December 2022, South East Water saw demand start to
      steadily increase. On 15 December 2022, South East Water set up an incident
      response team, established bottle water stations and all business as usual
      activity was stopped, including planned maintenance. On 17 December 2022,
      temperatures reached 13 degrees and there was an unplanned outage
      (10 hours) at Barcombe WTW. Blackhurst Lane service reservoir, which requires
        minimum of [CONFIDENTIAL], repeatedly fell below [CONFIDENTIAL],
        impacting supplies. 24-hour tankering was established but was unable to
        mitigate supply/demand balance.


A.5.6 South East Water noted that demand on 18 December 2022 was approximately
      100Ml/d greater compared with the demand around the same time in the

          Plan as having signiﬁcant ﬂood risks. 689


A.5.7 South East Water was able to bring Tonbridge WTW back into service during the
      2022 Freeze Thaw Event, but it acknowledged that this outage did contribute to
          entering the period with low levels in service reservoirs. 690 Out of a possible
          23.3Ml/d of storage assets in the area, only 9.36Ml/d was actually stored at the
          time of the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event. This, coupled with an average output from
          supply works of 64.74%, resulted in a severe water deﬁcit. 691


A.5.8 South East Water noted that two of the three outages listed at paragraph A.5.6
          were for planned maintenance to increase supply resilience and to buffer

A.5.9 In addition to the outages listed at paragraph A.5.6, Groombridge WTW shut
      down on 10 December 2022 due to a frozen pH monitor which resulted in the
        levels in [CONFIDENTIAL] dropping low prior to the weekend of 17 and 18
        December 2022.


A.5.10 Between 19 and 26 December, South East Water repaired 473 leaks (three times
        its average amount).

A.5.11 In its January 2023 Board Meeting, South East Water noted its initial
       understanding of the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event that it "lost over 100Ml [sic] of
       storage in a matter of hours with the majority being lost from customers [sic]
       properties" and that while it did see an "increase in burst mains but not enough
       to explain the rapid loss of storage".694

A.5.12 South East Water requested mutual aid from other water companies which was
       limited due to the country-wide impact of the event. 695

A.5.13 Notwithstanding that water had been lost from customer's pipes during both
       events, given the similarities between the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event and the 2022
       Freeze Thaw Event, South East Water again reviewed the 61 actions in its 2018
        Freeze Thaw Event Action Plan. Some of the actions were still in the process of
        being implemented at that time, four years after the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event.
        This included "[i]nfrastructure reviews with businesses to generate business
        continuity plans e.g. for schools". 696


A.5.14 On 17 May 2023, the DWI's report on the incident also noted the similarities
       between the 2018 Freeze Thaw Event and the 2022 Freeze Thaw Event. 697 The

A6: Timeline of the 2023 High Demand Event
A.6.1 During the 2023 High Demand Event several schools in the South East Water
      region was forced to close as a result of the supply interruption. 701


A.6.2 From 1 June 2023, demand was already above that of South East Water's normal
      daily summer demand (540Ml/d). SEW had zero effective rainfall since April
        2023. On 10 June 2023, demand levels exceeded the red drought plan trigger
        (678Ml/d).


A.6.3 On 12 June 2023, a level 3 demand incident team was formed and an incident
        was declared.


A.6.4 13 of the 16 reservoir cells supplying the affected were available but already at
      low level due to increased demand throughout June. Three cells were out for
      repair and inspection.


A.6.5 Given the history of Events, Ofwat engaged with South East Water and its Board
      over the summer 2023 asking it to explain its poor performance, particularly in
      regards to the 2023 High Demand Event. South East Water's August 2023
      Response was unsatisfactory in terms of identifying root cause(s) and the
      company taking responsibility for addressing our concerns, so we subsequently
      opened this investigation.


A.6.6 In August 2024, Ofwat and the Consumer Council for Water (CCW) published
        research conducted with a sample of South East Water household customers to
        understand how people and households were impacted when their water and
        wastewater services were disrupted as a result of the 2023 High Demand
        Event. 702 This research is not intended to be a statistical sample, it simply
        highlights how customers feel they have been impacted by South East Water's
        performance. Key ﬁndings included: 703




701
    Four schools in East Sussex forced to close due to water shortages | Water | The Guardian, Households
remain without water in parts of Kent and Sussex - BBC News; East Sussex schools forced to close over
South East Water issues | The Argus
702
    Blue Marble, Research into incident response; overview of findings from customer research, final report
for Ofwat/CCW – August 2024, link, p.4.

    Blue Marble, Research into Incident Response: June 2023 water supply interruption in the South East

          Notice of Ofwat’s proposal to issue an enforcement order and impose a penalty
                                   on South East Water Limited



      •   All customers who participated in the research were disappointed by South East
          Water's management of the incident and in some cases, this amounted to a loss
          of trust in the company.
      •   South East Water's communication was poor; it was not proactive or honest and
          this was exacerbated by the tone of some of the communications. For example,
          an email from South East Water's CEO announcing a TUB was sent to customers,
          asking them to "use less water" when they were without supply. Customers felt
          this was insensitive and felt that they were being blamed for the problems they
          were experiencing. 704
      •   Participants did not feel supported by South East Water.
